i_{t}:S^{1}\times\{t\}\to S_{i}\times T^{\ast}[0,1]
.
Say
\theta_{S^{i}}
and
\theta_{T^{\ast}[0,1]}
are the (standard, given) primitives of the symplectic forms on
S^{i}
and on
T^{\ast}[0,1]
.

By Stokes’ theorem,

\int_{S^{1}\times\{0\}}i_{0}^{\ast}(\theta_{S^{i}}\oplus\theta_{T^{\ast}[0,1]})=\int_{S^{1}\times\{t\}}i_{t}^{\ast}(\theta_{S^{i}}\oplus\theta_{T^{\ast}[0,1]})

On the other hand, for any
t
,

\int_{S^{1}\times\{t\}}i_{t}^{\ast}\theta_{T^{\ast}[0,1]}=0

as
\theta_{T^{\ast}[0,1]}=pdq
where under the pullback
q
is the constant
t
.
This means that for each
t
, the flux between
L_{0}
and
L_{t}
is zero. Thus there exists a Hamiltonian isotopy
\phi_{t}
of
S_{i}
taking
L_{0}
to
L_{t}
, and we have
\mathcal{L}=\phi_{\mathbb{H}}(L_{0})
for some Hamiltonian function
\mathbb{H}
as above. Now for any
\lambda\in[0,1]
, set
\lambda\mathbb{H}\coloneq\{\lambda H_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
, and let
\mathcal{L}^{\lambda}
be the Lagrangian given by
\phi_{\lambda\mathcal{H}}(L_{0})
. Then
\mathcal{L}^{(1-\lambda)}
gives a one-parameter family of Lagrangian sections of
\pi
from
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{1}
to the constant Lagrangian section
L_{0}\times\{0\}
.
Moreover, note that if
\mathcal{L}
was exact, then each
\mathcal{L}^{\lambda}
is also immediately exact (and indeed, the
L_{t}
are exact Lagrangians in
S_{i}
).
∎

More generally, we’ll need to classify Lagrangian section of
\pi_{D}
. First, note the following immediate corollary of Lemma
2.28
.

Corollary 2.29
.

Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}:M_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\to[0,1]^{2}
are classified up to equivalence by

H^{1}\left(R^{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}\right)\cong\mathbb{Z}

where the correspondence is again given by taking a Lagrangian section
L
to the class of
[L]-[L_{0}]
.

Moreover, any Lagrangian section of
\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
can be deformed through Lagrangian sections to a constant section, i.e. the product of a section of
(T^{\ast}S^{1})^{-}
and the zero-section of
T^{\ast}[0,1]
. Deformations to constant sections can also obtained over a small neighbourhood of any side of the base, while keeping the section constant over a small neighbourhood of the opposite side.

As before, the corollary holds irrespective of whether we have taken finite height open truncation of
M_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
in the direction of the cylindrical infinite boundary.

Proposition 2.30
.

Suppose that
D
is any decorated graph configuration of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
s, and let
M_{D}
be its mirror Weinstein sector from Section
2.2.2
, with its Lagrangian fibration
\pi_{D}:M_{D}\to R_{D}
. Then exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{D}:M_{D}\to R_{D}
, up to equivalence, are classified by

H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{D})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{|e(G_{D})|}

where the equivalence is given by mapping
L
to the class of
[L]-[L_{0}]
and, explicitly,
there is one generator for each edge of the graph
G_{D}
.

Moreover, any equivalence class of exact Lagrangian section of
\pi_{D}
has a representative which is constant in the following sense: it’s equal to
L_{0}
whenever the fibre of
\pi_{D}
is contractible (in particular, over the discs
D^{2}_{v}
), and given by a constant section for each
\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1},e}
piece. There is a unique such representative up to deformation through sections satisfying these conditions. ‘Relative’ versions of these statements also hold, e.g. for representatives equal to constant sections over neighbourhoods of the boundary components of the ribbon graph
R_{D}
.

Proof.

Say
D=\cup_{e}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{e}
, where
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{e}
is the copy of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
corresponding to edge
e
of
G_{D}
.
Recall that
M_{D}
is given by the pushout of

\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{e}T^{\ast}D^{2}_{\pm,e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{v}T^{\ast}D^{2}_{v}}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{e}M_{\mathbb{P}^{1},e}}

which is compatible with the Lagrangian fibrations
\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1},e}:M_{\mathbb{P}^{1},e}\to[0,1]^{2}
and the projections of cotangent bundles onto their zero-sections.
By restriction, any equivalence class of exact Lagrangian section of
\pi_{D}
gives a collection of equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of the
\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1},e}
, for all
e\in e(G_{D})
.
Conversely, using Corollary
2.29
, we see that given any collection of Lagrangian sections of the
\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1},e}
, they can be deformed to be equal to the zero-section in each
T^{\ast}D^{2}_{\pm,e}
, and then glued together to get a Lagrangian section of
\pi_{D}
. Moreover, we can arrange our deformations so that the resulting Lagrangian section is exact (this can be done by hand, or using arguments from the proof of Lemma
2.28
). The resulting exact Lagrangian section of
\pi_{D}
is clearly well-defined up to deformation through exact Lagrangian sections. Thus exact Lagrangian sections are classified by
\mathbb{Z}^{|e(G_{D})|}
. The claim about deforming to constant sections follows from the corresponding statement in Corollary
2.29
(see also Lemma
2.28
for relative versions).

It remains to check that we have an isomorphism
H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{D})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{|e(G_{D})|}
as claimed. This is extremely similar to the calculation of
H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{D_{i}})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D_{i}}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{k_{i}}
in the proof of Lemma
2.28
. In this case, we have that

R^{1}(\pi_{D})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D}})=\bigoplus_{e}i_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{[a,b]\times[0,1]}

with
[a,b]\times[0,1]\subset[0,1]^{2}
is the locus in the base of
\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1},e}
above which the fibres are non-contractible. The claim then follows.
∎

2.5.2.
Classification of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{i}

Consider the fibration
\pi_{i}:M_{i}\to B_{i}
from Lemma
2.17
. Without loss of generality, it has no critical points in a closed annulus
B_{i}[-2\epsilon,-\epsilon]
(so the fibration above that annulus is simply a thickened mapping torus of the total integral affine monodromy of
B_{i}
).

Let
A\subset B_{i}
denote the closed annulus
B_{i}[-2\epsilon,0]
, and let
\pi_{A}:M_{A}\to A
denote the restriction of
\pi_{i}:M_{i}\to B_{i}
to
A
.

Corollary 2.31
.

The exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A}
, up to deformation through exact Lagrangian sections,
are classified by
H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{A})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{A}})
; as before, the correspondence is given by mapping a Lagrangian section
L
to the class
[L]-[L_{0}]
, where
L_{0}
is our preferred reference section.

Proof.

Our proof has two components. First, we show that there’s a natural one-to-one correspondence between equivalence classes of exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A}
and exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{D_{i}}
(Step 1). And separately, we prove that there’s a natural isomorphism
H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{A})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{A}})\cong H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{D_{i}})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D_{i}}})
(Step 2).

Step 1.

Let
A^{\prime}
denote the closed annulus
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
. By construction in Lemma
2.17
, the fibration
\pi_{i}:M_{i}\to B_{i}
restricts on
A^{\prime}
to a fibration
\pi_{A^{\prime}}:M_{A^{\prime}}\to A^{\prime}
which is just a copy of the fibration
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-\epsilon_{1},0]\to S^{1}\times[-\epsilon_{1},0]
, whose Lagrangian sections we classified in Lemma
2.28
.

Suppose we’re given an equivalence class of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A}
. By restriction, we get an equivalence class of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A^{\prime}}
. We want to show that this is one-to-one.
Consider the inclusion
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-\epsilon_{1},0]\cong M_{A^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow M_{A}
. We can extend this trivially over
B_{i}[-2\epsilon,-\epsilon_{1}]
to get, say,
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-2\epsilon,0]\hookrightarrow M_{A}
, such that
\pi_{A}
restricted to
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-2\epsilon,0]
simply gives the usual fibration, with the base viewed as
S^{1}\times[-2\epsilon,0]
.
Now using Lemma
2.28
, any family of exact Lagrangian section of
\pi_{A^{\prime}}
can be extended to a family of exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A}:S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-2\epsilon,0]\to B_{i}[-2\epsilon,0]
,
and therefore a section of
\pi_{A}:M_{A}\to A
.

On the other hand, given
\pi_{A}:M_{A}\to A
, we can also trivially extend this fibration to a larger annulus, by gluing onto the ‘outer’ vertical boundary a fibration of the form, say,
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[0,2\epsilon]\to S^{1}\times[0,2\epsilon]
.
Call
\pi_{A+}
the resulting extended fibration.
Using Lemma
2.28
, any family of exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A}
, say
L_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
can be extended to a family of exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A+}
, say
L^{+}_{t}
.
On the other hand, for a fixed
t
, we can use
L^{+}_{t}
to write down a one-parameter family of exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A}
starting with
L^{0}\coloneq L_{t}
and ending with a section
L^{1}
which lies inside
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-2\epsilon,0]\subset M_{A}
.
To see this, notice that for
\lambda\geq 0
, radial translation of base annuli give exact symplectic inclusions
\pi_{A+}^{-1}(B[-2\epsilon+\lambda,f(\lambda)])\hookrightarrow\pi_{A+}^{-1}(B[-2\epsilon,0])
(for some monotonically increasing function
f
), intertwining the obvious maps of bases, and agreeing with
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-2\epsilon,0]\subset M_{A}
for
\lambda=2\epsilon
.
Now use images of (the restrictions of)
L^{+}_{t}
for increasing
\lambda
to get the desired one-parameter family of exact Lagrangian sections.
Thus we indeed have a one-to-one correspondence between equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A}
and of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A^{\prime}}
.

Step 2.

Consider the inclusion
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-2\epsilon,0]\hookrightarrow M_{A}
from above. For notational simplicity, set
M\coloneq M_{A}
,
M^{\prime}=S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[-2\epsilon,0]
, and let
i:M^{\prime}\hookrightarrow M
be the open inclusion. Let
N
denote
M\backslash M^{\prime}
, and
j:N\hookrightarrow M
be the (closed) inclusion. Denote
\pi_{A}
by
f
, and let
g
be its restriction to
M^{\prime}
and
h
its restriction to
N
.
There’s a short exact sequence of constructible sheaves on
M
:

0\longrightarrow i_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\longrightarrow j_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\longrightarrow 0.

We have that
Rf_{!}\circ j_{\ast}=Rf_{!}\circ Rj_{\ast}=Rh_{\ast}
. Also, in our case, the open embedding
i
is such that the closure of
M^{\prime}
in
M
is a manifold with boundary
\bar{M}^{\prime}
(with interior
M^{\prime}
, and such that we can find smooth charts on
M
which restrict to manifold-with-boundary charts for
\bar{M}^{\prime}
); from definitions this implies that
Ri_{!}=i_{!}
, and so
and
Rf_{!}\circ i_{!}=Rg_{!}
.
Thus applying
Rf_{!}
to the short exact sequence of sheaves above, we get a long exact sequence of sheaves

\ldots\to f_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\to h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\to R^{1}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M^{\prime}}\to R^{1}f_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\to R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\to R^{2}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M^{\prime}}\to R^{2}f_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\to\ldots

Now notice that
f_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\to h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
is surjective (the fibres of
h
are all connected or empty).
Also, any fibre
G
of
g
has
H^{2}_{c}(G,\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and any fibre
F
of
f
has
H^{2}_{c}(F,\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
,
and we get that the map
R^{2}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M^{\prime}}\longrightarrow R^{2}f_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}
is an isomorphism. This means that we are left with a short exact sequence of constructible sheaves:

0\longrightarrow R^{1}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M^{\prime}}\longrightarrow R^{1}f_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\longrightarrow R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\longrightarrow 0

Now take the associated cohomology long exact sequence. We get

\ldots\longrightarrow H^{0}(R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M^{\prime}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}f_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})\longrightarrow\ldots

To compute
R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
, we want the
\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology groups of fibres of
h
; these can be
0,\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Moreover, by direct observation, we see that

R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^{k_{i}}l_{!}\big(\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B^{j}}\big)

where for each
j
,
l:B^{j}\hookrightarrow A
is the inclusion of a half-open disc (for any
j
) as in Figure
2.6
.

Figure 2.6.
Decomposition of the support of
R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
in the proof of Corollary
2.31

A standard calculation then gives

H^{0}(R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})=H^{1}(R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})=0
,
and so the exact sequence above reduces to an isomorphism

H^{1}(R^{1}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{1}(R^{1}f_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}).

This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.32
.

Equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{i}
are classified by

H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{i})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{i}}),

where the correspondence is given by taking a Lagrangian section
L
to the class of
[L]-[L_{0}]
.

Proof.

For notational convenience, set
\widetilde{B}\coloneq B_{i}
,

B\coloneq B_{i}[-\epsilon]
, and
A\coloneq B_{i}[-2\epsilon,0]
as before; and set
C\coloneq B_{i}[-2\epsilon,-\epsilon]=A\cap B
.
Let
M\coloneq M_{i}
, and let
M_{A}
,
M_{B}
, and
M_{C}
be the subsets of
M
mapping to
A,B
and
C
under
\pi_{i}
;
finally, we will denote
\pi_{i}
simply by
\pi
, and by
\pi_{A}
,
\pi_{B}
and
\pi_{C}
the obvious restrictions.

We already have classifications of equivalence classes of (exact) Lagrangian sections over each of
A
and
B
:

(1)

By Corollary
2.31
, exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{A}
are classified by
H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{A})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{A}})
.

(2)

By
[
HK
, Lemma 4.3]
, Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{B}
are classified by

\operatorname{ker}(\smallfrown F_{B}:H_{2}(M_{B},\partial M_{B})\to\mathbb{Z}),

where
F_{B}
is the class of a smooth fibre of
\pi_{B}
. Using the Leray sequence, this is the same as
H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{B})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{B}})
.

In each case the correspondence is given by taking a Lagrangian section
L
to the class of
[L]-[L_{0}]
. For Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{C}
, we can apply
[
ABC+09
, Proposition 6.69]
.
This shows that each class in
H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{C})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})
has a Lagrangian representative, and that for any given class, if there is an exact Lagrangian representative, it is unique up to fibrewise Hamiltonian isotopy. Then, using the fact that the Lagrangian
L_{0}
is exact, we can see that any other class must have an exact representative (e.g. using the developing map for
C
and differentials of linear functions).
Thus
H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{C})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})
classifies equivalence classes of exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{C}
.

Consider the short exact sequence of sheaves on
\widetilde{B}
:

0\longrightarrow R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\longrightarrow R^{1}(\pi_{B})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{B}}\oplus R^{1}(\pi_{A})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{A}}\longrightarrow R^{1}(\pi_{C})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{C}}\longrightarrow 0

The corresponding long exact sequence in cohomology gives

\ldots\longrightarrow H^{0}(R^{1}(\pi_{B})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus H^{0}(R^{1}(\pi_{A})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow H^{0}(R^{1}(\pi_{C})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\\
\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{B})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{A})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{C})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow H^{2}(R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\ldots

where for legibility we have omitted the domains of constant
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
sheaves, which are clear from context.
As
H^{3}(M;\mathbb{Z})=0
, the Leray sequence implies that
H^{2}(R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})=0
.
Also, from definitions, we have that
H^{0}(R^{1}(\pi_{C})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})=\operatorname{ker}(W-I)
, where
W\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
is the monodromy of
\pi_{C}
(i.e.
M_{C}
is the mapping torus of
W
acting on
T^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, times an interval). Thus the long exact sequence reduces to an exact sequence

(2.33)

\operatorname{ker}(W-I)\ \longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{B})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{A})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{C})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow 0

Suppose we’re given (equivalence classes of) a Lagrangian section of
\pi_{B}
and a Lagrangian section of
\pi_{A}
, such that their restrictions to
C
agree (again, as equivalence classes). Then we can glue them to give a Lagrangian section of the whole of
\pi
. However, because we’re working with equivalence classes, there may be more than one possible gluing: indeed, there are as many choices of gluings as there are exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{C}
which agree with a fixed exact Lagrangian section at the boundary of
C
, up to fibre-preserving Hamiltonian isotopy
on the interior
of
C
. (All this uses is that fibre-preserving Hamiltonian isotopies can be cut-off using bump functions on the base of the fibration.)
Using
[
HK
, Proposition 4.7]
, these choices of gluings are classified by
H_{2}(M_{C};\mathbb{Z})/(\gamma_{C}\cdot\mathbb{Z})
, where
\gamma_{C}
is a fibre of
\pi_{C}
. (Note that the proposition is stated in terms of Lagrangian fibrations with base a disc, but the proof carries over essentially verbatim; and we’re using the fact that fixing any reference Lagrangian section of
\pi_{C}
equips the space of all Lagrangian sections with an additive structure.)
In our case, recalling that
M_{C}
deformation retracts onto the mapping torus of
W
, we get that

H_{2}(M_{C};\mathbb{Z})/(\gamma_{C}\cdot\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{ker}(W-I).

In particular, we’ve now precisely matched things up with Equation
2.33
: we see that equivalence classes Lagrangian sections of
\pi
must be in one-to-one correspondence with
H^{1}(R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}})
.
∎

Remark 2.34
.

Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{i}
have boundaries on the finite boundary of the Liouville sector
M_{i}
. In particular, they are not (or at least, not readily) objects of the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(M_{i})
, which only allows conical boundaries on
\partial_{\infty}M_{i}
. Nevertheless, these sections are natural from the perspective of SYZ mirror symmetry. We will later (geometrically) glue them to get well-defined compact Lagrangian sections of
\pi
, themselves objects of
\mathcal{W}(M)
.

2.5.3.
Relation between line bundles on
Y_{i}
and sections of
\pi_{i}

Let
(\widetilde{Y}_{i},\widetilde{D}_{i})
be the log CY2 surface obtained by starting with
(Y_{i},D_{i})
and doing one interior blow up on each irreducible component of
D_{i}
(still at the distinguished points).
Let
\widetilde{U}_{i}
denote
\widetilde{Y}_{i}\backslash\widetilde{D}_{i}
.
Let
M_{\widetilde{U_{i}}}
be the mirror Weinstein domain, with almost toric fibration
\pi_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}:M_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}\to\widetilde{B}_{i}
.
By sliding one nodal fibre on each invariant ray until they lie in
\widetilde{B}_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
, we see that
\widetilde{B}_{i}
can obtained from
\pi_{U_{i}}:M_{U_{i}}\to B_{i}
replacing modifying the fibration over the annulus
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
by adding
k_{i}
nodal fibres.
Let
x_{j}\in\widetilde{B}_{i}
be the nodal point corresponding to the additional blow-up of the
j
th component of
D_{i}
.
Moreover, notice that we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{M_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{M_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}}
\textstyle{B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}_{i}}

where
\iota
is a symplectic embedding, and
\cong
is a diffeomorphism which is an isomorphism of integral affine manifolds from
B_{i}[-\epsilon]
to
\widetilde{B}_{i}[-\epsilon]
.
(Note that because of non-compact fibres,
M_{i}
only determines an integral affine structure on
B_{i}
for the subset
B_{i}[-\epsilon]
.)
To help visualise things, the inclusion of
M_{i}
into
M_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}
over the annulus
\widetilde{B}_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
(in which
M_{i}
restricts to
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[0,1]
) is given in Figure
2.7
.

Figure 2.7.
\iota:M_{i}\hookrightarrow M_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}
, restricting to the annulus
\widetilde{B}_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
.
M_{i}
is shaded in green, with the dashes denoting open subsets. (The base is divided up according to the topology of the restriction of
M_{i}
in each fibre.)

Proposition 2.35
.

The map
\iota
induces a one-to-one correspondence between equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{i}
and equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}
.

Note that as the base of the fibration is a disc, any Lagrangian section is automatically exact.

Proof.

To simplify notation, we denote spaces by
M\coloneqq M_{i}
,
\widetilde{M}\coloneqq M_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}
,
N=\widetilde{M}\backslash M
and
B\coloneqq\widetilde{B}_{i}\cong D^{2}
; and maps by
f\coloneqq\widetilde{\pi}_{i}:\widetilde{M}\to B
,
g\coloneqq{\pi}_{i}:M\to B
, and
h:N\to B
the restriction of
f
. We have an inclusion
\iota:M\hookrightarrow\widetilde{M}
, which is open, and an inclusion
j:N\hookrightarrow\widetilde{M}
, which is closed.

First, by
[
HK
, Lemma 4.3]
, Lagrangian sections
L
of
f
are in one-to-one correspondence with

\operatorname{ker}(\smallfrown F:H^{2}(\widetilde{M},\partial\widetilde{M})\to\mathbb{Z})

where
F
denotes the class of a smooth fibre of
f
. This is given by taking
L
to the class
[L]-[L_{0}]
. Now notice that by the Leray sequence, there’s a natural identification

\operatorname{ker}(\smallfrown F:H^{2}(\widetilde{M},\partial\widetilde{M})\to\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{1}(R^{1}f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{M}}).

This means we need to compare the two classifying spaces for Lagrangian sections.
Start with the short exact sequence of sheaves on
\widetilde{M}
:

0\longrightarrow\iota_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{M}}\longrightarrow j_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\longrightarrow 0

Now apply
Rf_{\ast}
to this. We have that
Rf_{\ast}\circ j_{\ast}=Rh_{\ast}
; also, similarly to the proof of Corollary
2.31
, in this case we get that
R\iota_{!}=\iota_{!}

Rf_{\ast}\circ\iota_{!}=Rg_{!}
, we get a long exact sequence of sheaves

\ldots\to f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\to h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\to R^{1}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\to R^{1}f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{M}}\to R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\to R^{2}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\to R^{2}f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{M}}\to\ldots

Now notice that
f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\to h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
is surjective (it’s the restriction
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B}\to\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{A}
, for
A
a closed annulus). Also, any fibre
G
of
g
has
H^{2}_{c}(G,\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and any fibre
F
of
f
has
H^{2}(F,\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, so the map
R^{2}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\longrightarrow R^{2}f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{M}}
is simply the identity
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B}
. This means that we are left with a short exact sequence of constructible sheaves:

0\longrightarrow R^{1}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\longrightarrow R^{1}f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{M}}\longrightarrow R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\longrightarrow 0

Now take the associated cohomology long exact sequence. We get

\ldots\longrightarrow H^{0}(R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{M}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})\longrightarrow\ldots

To compute
R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
, we want the
\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology groups of fibres of
h
; these can be
0,\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Moreover, by direct observation, we see that

R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\cong\bigoplus_{j}i_{!}\big(\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B^{j}\backslash\{x_{j}\}}\big)

where
i:B^{j}\hookrightarrow B
is the inclusion of a closed disc containing the nodal point
x_{j}
in its interior (for any
j
) as is Figure
2.8
.

Figure 2.8.
Decomposition of the support of
R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
.

Finally, using for instance
the short exact sequence of sheaves on
B^{j}
given by

0\longrightarrow i_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B^{j}\backslash\{x_{j}\}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B^{j}}\longrightarrow j_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\{x_{j}\}}\to 0

we get that

H^{0}(R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})=H^{1}(R^{1}h_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})=0
.

Thus the exact sequence above reduces to an isomorphism

H^{1}(R^{1}g_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{1}(R^{1}f_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{M}}).

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.36
.

Let
p:\widetilde{Y}_{i}\to Y_{i}
be the blow-down map, and
i:\widetilde{U}_{i}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{Y}_{i}
the inclusion. We have an isomorphism

i^{\ast}\circ p^{\ast}:\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Pic}\widetilde{U}_{i}.

Proof.

Say
D_{i1},\ldots,D_{ik_{i}}
are the irreducible components of
D_{i}
. We have
p^{\ast}D_{ij}=\widetilde{D}_{j}+E_{j}
, where
\widetilde{D}_{j}
denotes the strict transform and
E_{j}
the exceptional divisor.

There are natural isomorphisms

\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}\simeq\operatorname{Pic}\widetilde{Y}_{i}/\langle E_{i1},\ldots,E_{ik_{j}}\rangle

and

\operatorname{Pic}\widetilde{U}_{i}\simeq\operatorname{Pic}\widetilde{Y}_{i}/\langle\widetilde{D}_{i1},\ldots,\widetilde{D}_{ik_{i}}\rangle.

First note that
\operatorname{ker}i^{\ast}=\langle\widetilde{D}_{i1},\ldots,\widetilde{D}_{ik_{i}}\rangle
, and
p^{\ast}\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}=\langle E_{1},\ldots,E_{k_{i}}\rangle^{\perp}
. As
\widetilde{D}_{ij}\cdot E_{il}=\delta_{jl}
, we get that
(\operatorname{ker}i^{\ast})\cap(p^{\ast}\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i})=\{0\}
. As
p^{\ast}:\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}\to\operatorname{Pic}\widetilde{Y}_{i}
is injective, we get that
i^{\ast}\circ p^{\ast}
is injective too.

We want to show that
i^{\ast}\circ p^{\ast}
is also surjective. Given
L\in\operatorname{Pic}\widetilde{Y}_{i}
, there exists some integers
a_{j}
so that
L+\sum a_{j}\widetilde{D}_{ij}=\pi^{\ast}L^{\prime}
, some
L^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}
. Equivalently,
L\cdot E_{ij}+a_{j}=0
for all
j=1,\ldots,k_{i}
.
This means that
\pi^{\ast}L^{\prime}=L-\sum(L\cdot E_{ij})\widetilde{D}_{ij}
. In particular,
i^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}L^{\prime}=i^{\ast}L
, and so
i^{\ast}\circ p^{\ast}
is surjective.
∎

Corollary 2.37
.

There is a bijection

\Phi_{i}:\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}\longrightarrow\{\text{equiv. classes of Lagrangian sections of }\pi_{i}\}

determined by our choice of reference Lagrangian section
L_{0}
.

Given a Lagrangian section
L
of
\pi_{i}
, we can deform it so that it is locally constant above
B_{i}[\epsilon_{1},\epsilon]
; by restriction, we get an exact section
l
of
S_{i}\to S^{1}
, unique up to (exact) deformation. This gives a restriction map

(2.38)

\eta:\{\text{equiv.~classes of Lag.~sections of }\pi_{i}\}\longrightarrow\{\text{exact Lagrangians in }S_{i}\}

Say
L=\Phi_{i}\mathcal{L}
. Then we have
l=\tau l_{0}
, where
l_{0}
is the preferred longitude of
S_{i}
(a component of
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
) and
\tau
is a composition of Dehn twists in meridians of
S_{i}
: we apply
\mathcal{L}\cdot D_{ij}
twists in the meridian corresponding to the ray of the toric model indexed by
j
, for
j=1,\ldots,k_{i}
.

Lemma 2.39
.

The correspondence
\Phi_{i}
between
\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}
and Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{i}
is compatible with HMS for the boundary
D_{i}
in the following sense: we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{i}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Pic}D_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\text{HMS for }D_{i}}
\textstyle{\{\text{classes of Lagrangian sections of }\pi_{i}\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}(S_{i})}

Proof.

We start with the HMS compatibilities of Equation
2.15
.
The map
\eta^{\ast}:\mathcal{W}(M_{U_{i}},\mathfrak{f}_{i})\to\mathcal{F}(S_{i})
therein is given by using the model
\mathcal{F}^{\to}(w_{i})\simeq\mathcal{W}(M_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i})
;
\mathcal{F}^{\to}(w_{i})
is generated by Lagrangian thimbles for the Lefschetz fibration
w_{i}
, all of which end on a copy of
S_{i}
, and
\eta^{\ast}
is given by restricting the thimbles to their boundaries. Tracing through our different models for
M_{i}
, the Weinstein core of this copy of
S_{i}
gets taken to
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
, and the claim follows.
∎

2.5.4.
Symplectomorphisms associated to Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{i}

Proposition 2.40
.

Given
L
a Lagrangian section of
\pi_{i}
, we can associate to it a symplectomorphism of
M_{i}
, say
\sigma_{L}
, such that:

(i)

\sigma_{L}^{\ast}\theta=\theta+df
, where the smooth function
f
needn’t have compact support; and, if
I:\partial M_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
is our ‘Liouville sector’ function (linear near infinity, and whose Hamiltonian vector field is outward pointing along
\partial M_{i}
) then

I\circ\sigma_{L}:\partial M_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
has the same property.

(ii)

\sigma_{L}
is linear on each fibre of
\pi_{i}
over
B_{i}
, where it is given by the Lagrangian translation associated to
L
(and
L_{0}
), as defined in
[
HK
, Section 4]
.

(iii)

Above
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
,
\sigma_{L}
restricts to the symplectomorphism of
S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[0,1]
given by
\tau_{L}\times\operatorname{Id}
, where
\tau_{L}
is the composition of Dehn twists in meridians of
S_{i}
which takes
l_{0}
to
l=\eta(L)
. In general Dehn twists are defined up to compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy; here we will pick a representative for
\tau_{L}
which intertwines the Lagrangian fibration
S_{i}\to S^{1}
, and which is constant on the non-proper locus.

The map
\sigma_{L}
is independent of auxiliary choices up to deformation through symplectomorphisms satisfying all three properties above.
Similarly, if
L^{\prime}
is another Lagrangian section of
\pi_{i}
which is in the same equivalence class as
L^{\prime}
, then
\sigma_{L}
and
\sigma_{L^{\prime}}
are related through a one-parameter family of symplectomorphisms satisfying all three properties above.

Proof.

Start with
L
, any Lagrangian section of
\pi_{i}
.
Lemma
2.28

together with the proof of Proposition
2.32
show that
L
can be deformed, through Lagrangian sections, to a section which, above
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
, is of the form
l\times\{0\}\subset S_{i}\times D^{\ast}[0,1]
, where
l\subset S_{i}
is an exact Lagrangian which is a section of the fibration
S_{i}\to S^{1}
. Further, we can arrange to have
l=l_{0}
whenever the fibres
S_{i}\to S^{1}
are not proper, where
l_{0}
is the restriction of
L_{0}
. Let
L^{\prime}
be such a deformation of
L
(this is the auxiliary choice for defining
\sigma_{L}
).

We now revisit the proof of Lemma
2.17
. Consider the (non-proper) Lagrangian fibration
\pi_{U_{i}}\circ\psi:M^{c}_{i}\to B_{i}
.
By construction, this is identified with the Lagrangian fibration
\pi_{U_{i}}:M_{U_{i}}\backslash(N_{1}\cup N_{2})\to B_{i}
, where, in coordinates near the boundary the
N_{j}
are of the form
S_{i}
times a half-disc in
\mathbb{C}_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}
(one on either side of the origin).
We get
L_{0},L^{\prime}\hookrightarrow M_{i}\backslash(N_{1}\cup N_{2})
. Both have images in
S_{i}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in the boundary neighbourhood chart, and both are sections of
\pi_{U_{i}}
.

Using the construction of
[
HK
, Section 4]
, we can use
L^{\prime}
and
L_{0}
to define a Lagrangian translation of
M_{U_{i}}
, say
\sigma_{L}
. This is an exact symplectomorphism.
Now observe our local neighbourhood chart
\mathbb{C}_{\operatorname{Re}\geq 0}\times S_{i}
is preserved by
\sigma_{L}
: it restricts to
\operatorname{id}\times\tau
, where
\tau
is a symplectomorphism of
S_{i}
which intertwines the fibration to
S^{1}
and takes
l_{0}
to
l
, i.e. a composition of Dehn twists in meridians of
S_{i}
. In particular, this preserves
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
set-wise. Thus
\sigma_{L}
restricts to an exact symplectomorphism of
M_{i}^{c}
, and induces an exact symplectomorphism of
M_{i}
. (Recall that to get
M_{i}
, in the proof of
2.17
we need to take a slightly smaller ‘infinite-boundary cut-off’; we get a symplectomorphism by conjugating with a small-time Liouville flow.)

By construction,
\sigma_{L}
satisfies properties (ii) and (iii), is exact (in the sense of (i)), and satisfies the second condition in (i) from our local model description. Finally, the claims about the independence on the auxiliary choice
L^{\prime}
, and about the choice of representative for an equivalence class of Lagrangian section, are both clear.
∎

We want to study the action of such maps
\sigma_{L}
on
\mathcal{W}(M_{i})
. We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.41
.

There are equivalences

\textstyle{H_{2}(M_{U_{i}},\partial M_{U_{i}}\backslash\mathfrak{f}_{i})}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathcal{W}(M_{U_{i}},\mathfrak{f}_{i}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K(Y_{i})}

where the first map is given by taking classes of Lagrangians, and the second one by starting with the homological mirror symmetry equivalence
\mathcal{W}(M_{U_{i}},\mathfrak{f}_{i})\simeq\Coh(Y_{i})
and passing to
K
-theory on both sides.

Proof.

Consider the commutative diagram:

\textstyle{H_{2}(\partial M_{U_{i}},\partial M_{U_{i}}\backslash\mathfrak{f}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(M_{U_{i}},\partial M_{U_{i}}\backslash\mathfrak{f}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(M_{U_{i}},\partial M_{U_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathcal{W}(S_{i}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathcal{W}(M_{U_{i}},\mathfrak{f}_{i}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathcal{W}(M_{U_{i}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{K(D_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K(Y_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K(U_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{0}

The isomorphism between the middle and bottom rows comes from taking the triple of compatible HMS equivalences in
[
HK22
]
and passing to
K
theory. The vertical maps from the middle to the top row are given by taking homology classes of Lagrangians.
Each of the rows is exact, and the top-right map is know to be an isomorphism (see e.g.
[
HK
, Lemma 4.20]
).

To complete the diagram in the top left corner, observe that we have isomorphisms

H_{2}(\partial M_{U_{i}},\partial M_{U_{i}}\backslash\mathfrak{f}_{i})\simeq H^{1}(\mathfrak{f}_{i})\simeq H^{1}(S_{i})\simeq H_{1}(S_{i},\partial S_{i}).

Moreover, observe that the resulting isomorphism
H_{1}(S_{i},\partial S_{i})\to H_{2}(\partial M_{U_{i}},\partial M_{U_{i}}\backslash\mathfrak{f}_{i})
can be obtained explicitly by starting with a collection of arc generators for
H_{1}(S_{i},\partial S_{i})
and taking their images under the linking discs construction of
[
GPS24
]
(viewed as a smooth procedure). This means that the top-left square can be completed with a map
a:K_{0}(\mathcal{W}(S_{i}))\to H_{1}(S_{i},\partial S_{i})
, taking each Lagrangian to its homology class, such that the square commutes. As
H_{1}(S_{i},\partial S_{i})
has a collection of generators given by Lagrangian arcs, the map
a
is onto.
Finally, the category
\mathcal{W}(S_{i})
has an explicit collection of generators, given by cocores of handles for a Weinstein handlebody decomposition of
S_{i}

[
CDRGG24
,
GPS24
]
; it then follows that
a
is an isomorphism. By comparing the top and middle lines, this completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.42
.

Suppose
L
is any Lagrangian section of
\pi_{i}
, and
\sigma_{L}
the symplectomorphism constructed in Proposition
2.40
. Then
\sigma_{L}
induces a well-defined autoequivalence of the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(M_{i})
, which only depends on the equivalence class of
L
.

Moreover, suppose that
\mathcal{L}\in\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}
is such that
\Phi_{i}(\mathcal{L})=L
. Then, under the homological mirror symmetry equivalence for
M_{i}
,
[\sigma_{L}]\in\Auteq\mathcal{W}(M_{i})
corresponds to
(-\otimes\mathcal{L})\in\Auteq\Coh Y_{i}
.

Proof.

First, we claim property (i) from Proposition
2.40
is enough for
\sigma_{L}
to induce a well-defined autoequivalence of
\mathcal{W}(M_{i})
, up to an overall shift (note that as
H^{1}(M_{i};\mathbb{Z})=0
, so there’s a unique choice of grading for
\mathcal{W}(M_{i})
up to an overall shift). For a symplectomorphism
\sigma
of a Liouville domain
(N,\theta)
such that
\sigma^{\ast}\theta=\theta+df
, where there are no assumptions on the support of
f
, this is carefully done in
[
KS25
, Section 2.2]
.
In the case here, the property about the behaviour of
\sigma_{L}
near the finite boundary
\partial M_{i}
ensures that the arguments in op. cit. carry through to show that
\sigma_{L}
induces an autoequivalence of
\mathcal{W}(M_{i})
,
well-defined up to an overall shift, say
[\sigma_{L}]
, and that this is invariant of the deformations allowed in
2.40
, in particular the representative of the equivalence class of the section
L
.

Now suppose that
\mathcal{L}\in\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}
is such that
\Phi_{i}(\mathcal{L})=L
. We want to show that under the HMS isomorphism
\mathcal{W}(M_{i})\simeq\Coh Y_{i}
,
[\sigma_{L}]
corresponds to
(-\otimes\mathcal{L})\in\Auteq(\Coh Y_{i})
(we also use this to pin down the choice of shift / grading for
[\sigma_{L}]
). By
[
Ueh19
]
, autoequivalences of
\Coh Y_{i}
are well understood. In our case, we have the following criterion
[
HK
, Proposition 2.14]
: suppose that
\phi\in\Auteq(\Coh Y_{i})
is such that

(i)

\phi
induces the identity on the
K
-theory
K(Y_{i})
;

(ii)

for each
(-2)
curve
C\subset Y_{i}
,
\phi
is the identity on
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C}
and
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C}(-1)
;

(iii)

we have
i^{\ast}\circ\phi=i^{\ast}
, where
i:D_{i}\to Y_{i}
is the inclusion;

then
\phi
must be the identity.

Now suppose that
\phi
corresponds to
[\sigma_{L}]
under HMS.
For (i), by Lemma
2.41
,
\phi
has the same action on
K(Y_{i})
as
(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
. (The map
[\sigma_{L}]
the correct action on
H_{2}(M_{U_{i}},\partial M_{U_{i}})\simeq K_{0}(\mathcal{W}(M_{U_{i}}))
by previous work on Lagrangian translations, see e.g.
[
HK
, Proposition 4.14]
; and the correct boundary action on
H^{1}(S_{i})
by direct observation. By the proof of
2.41
, this determines the action on the whole
K
-theory.)

For (ii), given any
(-2)
curve
C\subset(Y_{i}\backslash D_{i})
,
\phi
has the same action on
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C}
and
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C}(-1)
as
(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
. This too is immediate from known properties of Lagrangian translations and (mirrors to) log CY surfaces, see
[
HK
, Lemma 4.15 and Proposition 5.2]
.

For (iii), notice that
(-\otimes\mathcal{L}|_{D_{i}})\in\Auteq(\Perf D_{i})
corresponds under mirror symmetry to
\tau\in\Auteq(\mathcal{F}(S_{i}))
, where
\tau
is the composition of Dehn twists in meridians of
S_{i}
which takes
l_{0}
to
l
. In particular, by Lemma
2.39
, we have that
i^{\ast}\circ\phi=i^{\ast}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
.

Taken together, these three points imply that
if
we are in a case there
D_{i}\subset Y_{i}
has no
(-2)
components, then we can conclude that
[\sigma_{L}]
is mirror to
(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
.

It remains to consider the case where
D_{i}
does contain some
(-2)
components.
We use an auxiliary log CY surface with split mixed Hodge structure, say
(Y_{i}^{\prime},D_{i}^{\prime})
, given by starting with
(Y_{i},D_{i})
and making blow-ups at interior points of
D_{i}
until
D_{i}^{\prime}
has no
(-2)
curves. We use the obvious notation for mirror structures associated to
(Y_{i}^{\prime},D_{i}^{\prime})
.
The Weinstein Lefschetz fibration
w_{i}^{\prime}
is given by starting with the fibration
w_{i}
and adding critical points (keeping the same fibre). This gives a map

\rho^{\ast}:\mathcal{W}(M_{i})\longrightarrow\mathcal{W}(M_{i}^{\prime}).

A key feature of
[
HK22
]
is that this is compatible with HMS: we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(M_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\ast}}
HMS
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(M_{i}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
HMS
\textstyle{\Coh Y_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\Coh Y_{i}^{\prime}}

where
p:Y_{i}^{\prime}\to Y_{i}
is the blow-down map.

At the start of Section
2.5.3
,
we constructed an inclusion
\iota:M_{i}\hookrightarrow M_{\widetilde{U}_{i}}
, where
(\widetilde{Y}_{i},\widetilde{D}_{i})
was given by starting with
(Y_{i},D_{i})
and blowing up an interior point on each component of
D_{i}
.
Ignoring the extra blow-ups that we don’t need, we get an inclusion
\iota:M_{i}\hookrightarrow M_{U_{i}^{\prime}}
. Moreover, any Lagrangian section
L
of
\pi_{i}
gives a Lagrangian section of
\pi_{U^{\prime}_{i}}
, which can be extended to an (equivalence class of) Lagrangian section
L^{\prime}
of
\pi_{i}^{\prime}
in a preferred way.
(Take a representative for
L
which is constant over
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
and then extend in the obvious way).
Under the correspondences
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
, this is the pullback map
p^{\ast}:\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}\to\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}^{\prime}
.

Assume
L
and
L^{\prime}
are as above (with
L
constant over
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
). Then we have symplectomorphisms
\sigma_{L}
of
M_{i}
and
\sigma_{L^{\prime}}
of
M_{i}^{\prime}
such that, by construction,
\sigma_{L^{\prime}}
preserves
M_{i}\subset M_{i}^{\prime}
and restricts to
\sigma_{L}
on it. Again using the interplay between the sectorial and Lefschetz fibration definitions of
\mathcal{W}(M_{i})\cong\mathcal{F}^{\to}(w_{i})
and
\mathcal{W}(M_{i}^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{F}^{\to}(w^{\prime}_{i})
, we get that the autoequivalences
[\sigma_{L}]
and
[\sigma_{L^{\prime}}]
are compatible with
\rho^{\ast}
: we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(M_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{[\sigma_{L}]}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(M_{i}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\sigma_{L^{\prime}}]}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(M_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(M_{i}^{\prime})}

Say
[\sigma_{L^{\prime}}]
is mirror to
\phi^{\prime}\in\Auteq Y_{i}^{\prime}
and
[\sigma_{L}]
is mirror to
\phi\in\Auteq Y_{i}
. Then the commutative diagram above becomes

\textstyle{\Coh Y_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\Coh Y_{i}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Coh Y_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\Coh Y_{i}^{\prime}}

Our argument for the case where
D_{i}
contains no
(-2)
curves shows that
\phi^{\prime}=(-\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=p^{\ast}\mathcal{L}
. Now, using the fact that
\Coh Y_{i}
is generated by line bundles (this follows from
[
Bei78
,
Orl92
]
, see
[
HK22
, Corollary 2.15]
for an explicit description), we see that we must have
\phi=(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
. This completes the proof.
∎

2.5.5.
Line bundles on
Y
and sections of
\pi:M\to S^{2}

Proposition 2.43
.

Equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of
\pi:M\to S^{2}
are classified by

H^{1}(R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})

where the correspondence is given taking a section
L
to the class of
[L]-[L_{0}]
.

Moreover, there is a one-to-one correspondence
\Phi
between equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of
\pi
and elements of
\operatorname{Pic}Y
, such that for each
i
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\{\text{classes of Lag.~sections of }\pi\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
restriction
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\{\text{classes of Lag.~sections of }\pi_{i}\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{i}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Pic}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}}

Proof.

The key ingredients are Lemma
2.28
and Propositions
2.30
and
2.32
.

We have the following short exact sequence of sheaves on
S^{2}
:

0\longrightarrow R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i}R^{1}(\pi_{i})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{i}}\oplus R^{1}(\pi_{D})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D}}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i}R^{1}(\pi_{D_{i}})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D_{i}}}\longrightarrow 0

The associated long exact sequence in cohomology reduces to:

(2.44)

0\longrightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i}H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{i})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{i}})\oplus H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{D})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D}})\longrightarrow\\
\bigoplus_{i}H^{1}(R^{1}(\pi_{D_{i}})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M_{D_{i}}})\longrightarrow 0

On the other hand, any equivalence class of Lagrangian section of
\pi
restricts to give equivalence classes of an exact Lagrangian section of
\pi_{D}
, and of Lagrangian sections of each
\pi_{i}
, which agree when further restricted to their overlaps
\pi_{D_{i}}
. Conversely, by deforming to constant sections for each
\pi_{D_{i}}
, we see that given any equivalences classes of an exact Lagrangian section of
\pi_{D}
and of each
\pi_{i}
, such that they restrict to the same equivalences of exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{D_{i}}
, we can patch them to get a well-defined equivalence class of Lagrangian section of
\pi
. Together with Equation
2.44
, this proves the first part of the Proposition.

For the correspondence
\Phi
, we need our assumption that
Y
has split mixed Hodge structure. This implies that we have an exact sequence

(2.45)

0\to\operatorname{Pic}Y\to\oplus_{i}\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}\to\oplus_{i,j}\operatorname{Pic}D_{ij}

where the
D_{ij}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
are the irreducible components of
D
, and all maps are given by pullback. On the mirror side, the map
\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}\to\operatorname{Pic}D_{ij}
corresponds to restricting Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{i}
to sections of
\pi_{D_{ij}}=\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
. These are just classified by
\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, suppose we’re given equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections
L_{i}
of
\pi_{i}
(for all
i
) such that their restrictions to sections of
\pi_{D_{i}j}
(running over all
i,j
) give the same element of
\mathbb{Z}^{|e(G_{D})|}
. Then from Proposition
2.30
, there’s a uniquely determined equivalence class of exact Lagrangian section of
\pi_{D}
, classified by this element of
\mathbb{Z}^{|e(G_{D})|}
, which allows us to patch the
L_{i}
to get a section of
\pi
. Now from the first half of our proof, equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of
\pi
must be in one-to-one correspondence with
\operatorname{Pic}Y
; and that correspondence
\Phi
is determined by its restrictions
\Phi_{i}
to each
\operatorname{Pic}Y_{i}
, as desired.
∎

Each Lagrangian section of
\pi
is a Lagrangian
S^{2}
in
M
, and, after a choice a grading, gives an object of the Fukaya categories
\mathcal{W}(M)
(and
\mathcal{F}(M)
). We want to show that
\Phi
is simply the HMS isomorphism of Theorem
2.22
for those objects. We first check this just for
L_{0}
.

Lemma 2.46
.

Under the HMS isomorphism of Theorem
2.22
, the Lagrangian sphere
L_{0}
in
M
, equipped with a suitable grading, is mirror to the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}\in\Coh Y
.

Proof.

Let
Y^{[i]}
denote the disjoint union of the normalisations of the codimension
i
strata of
Y
. Then the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
can be resolved as

(2.47)

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y}\longrightarrow i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y^{[0]}}\longrightarrow i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y^{[1]}}\longrightarrow i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y^{[2]}}\longrightarrow 0

where
i
always denotes inclusion into
Y
, and all maps are given by evaluations (with the obvious signs coming from an overall choice of orientation on the intersection complex of
Y
).
Using
\{i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y^{[1]}}\longrightarrow i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y^{[2]}}\}\simeq i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{D}
, and similarly for the
D_{i}
, we get that
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is also quasi-isomorphic to the twisted complex:

(2.48)

\left\{\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 14.53696pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 38.70236pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i}i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.87805pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.53696pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.53696pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.53696pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i}i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{D_{i}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right\}

with the obvious maps. The rest of the proof consists of identifying mirrors to the sheaves in this complex, and using Polterovich surgery to simplify the mirror complexes of Lagrangians to recover
L_{0}
.

Let’s identify mirrors to the coherent sheaves which appear in the resolution. First, in
M_{v}=T^{\ast}D^{2}_{v}
, the mirror to
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in\Coh\{v\}
is the cotangent fibre to an interior point (this is classical). This Lagrangian can be deformed to a Lagrangian
L_{v}
(still conical at infinity, giving the same object in
\mathcal{W}(M_{v})
) such that on each of the patches
[0,1]^{2}\subset D^{2}_{v}
used in Section
2.2.2
,
L_{v}
restricts to a product Lagrangian as in Figure
2.9
.

Figure 2.9.
The Lagrangian
L_{v}
in
T^{\ast}D^{2}_{v}
, which is mirror to
\mathcal{O}_{v}
.

Second, in
(T^{\ast}S^{1})^{-}
, the mirror to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\in\Coh\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the Lagrangian
l_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
as in Figure
2.10
. This follows from classical HMS for
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, translated to the sectorial set-up.

Figure 2.10.
The Lagrangian
l_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
in
(T^{\ast}S^{1})^{-}
, which is mirror to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
.

In
M_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}=(T^{\ast}S^{1})^{-}\times T^{\ast}[0,1]
, the mirror to
\mathcal{O}
is
L_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}:=l_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\times T^{\ast}_{1/2}
. Similarly, the mirror to
\mathcal{O}_{D_{i}}\in\Coh D_{i}
is
l_{0}\subset S_{i}
(see
[
LP17
]
, or this can be shown directly from the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
case using a sectorial covering); stabilising, we get
L_{D_{i}}:=l_{0}\times T^{\ast}_{1/2}\subset S_{i}\times T^{\ast}[0,1]
.

Third, we claim that in
M_{i}
, the mirror to
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}}\in\Coh Y_{i}
is a Lagrangian, say
L_{Y_{i}}
, which agrees with
L_{0}
away from
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
, and, in
\pi_{i}^{-1}(B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0])=S_{i}\times T^{\ast}[0,1]
, is given by the product of
l_{0}
with the Lagrangian arc
l_{d}
of Figure
2.11
. (Here
S_{i}\times T^{\ast}_{0}
maps to the inner boundary of the annulus
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]
, and
S_{i}\times T^{\ast}_{1}
to the outer one.)

Figure 2.11.
Some distinguished Lagrangian arcs in
T^{\ast}[0,1]
.

To check the claim, recall from
[
HK22
]
that in the directed Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}^{\to}(w_{i})
, the mirror to
\mathcal{O}
is a Lagrangian thimble which ends on (our distinguished copy of
S_{i}
); and under our identification of the total space of
w_{i}
with
M_{U_{i}}
, this thimble is taken to our preferred Lagrangian section
L_{0}
(with boundary
on
the stop
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
). If we switch to working with the Fukaya category stopped at
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
, the object corresponding to
L_{0}
is given by applying a small negative Reeb flow to
L_{0}
, to displace its boundary off
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
. Passing from stops to sectors, we delete a very small neighbourhood of
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
, not intersecting our displaced Lagrangian; we then recognise the resulting Lagrangian as
L_{Y_{i}}
.

In our scenario, inside the Weinstein sectorial pushout
M
, we get Lagrangians
L_{v}
for each
v\in Y^{[2]}
, mirror to
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{v}\in\Coh Y
;
L_{D_{ij}}
for each irreducible
D_{ij}\subset Y^{[1]}
, mirror to
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{D_{ij}}\in\Coh Y
; and
L_{Y_{i}}
for each
Y_{i}\in Y^{[0]}
, mirror to
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}}
. In order to recover the mirror to
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, we now perform (many!) Polterovich surgeries for cleanly intersecting Lagrangians. First, consider the mapping cone

\mathbb{L}_{D}=\left\{\bigoplus_{i,j}L_{D_{ij}}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{v}L_{v}\right\}

where we’re taking a cone over the ‘obvious’ map, mirror to the evaluation in Equation
2.47
. By standard results about mapping cones in Fukaya categories, this is quasi-isomorphic in
\mathcal{W}(M_{D})
(or
\mathcal{W}(M)
) to the Lagrangian given by

\left(\bigoplus_{i,j}L_{D_{ij}}\right)\#\left(\bigoplus_{v}L_{v}\right)

where
\#
denotes Polterovich surgery (for cleanly intersecting Lagrangians). Locally, using Figure
2.11
, each such surgery is modelled on

\left((l_{d}\sqcup l_{u})\#T^{\ast}_{1/2}\right)\times T^{\ast}_{1/2}

inside
T^{\ast}[0,1]^{2}
. Up to compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy,
(l_{d}\sqcup l_{u})\#T^{\ast}_{1/2}
is simply the disjoint union of
l_{0}
and of two small arcs. The latter both give the zero object in
\mathcal{W}(M)
(e.g. by displaceability). Let
L_{D}
be the non-trivial component of the Lagrangian we get this way. This is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathbb{L}_{D}
, mirror to
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{D}
, and, when restricted to the annulus
B_{i}[-\epsilon_{1},0]\subset R_{D}
, is given by
l_{0}\subset l_{u}
.
It follows from Equation
2.48
that
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is mirror to the mapping cone

\mathbb{L}_{0}:=\left\{\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.02966pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 34.19507pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i}L_{Y_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.86346pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-10.02966pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L_{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.02966pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.02966pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i}L_{D_{i}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right\}

where we’re again taking the cone over the mirrors to evaluation maps. Now notice that these are modelled on exactly the same Polterovich surgeries as before; moreover, the resulting non-trivial Lagrangian is precisely our preferred Lagrangian section
L_{0}
of
\pi
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.49
.

Let
L
be any Lagrangian section of
\pi
. Then we can associate to it a symplectomorphism of
M
, say
\sigma_{L}
, well-defined in
\pi_{0}\operatorname{Symp}M
, such that:

(i)

On each
M_{i}
,
\sigma_{L}
restricts to the symplectomorphism
\sigma_{L_{i}}
associated to
L_{i}:=L|_{M_{i}}
from Proposition
2.40
. In particular,
\sigma_{L}
maps the equivalence class of
L_{0}
to the equivalence class of
L
.

(ii)

The map
\sigma_{L}
induces an autoequivalence of the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(M)
, well-defined up to an overall shift.

(iii)

Under the HMS isomorphism of Theorem
2.22
,
[\sigma_{L}]\in\Auteq\mathcal{W}(M)
, for a suitable choice of shift, is mirror to
(-\otimes\mathcal{L})\in\Auteq\Coh Y
, where
\mathcal{L}=\Phi(L)\in\operatorname{Pic}Y
.

Proof.

For (i), start with our section
L
. By Proposition
2.30
, we can assume it is in constant form over
R_{D}
. Let
L_{i}
be the restriction of
L
over
B_{i}[\epsilon]
. For each of these, Proposition
2.40
gives a symplectomorphism
\sigma_{L_{i}}
; by construction, these agree on their overlaps (the total spaces of the fibrations
\pi_{D_{ij}}\to[0,1]^{2}
), and can be extended by the constant map over the
D^{2}_{v}\subset S^{2}
. This gives our symplectomorphism
\sigma_{L}
. As with Proposition
2.40
, different auxiliary choices give the same element of
\pi_{0}\operatorname{Symp}M
.

For (ii), as
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})=0
, the map
\sigma_{L}
automatically lifts to the group of graded symplectomorphisms of
M
. By
[
KS25
, Corollary 2.9]
, it induces an autoequivalence of
\mathcal{W}(M)
, well-defined up to a shift.

For (iii), note that the map
\sigma_{L}
also restricts to give a symplectomorphism of
M_{D}
, say
\sigma_{L_{D}}
; and symplectomorphisms of each
M_{D_{ij}}
(i.e.
M_{\mathbb{P}^{1},e}
for
e
varying over
e(G_{D})
), say
\sigma_{L_{ij}}
.
In each case, as in the proof of Proposition
2.42
, it induces autoequivalences of
\mathcal{W}(M_{D})
and the
\mathcal{W}(M_{D_{ij}})
, again well-defined up to an overall shift.

Let
\mathcal{L}=\Phi(L)\in\operatorname{Pic}Y
. For each
M_{D_{ij}}
,
\sigma_{L}
restricts to a fibre-preserving symplectomorphism, say
\sigma_{L_{ij}}
, which is the stabilisation (by a product with the identity) of
(\mathcal{L}\cdot D_{ij})
Dehn twists in the zero section of the relevant copy of
(T^{\ast}S^{1})^{-}
.
By homological mirror symmetry for
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, the autoequivalence
[\sigma_{L_{ij}}]\in\Auteq\mathcal{W}(M_{D_{ij}})
is mirror to
(-\otimes\mathcal{L}|_{D_{ij}})\in\Auteq\Coh D_{ij}
.
Also, as
\sigma_{L}
restricts to the identity on
D^{\ast}D^{2}_{v}
, and
(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
restricts to the identity on
\Coh\{pt\}
, the pushout diagrams in the proof of Theorem
2.7
imply that
[\sigma_{L_{D}}]\in\Auteq\mathcal{W}(M_{D})
is mirror to
(-\otimes\mathcal{L}|_{D})\in\Auteq\Coh D
.
Finally, by Proposition
2.42
, we know that
[\sigma_{L_{i}}]\in\Auteq\mathcal{W}(M_{i})
is mirror to
(-\otimes\mathcal{L}|_{Y_{i}})\in\Auteq\Coh Y_{i}
. Putting this together and using the pushout diagrams in the proof of Theorem
2.22
, we get that
[\sigma_{L}]\in\Auteq\mathcal{W}(M)
must be mirror to
(-\otimes\mathcal{L})\in\Auteq\Coh Y
, as required.
∎

Corollary 2.50
.

Let
L
be any equivalence class of Lagrangian section of
\pi
. Then, for a suitable choice of grading,
the HMS isomorphism of Theorem
2.22
maps
L\in\mathcal{W}(M)
to
\mathcal{L}=\Phi(L)
.

Proof.

This follows from combining Lemma
2.46
together with Proposition
2.49
.
∎

Remark 2.51
.

Suppose that the dual complex of
Y
was a general orientable compact surface
S
rather than
S^{2}
.
Then the proof of Proposition
2.43
generalises to show that equivalence classes of exact Lagrangian sections of
\pi\colon M\to S
are classified by
H^{1}(R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})
.
On the other hand, Equation
2.45
no longer holds, as
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\neq 0
. We have
a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(S,\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{C}^{*})\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}Y\rightarrow\operatorname{ker}\phi\rightarrow 0

where
\phi\colon L\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
is the extension class of the MHS on
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
, with

W_{2}/W_{0}=L=\operatorname{ker}(\operatorname{Pic}Y^{[0]}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}Y^{[1]})

In the split MHS case, we have
\phi=1
. This suggests that
\operatorname{Pic}Y
will be identified under HMS with exact Lagrangian sections of the SYZ fibration
\pi\colon M\rightarrow S
(modulo fibre preserving Hamiltonian isotopy) equipped with a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-local system.

2.6.
Mirror symmetry: compact Fukaya category

Theorem 2.52
.

Assume that
Y
is a maximal normal crossing Calabi-Yau surface which is projective, has split mixed Hodge structure, and whose dual complex is a triangulation of
S^{2}
. Let
M
be the Weinstein manifold constructed in Definition
2.20
. Then we have a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
categories:

\mathcal{F}(M)\simeq\Perf(Y).

This is compatible with the HMS equivalence of Theorem
2.22
in the obvious way. In particular, the explicit correspondences between favourite objects established in Sections
2.4
and
2.5
still hold here.

Proof.

First, we show that under the isomorphism in Theorem
2.22
, we get a fully faithful map
\mathcal{F}(M)\subset\Perf(Y)
.
To see this, notice, first, that an element in
\mathcal{F}(M)
has finite rank total
HF^{\ast}
with
any
element in
\mathcal{W}(M)
, because for generic Floer perturbation data, the Floer complex
CF^{\ast}
itself has finite total rank: it is generated by finitely many transverse intersection points.
On the other hand, a complex of coherent sheaves
F\in\Coh Y
lies in
\Perf Y
if and only if it has finite total
\Ext^{\ast}
with any
G\in\Coh Y
. This is also standard:
F
is perfect if and only if
F_{p}
is perfect for all
p\in Y
; now consider the minimal free resolution of
F_{p}
over
\mathcal{O}_{Y,p}
;

F_{p}
is perfect if and only if this is finite; as
\Ext^{n}(F,\mathcal{O}_{p})
is computed by applying
\operatorname{Hom}(-,\mathcal{O}_{p})
, we get that
F
is perfect if and only if
\oplus_{n}\operatorname{Hom}(F,G[n])
is finite dimensional for all
G\in\Coh Y
.

We now want to establish that the inclusion
\mathcal{F}(M)\subset\Perf(Y)
is in fact a quasi-isomorphism.
Since
Y
is projective,
\Perf Y
has an explicit collection of line bundle split-generators given by the pullbacks of
\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(2)\in\Perf\mathbb{P}^{N}
for some embedding
Y\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N}

[
Orl09
, Theorem 4]
.
By Corollary
2.50
, each of these bundles is mirror to a Lagrangian
S^{2}
(with a suitable brane datum), which in particular is an object of
\mathcal{F}(M)
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.
Type III K3 surfaces and A-side compactification

3.1.
Some background on type III K3 surfaces

Definition 3.1
.

A type III K3 surface is a proper scheme
Y
over
\mathbb{C}
of dimension
2
with normal crossing singularities such that the dualising sheaf
\omega_{Y}
is trivial, the dual complex of
Y
is a triangulation of
S^{2}
, and the triple point formula is satisfied. This means that for each irreducible component
C
of the singular locus of
Y
, if
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are the connected components of the inverse image of
C
on the normalisation of
Y
, then
C_{1}^{2}+C_{2}^{2}=-2
.

We are interested in invariants of type III K3 surfaces.

Lemma 3.2
.

For a type III K3 surface
Y
the group
H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
is finite cyclic, and its order is equal to the index of
Y
as defined in
[
FS86
, p. 4]
.

Proof.

We use the formalism of nearby and vanishing cycles of
[
SGA73
]
, cf.
[
PS08
,
\lx@sectionsign
C.2.2]
.

We may assume (replacing
Y
by a locally trivial deformation) that
Y
is smoothable (equivalently, d-semistable in Friedman’s terminology).
Let
\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}
be a semistable smoothing of
Y
over the disc
\mathbb{D}
. Let
t\in\mathbb{D}
be very general,
0<|t|\ll 1
, and let
i_{t}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{t}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
denote the fibre of
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
over
t\in\mathbb{D}
.
Possibly after passing to a disc of smaller radius, we have a deformation retraction
r\colon\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow Y
, and its restriction
r_{t}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{t}\rightarrow Y
to
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
, the so called Clemens collapsing map.

We define the complex of nearby cocycles by

\psi_{p}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}}=R{r_{t}}_{*}i_{t}^{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}}=R{r_{t}}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{t}}.

Let
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{Y}\rightarrow\psi_{p}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
be the map of complexes of sheaves on
Y
induced by
r_{t}
.
We define the complex of vanishing cocycles
\phi_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
to be the cone over this map, so that we have a distinguished triangle in the derived category of sheaves on
Y

\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{Y}\rightarrow\psi_{p}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\phi_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[+1]}}{{\longrightarrow}}

Passing to hypercohomology yields a long exact sequence

(3.3)

\cdots\rightarrow H^{k}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{k}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{k}(\phi_{p}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\rightarrow H^{k+1}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\cdots

Each irreducible component of the normalisation of
Y
is a maximal log Calabi–Yau surface, and so admits a toric model and an associated almost toric fibration for some choice of ample line bundle. These glue to give a
C^{\infty}
singular torus fibration
f^{\vee}\colon Y\rightarrow B^{\vee}
over
B^{\vee}\simeq S^{2}
. (Note: since
Y
is not assumed projective, we do not have compatible symplectic forms on the components of
Y
.)
Let
\Gamma\subset B^{\vee}
be the
1
-skeleton of the induced polyhedral subdivision of
B^{\vee}
, a trivalent graph.
It follows from the explicit description of
r_{t}
(see e.g.
[
Per77
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2]
) that there is a torus fibration
g^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{t}\rightarrow B^{\vee}
such that
f^{\vee}\circ r_{t}=g^{\vee}
: indeed, the restriction
r_{t}^{-1}(Y_{i})\rightarrow Y_{i}
of
r_{t}
to (the normalisation of) each irreducible component of
Y
is given by the real oriented blowup of the boundary
D_{i}\subset Y_{i}
. Cf.
[
Sym03
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.2]
. (Alternatively, one can use the Kato–Nakayama space of the smooth log structure on
Y
over the standard log point (obtained by restricting the divisorial log structures on
(\mathcal{Y},Y)/(\mathbb{D},0)
) to study the collapsing map
r_{t}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{t}\rightarrow Y
and the monodromy action on
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
cf. e.g.
[
NO10
, Theorem 0.3]
.)

Let
N=r_{t}^{-1}(\operatorname{Sing}Y)={f^{\vee}}^{-1}(\Gamma)
.
By excision, we also have a distinguished triangle

\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{Sing}Y}\rightarrow R{r_{t}}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\rightarrow\phi_{p}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[+1]}}{{\longrightarrow}}

and associated long exact sequence of hypercohomology

\cdots\rightarrow H^{k}(\operatorname{Sing}Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{k}(N,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{k}(\phi_{p}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\rightarrow H^{k+1}(\operatorname{Sing}Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\cdots

Let
E
denote the set of edges of the graph
\Gamma
.
Note that
H^{2}(\operatorname{Sing}Y,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{E}
by Mayer–Vietoris and
H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{Z})=0
by dimension. Thus we have an exact sequence

(3.4)

H^{2}(\operatorname{Sing}Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(N,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{2}(\phi_{p}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\rightarrow 0.

The Leray spectral sequence for the restriction
\pi\colon N\rightarrow\Gamma
of the torus fibration
g^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{t}\rightarrow B^{\vee}
to
\Gamma
yields a short exact sequence

(3.5)

0\rightarrow H^{1}(\Gamma,R^{1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})\rightarrow H^{2}(N,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow 0

where the second map to
\mathbb{Z}=H^{0}(R^{2}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})
is cap product with the fibre class
\gamma^{\vee}
.
Now consider the
2
-cycle
\Sigma^{\vee}\subset N
defined as follows (cf. also
[
EF21
]
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.2). Over the interior of each edge of
\Gamma
the restriction of
\Sigma^{\vee}
is a cylinder with fibre the vanishing cycle of the specialisation map
r_{t}
.
Over a (trivalent) vertex of
\Gamma
the fibre of
\Sigma^{\vee}\rightarrow\Gamma
is a
2
-chain in the
2
-torus fibre of
\pi
with boundary the negative sum of the boundaries of these cylinders (suitably oriented).
The class of
\Sigma^{\vee}
in
H_{2}(N,\mathbb{Z})
is determined up to a multiple of the fibre class
\gamma^{\vee}
.
Let
i\colon\Sigma^{\vee}\hookrightarrow N
denote the closed embedding of
\Sigma^{\vee}
and
j\colon N\setminus\Sigma^{\vee}\hookrightarrow N
the open embedding of its complement. Let
a\colon N\setminus\Sigma^{\vee}\rightarrow\Gamma
and
b\colon\Sigma^{\vee}\rightarrow\Gamma
denote the restrictions of
\pi
.
Applying
R\pi_{*}
to the short exact sequence of sheaves on
N

0\rightarrow j_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N\setminus\Sigma^{\vee}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\rightarrow i_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\Sigma^{\vee}}\rightarrow 0

yields a short exact sequence of sheaves on
\Gamma

0\rightarrow R^{1}a_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N\setminus\Sigma^{\vee}}\rightarrow R^{1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\rightarrow R^{1}b_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\Sigma^{\vee}}\rightarrow 0.

The first term
R^{1}a_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N\setminus\Sigma^{\vee}}
equals
\bigoplus_{e\in E}{j_{e}}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{e^{o}}
where
j_{e}\colon e^{o}\hookrightarrow\Gamma
denotes the inclusion of the interior of the edge
e
of
\Gamma
(note that the fibre of
a
over a vertex of
\Gamma
is a topological disc). Passing to cohomology yields an exact sequence

(3.6)

\mathbb{Z}^{E}\rightarrow H^{1}(R^{1}g_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow 0

where the second map is cap product with
[\Sigma^{\vee}]
. Combining (
3.4
), (
3.5
), and (
3.6
), we deduce that

(3.7)

H^{2}(\phi_{p}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\operatorname{coker}(H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(N,\mathbb{Z}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}^{2}

where the isomorphism is given by cap product with
[\Sigma^{\vee}]
and
[\gamma^{\vee}]
.

The torus fibration
g^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{t}\rightarrow B^{\vee}
admits a topological section; let
s^{\vee}\in H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
denote the class of a section (identifying
H_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})=H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
by Poincaré duality).
Existence of a section implies that the Leray spectral sequence for
g^{\vee}
degenerates at
E_{2}
and
H^{1}(R^{1}g^{\vee}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{t}})={\gamma^{\vee}}^{\perp}/\langle\gamma^{\vee}\rangle
.
The sublattice
\langle\gamma^{\vee},s^{\vee}\rangle\subset H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
is isometric to
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
, in particular unimodular. The map
\langle\gamma^{\vee},s^{\vee}\rangle^{\perp}\rightarrow{\gamma^{\vee}}^{\perp}/\langle\gamma^{\vee}\rangle
is an isometry, so
{\gamma^{\vee}}^{\perp}/\langle\gamma^{\vee}\rangle
is also unimodular, using Poincaré duality for
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
.
Now (
3.7
) and (
3.3
) yield

H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}/k\mathbb{Z}

where
k
is the divisibility of
\PD[\Sigma^{\vee}]
in
{\gamma^{\vee}}^{\perp}/\langle\gamma^{\vee}\rangle
.

The usual Picard–Lefschetz formula for a degeneration of a complex curve to a nodal curve yields the formula

T(x)=x+(x\cdot\Sigma^{\vee})\gamma^{\vee}

for the action of the monodromy transformation
T
on
{\gamma^{\vee}}^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
,
cf. e.g.
[
RS20
, Proposition 2.8]
.
Thus
\PD[\Sigma^{\vee}]\in{\gamma^{\vee}}^{\perp}/\langle\gamma^{\vee}\rangle
coincides up to sign with the class
\delta
defined in
[
FS86
, p. 7]
, and in particular
k
coincides with the Friedman-Scattone index by
[
FS86
, Lemma 1.1]

∎

Definition 3.8
.

Suppose
Y
is a type III K3 surface. We define invariants
n=n(Y),k=k(Y)\in\mathbb{N}
where
2n
is the number of triple points of
Y
, and
k
is the order of
H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark 3.9
.

We have
k^{2}\mid n
by
[
FS86
]
and Lemma
3.2
.
Indeed, in the terminology of
[
FS86
]
, let
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
be a semistable smoothing of a locally trivial deformation of
Y
and
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
a general fibre of
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
. Consider the monodromy weight filtration on
H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t})
. We have

W_{0}=\mathbb{Z}\cdot\gamma=W_{1}\subset W_{2}=W_{3}=\gamma^{\perp}\subset W_{4}=H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})

for some primitive isotropic class
\gamma\in H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
. In particular
W_{2}/W_{0}=\gamma^{\perp}/\langle\gamma\rangle
is the unimodular even lattice of signature
(2,18)
.
There is a class
\delta\in W_{2}/W_{0}
, uniquely determined up to sign, such that
\delta^{2}=2n
and
k
is the divisibility of
\delta\in W_{2}/W_{0}
. Thus
k^{2}\mid n
as claimed.

Let
Y
be a type III K3 surface. The sheaf

\mathcal{T}^{1}_{Y}=\cExt^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y})

is the push forward of a line bundle on the singular locus of
Y
, called the
infinitesimal normal bundle
in
[
Fri83b
]
, see Definition 1.9 and Proposition 2.3 therein.
Following
[
Fri83b
, Definition 1.13]
, we say
Y
is
d-semistable
if
\mathcal{T}^{1}_{Y}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Sing}Y}
.
The following are equivalent (1)
Y
is d-semistable, (2)
Y
is smoothable, and (3)
Y
admits a semistable smoothing, by
[
Fri83b
]
, Lemma 1.11, Proposition 2.5, and Theorem 5.10. Following
[
FS86
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
, let
Y^{[i]}
denote the disjoint union of the normalisations of the codimension
i
strata of
Y
and write

L=\operatorname{ker}(H^{2}(Y^{[0]},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y^{[1]},\mathbb{Z})).

The Deligne mixed Hodge structure on
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
is classified by the extension class for the graded pieces of the weight filtration, which is given by a homomorphism
\phi\colon L\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
. The Picard group of
Y
is identified with
\operatorname{ker}(\phi)\subset L

by restriction. Let
Y=\bigcup Y_{i}
denote the irreducible components of
Y
. There are classes
\xi_{i}\in L
such that, in case
Y
admits a semistable smoothing
(Y\subset\mathcal{Y})/(0\in\mathbb{D})
,
\xi_{i}
equals the class of the restriction of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{i})
.
Then
Y
is d-semistable if and only if the classes
\xi_{i}\in L
are realised by line bundles on
Y
, that is, if and only if
\phi(\xi_{i})=1
for all
i

[
FS86
, p. 25]
. In particular, if
Y
has split mixed Hodge structure (equivalently,
\phi\colon L\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
is trivial), then
Y
is d-semistable.

Given a type III d-semistable K3 surface
Y
, following
[
FS86
]
, there are two local moves one can perform to get another type III K3 surface
Y^{\prime}
, called elementary modifications of types 1) and 2). See Figures
3.1
and
3.2
. Type 1) elementary modifications are clearly uniquely determined. In the case of type 2), the elementary modification is uniquely determined by requiring that
Y^{\prime}
is d-semistable by Lemma
3.10
below.

Figure 3.1.
Type 1) elementary modification of a type III K3 surface. Intersection numbers are in blue.

Figure 3.2.
Type 2) elementary modification of a type III K3 surface.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
Y
be a type III K3 surface and
C
an irreducible component of the singular locus of
Y
such that the connected components of the inverse image of
C
in the normalisation of
Y
are
(-1)
-curves.

Working locally analytically in a neighbourhood of
C
, let
Y_{1},Y_{2}
be the components of
Y
containing
C
and
Y_{3},Y_{4}
the components intersecting
C
transversely in a point. For
i=1,2
let
Y^{\prime}_{i}
be the contractions of the
(-1)
-curve
C\subset Y_{i}
.
For
i=3,4
let
Y^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
be the blowup of the point
C\cap Y_{i}
, with exceptional divisor
C^{\prime}_{i}
.
Let
Y^{\prime}
be the normal crossing surface and
C^{\prime}\subset Y^{\prime}
the component of the singular locus obtained as follows:

Y\setminus C=Y^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime}
, and locally analytically near
C^{\prime}\subset Y^{\prime}
the surface
Y^{\prime}
is obtained by glueing the surfaces
Y^{\prime}_{3},Y^{\prime}_{4}
via an identification
\theta\colon C^{\prime}_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\prime}_{4}
, with
C^{\prime}\subset Y^{\prime}
the resulting component of the singular locus. Note that
\theta
is determined up to
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{1},0,\infty)
.
See Figure
3.2
.

If
Y
is d-semistable then there is a unique choice of the identification
\theta
such that
Y^{\prime}
is also d-semistable.

Proof.

If
Y
is d-semistable, existence of
\theta
such that
Y^{\prime}
is also d-semistable follows from Proposition
3.13
(recall that d-semistability is equivalent to smoothability). Uniqueness of
\theta
follows from
[
Lut
, Proposition 3.4]
. Indeed, let
Y^{\prime}
be a d-semistable glueing,
L^{\prime}=\operatorname{ker}(H^{2}({Y^{\prime}}^{[0]},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}({Y^{\prime}}^{[1]},\mathbb{Z}))
, and
\phi_{Y^{\prime}}\colon L^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
the homomorphism representing the extension class of the mixed Hodge structure on
H^{2}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})
.
Let
\xi_{3}\in L^{\prime}
denote the restriction of
c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime}_{3}))
, where
(Y^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})/(0\in\mathbb{D})
is a semistable smoothing of
Y^{\prime}
.
Then
\phi_{Y^{\prime}}(\xi_{1})=1
. If now
Y^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is obtained from
Y^{\prime}
by modifying the glueing along
C^{\prime}
by
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}

then
\phi_{Y^{\prime}_{\lambda}}(\xi_{3})=\lambda^{\pm 1}
(with the sign determined by the choice of orientations) by
[
Lut
, Definition 3.1 and Proposition 3.4]
. In particular,
Y^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is not d-semistable for
\lambda\neq 1
.
∎

Let
Y
be a d-semistable type III K3 surface.
Let
