(Y\subset\mathcal{Y})/(0\in\mathbb{D})
be a semistable smoothing of
Y
.
Let
t\in\mathbb{D}
,
0<|t|\ll 1
, and let
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
be the fibre of
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
over
t\in\mathbb{D}
.
Let
T\colon H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
be the monodromy action and
N=\log T
(recall
T
is unipotent since
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
is semistable).
Possibly after shrinking the radius of the disc, we have a retraction

r\colon\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow Y

and the induced map

r_{t}=r\circ i_{t}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{t}\rightarrow Y

where
i_{t}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{t}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is the inclusion.

Let
Y=\bigcup_{i=1}^{V}Y_{i}
be the irreducible components of
Y
.
We have the exact sequence

(3.11)

0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{V}/\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\operatorname{ker}N\rightarrow 0,

where the first arrow is given by

e_{i}\mapsto\xi_{i}:=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{i})|_{Y})

and the second arrow is given by
r_{t}^{*}
, see
[
FS86
, (4.13)]
.
(This is part of the Clemens-Schmid exact sequence
[
PS08
, Corollary 11.44]
, with the caveats that we are working over
\mathbb{Z}
instead of
\mathbb{Q}
and the total space
\mathcal{Y}
is not necessarily Kähler.)
The map
r_{t}^{*}\colon H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\operatorname{ker}N\subset H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
is a morphism of mixed Hodge structures, where we endow
H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
with the limiting mixed Hodge structure
[
PS08
, Theorem 11.29]
.

Lemma 3.12
.

The Hodge filtration on
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})
is given by
F^{1}\subset F^{0}=H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})
, and
F^{1}H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})
is the full inverse image of
F^{1}\operatorname{ker}N_{\mathbb{C}}
under the surjection
r_{t}^{*}\colon H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{ker}N_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

The Hodge filtration on
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})
is described in
[
FS86
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
.
The kernel of
r_{t}^{*}
is contained in
F^{1}H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})
by
[
FS86
, Proof of Proposition 3.1]
.
The map
F^{1}H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})\rightarrow F^{1}\operatorname{ker}N_{\mathbb{C}}
is surjective by strictness of morphisms of mixed Hodge structures
[
PS08
, Corollary 3.6]
.
∎

Let
f\colon Y\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
be an elementary modification of
Y
, with exceptional curves
C\subset Y
and
C^{\prime}\subset Y^{\prime}
. Then
f
is induced by the (analytic) flop
F\colon\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
of the
(-1,-1)
-curve
C\subset\mathcal{Y}
. Let
p\colon\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be the blowup of
C
, with exceptional divisor
E\rightarrow C
isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
q\colon\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
the contraction of
E
along the other ruling.
We have an isomorphism

H^{2}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})

given by the composition
q_{*}p^{*}
, and so an isomorphism

\Phi\colon H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{2}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})

using the retractions of the total spaces of the fibrations onto the special fibres.

Proposition 3.13
.

The isomorphism
\Phi\colon H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})
of abelian groups is an isomorphism of mixed Hodge structures.

Proof.

The isomorphism
\Phi
fits into a commutative diagram

(3.14)

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>\mathbb{Z}^{V}/\mathbb{Z}@>{}>{}>H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})@>{}>{}>\operatorname{ker}N@>{}>{}>0\\
\Big\|@V{\Phi}V{}V\Big\|\\
0@>{}>{}>\mathbb{Z}^{V}/\mathbb{Z}@>{}>{}>H^{2}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})@>{}>{}>\operatorname{ker}N@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

Let
p_{0}\colon\tilde{Y}\rightarrow Y
and
q_{0}\colon\tilde{Y}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
be the restrictions of
p
and
q
to the special fibre
\tilde{Y}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
(with its reduced structure).
The maps
p_{0}^{*}
and
q_{0}^{*}
are morphisms of mixed Hodge structures
[
Del74
, Proposition 8.2.2]
or
[
PS08
, Theorem 5.33]
. They coincide with
p^{*}
and
q^{*}
under the identifications given by the retractions of the total spaces of the fibrations to the special fibres.
For each of
Y
,
Y^{\prime}
, and
\tilde{Y}
, we have
W_{0}=H^{2}(|\Delta|,\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
by the Mayer–Vietoris spectral sequence, where
\Delta
denotes the dual complex of the normal crossing surface.
We deduce that
p_{0}^{*}
and
q_{0}^{*}
induce isomorphisms on
W_{0}
using
p_{*}p^{*}=q_{*}q^{*}=\operatorname{id}

[
Bre97
, Theorem 5.2(4)]
(note
p_{*}\colon H_{6}(\tilde{\mathcal{Y}},\partial\tilde{\mathcal{Y}})\rightarrow H_{6}(\mathcal{Y},\partial\mathcal{Y})
satisfies
p_{*}[\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}]=[\mathcal{Y}]
since
p
is a birational morphism).
Using
q_{*}q^{*}=\operatorname{id}
again, we find that the isomorphism

\Phi\colon H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})
induced by
q_{*}p^{*}
sends
W_{0}
to
W_{0}
.
The weight filtration on
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
and
H^{2}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})
is given by
W_{0}=W_{1}\subset W_{2}=H^{2}
. So the isomorphism
\Phi\colon H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})
respects the weight filtrations, and the induced map
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
respects the Hodge filtrations by the commutativity of the diagram
3.14
and Lemma
3.12
, thus
\Phi
is an isomorphism of mixed Hodge structures.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

(See
[
FS86
, Theorem 0.6]
and
[
Lut
, Theorem 3.7]
)
Suppose we are given two type III K3 surfaces
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
with the same invariants
k
and
n
, and such that
both
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
have split mixed Hodge structure. Then
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are related by a sequence of elementary modifications.

Proof.

By
[
FS86
, Theorem 0.6]
,
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are related by a sequence of elementary modifications followed by a locally trivial deformation. There is a unique type III K3 in each locally trivial deformation type with split mixed Hodge structure by the Global Torelli theorem for type III K3 surfaces
[
Lut
, Theorem 3.7]
. Elementary modifications preserve the condition that the mixed Hodge structure is split by Proposition
3.13
. Thus the locally trivial deformation is not needed in this case.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
Y
be a type III K3 surface with split mixed Hodge structure.
There exists a sequence of elementary modifications
Y\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
such that
Y^{\prime}
is projective (and also has split mixed Hodge structure).

Proof.

Elementary modifications induce isomorphisms of MHS and in particular preserve the condition that the MHS is split by Proposition
3.13
.
By Lemma
3.15
and Lemma
3.29
there exists a sequence of elementary modifications
Y\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
such that
Y^{\prime}

is in
(-1)
-form. We claim that
Y^{\prime}
is projective. There is a line bundle
L
on
Y^{\prime}
such that the restriction of
L
to the normalisation of each irreducible component
Y^{\prime}_{i}
of
Y^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the anti-canonical line bundle
-K_{Y^{\prime}_{i}}
of
Y^{\prime}_{i}
. Indeed by the definition of
(-1)
-form and the adjunction formula,
-K_{Y^{\prime}_{i}}
has degree
1
on each component of the boundary, so these line bundles glue to a line bundle
L
on
Y^{\prime}
since
Y^{\prime}
has split MHS.
A positive multiple of
-K_{Y^{\prime}_{i}}
defines a birational morphism
Y^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow\bar{Y}^{\prime}_{i}
with exceptional locus a configuration of
(-2)
-curves
C_{ij}
(contained in the complement of the boundary of
Y^{\prime}_{i}
) that are contracted to Du Val singularities on
\bar{Y}^{\prime}_{i}
. So there exist rational numbers
0<\epsilon_{ij}\ll 1
such that the line bundle

L^{\prime}=L^{\otimes N}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y}(-N(\textstyle{\sum\epsilon_{ij}C_{ij}}))

is ample for
N\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently divisible, and
Y^{\prime}
is projective as claimed.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

As already observed in
[
LU24
]
, if
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are d-semistable type III K3 surfaces related by an elementary modification then there is an equivalence

\Coh Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Coh Y^{\prime}

obtained as follows.
Let
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
be a semistable smoothing of
Y
over the disc. Then the elementary modification
Y\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
with exceptional curve
C\subset Y
is induced by the (analytic) flop
\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}/\mathbb{D}
of the
(-1,-1)
-curve
C\subset\mathcal{Y}
.
By
[
Bri02
]
the flop induces an equivalence

\Coh\mathcal{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Coh\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},

and this yields an equivalence

\Coh Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Coh Y^{\prime}

by
[
BP10
]
.

3.2.
Integral affine compactification of
M

3.2.1.
Construction of the closed symplectic K3 surface
X

Start with a type III K3 surface
Y
.
The dual complex of
Y
, say
\Delta
, gives a triangulation of
S^{2}
.

\Delta
has a vertex for each irreducible component
(Y_{i},D_{i})
of the normalisation of
Y
. We use
[
GHK15a
]
to associate to
Y
an integral affine
S^{2}
with singularities.
Let
S
denote the subset of vertices of
\Delta
which correspond to non-toric components.
The construction of
[
GHK15a
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.2]
endows the open star of each vertex with the structure of an integral affine manifold, with a singularity at the vertex if if belongs to
S
. Here we take each edge of
\Delta
to have integral affine length one, and each (open) face of
\Delta
to be a standard integral affine triangle.
The triple point condition implies that these integral affine structures are compatible on overlaps, and so we can glue the stars of different vertices. We call
B^{\natural}
the resulting integral affine
S^{2}
with singularities at
S
.

Next, we perturb
B^{\natural}
so as to get an integral affine
S^{2}
all of whose singularities are focus-focus, following
[
GHK15a
, pp. 107–108, Step IV]
.
Choose a toric model for each
Y_{i}
.
Near each point
s
in the singular locus of
B^{\natural}
, we replace the singularity of the integral affine structure at
s\in B^{\natural}
by singularities of focus-focus type: one for each interior blow up in the toric model for
Y
, with invariant direction given by the corresponding ray in the toric model. (This is usually an edge of
\Delta
, so long as the divisor for that ray does not come from a corner blow-up in the toric model.)
The exact position of the singularities along their invariant rays is immaterial, but we will assume that they are close to
s
.

Note that the construction above only depends on the complex deformation type of
Y
.
In the case where
Y
has split mixed Hodge structure, we are also in the setting of Section
2
. We let
(Y_{i},D_{i})
, for varying
i
, denote the irreducible components of the normalisation of
Y
together with the inverse image of the singular locus of
Y
. Each
(Y_{i},D_{i})
is a maximal log Calabi–Yau surface.
For each
(Y_{i},D_{i})
(together with a choice of toric model), we have an integral affine manifold with singularities
B_{i}
, as first introduced in Section
2.3.1
.
This is compatible with the integral affine manifold
B
as follows: by construction, on the star of each vertex of
\Delta
, the integral affine manifold with singularities
B
restricts to
B_{i}^{\circ}
, i.e. the interior of
B_{i}
.
(With our choice of cut-offs for
B_{i}
, this holds ‘on the nose’, except that near the corners of the star of the vertex,
B_{i}
– and so also
B_{i}^{\circ}
– ‘spills out’ by a very small amount, allowing it to have smooth boundary.)

Different choices of toric model for
(Y_{i},D_{i})
(and different choices of positions for the focus-focus singularities when perturbing from
B^{\natural}
) give different integral affine manifolds
B
. As with the
B_{i}
, these are related by nodal slides and cut transfers (see
[
HK
, Section 3.2]
for definitions).

Moreover, we have the following.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
Y
be any type III K3 surface, and let
B
be the integral affine
S^{2}
with singularities associated to its complex deformation type as above.
We have the following:

(i)

There is a symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
which is the total space of an almost-toric fibration
\pi_{X}:X\to B
, with focus-focus critical fibres at the singularities of
B
and smooth otherwise, and which admits a Lagrangian section
L_{0}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\omega})
is any other such symplectic manifold, with almost-toric fibration
\pi_{\widetilde{X}}:\widetilde{X}\to B
and section
\widetilde{L}_{0}
. Then
(X,\omega)
and
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\omega})
are symplectomorphic, and the symplectomorphism between them can be taken to be fibred away from an arbitrary small neighbourhood of each of the nodal fibres, and to match
L_{0}
and
\widetilde{L}_{0}
.

(iii)

Suppose that
B^{\prime}
is an integral affine manifold with singularities obtained from
B^{\natural}
by making different auxiliary choices, with associated almost-toric fibration
\pi_{X^{\prime}}:X^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
, and section
L^{\prime}_{0}
. Then there is a symplectomorphism from
(X,\omega)
to
(X^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
, which can be taken to intertwine the diffeomorphism
B\xrightarrow{\simeq}B^{\prime}
, given by nodal slides and cut transfers, away from an arbitrary neighbourhood of the nodes.

Proof.

This follows from standard results on almost-toric fibrations in
[
Eva23
,
Sym03
,
Zun03
]
. Existence of
(X,\omega)
(together with
\pi_{X}
and
L_{0}
) follows for instance by taking a cover of
B
by two open discs (intersecting, say, in an annulus containing no singular points), and using
[
Sym03
, Corollary 5.4]
together with e.g.
[
Eva23
, Theorem 2.26]
for the gluing.
The only choice available is the Vũ Ngọc invariants of each singular fibre. In particular, our uniqueness statement follows e.g. from
[
Eva23
, Theorem 6.17 and Theorem 2.26]
. Finally, (iii) follows from standard results about nodal slides and cut transfers (also called mutations), see e.g.
[
Eva23
, Section 8]
for exposition.
∎

Lemma 3.19
.

Given a type III K3 surface
Y
, let
(X,\omega)
be the symplectic manifold constructed above. Then
X
is a diffeomorphic to a K3 surface.

Proof.

Any almost-toric fibration over the sphere with only focus-focus singularities automatically has 24 singular fibres: see for instance
[
KS06
, Theorem 2]
on the ‘integral affine Gauss–Bonnet’ formula.
This means that smoothly,
X
is the total space of a torus fibration over sphere with Leschetz singularities. By a classical result of Moishezon and Livne
[
Moi77
]
, the diffeomorphism type of any such space is determined by the number of singularities (a multiple of 12); here
X
must be a K3 surface.
∎

3.2.2.
Compactifying
M

We will need the following notion.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
(N,\theta_{N})
and
(N^{\prime},\theta_{N}^{\prime})
be Liouville domains. We say that they are Liouville equivalent if, possibly after adding a finite cylindrical collar to
N^{\prime}
, there exists a codimension zero proper symplectic embedding
\phi:N^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\hat{N}
, where
\hat{N}
is the cylindrical completion of
N
, such that
\phi(\partial N^{\prime})
is a hypersurface in
\hat{N}\backslash N
, transverse to the Liouville flow
Z_{\hat{N}}
; and
\phi^{\ast}\theta_{\hat{N}}=\theta_{N^{\prime}}+df
, where the function
f
is not assumed to have support in the interior of
N^{\prime}
(formally, it is defined on a small open thickening of
N^{\prime}
).

This is clearly a reflexive condition. Also, the condition on the pull-back of the Liouville form is automatically satisfied if
H^{1}(N;\mathbb{R})=0
(or equivalently,
H^{1}(N^{\prime};\mathbb{R})=0
).

Lemma 3.21
.

Suppose that
(N,\theta_{N})
and
(N^{\prime},\theta_{N}^{\prime})
are Liouville equivalent domains, with symplectic embedding
\phi:N^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\hat{N}
. Then
\phi
induces a quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{W}(N)\simeq\mathcal{W}(N^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We use the notation of Definition
3.20
.
Under the additional assumption that the smooth function
f
has support in the interior of
N
, this is standard. The condition on
f
can be weakened by using
[
KS25
, Lemma 2.2]
.
∎

We now return to our construction.

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
Y
be a type III K3 surface with split mixed Hodge structure. Let
(X,\omega)
be the symplectic manifold of Construction
3.2.1
, and let
(M,\theta_{M})
be the Weinstein domain mirror to
Y
which we studied in Section
2
, first introduced in Definition
2.20
.
Fix choices of toric models for the components of
Y
, and let
\pi_{X}:X\to B
be the associated almost-toric fibration on
X
, and
\pi:M\to S^{2}
be the singular Lagrangian fibration on
M
.
Let
\Gamma\subset B
the trivalent graph with vertices at the barycenters of faces of
\Delta
, and straight-line edges.

Then there exists a smooth symplectic surface
\Sigma\subset X
such that:

(i)

We have
[\Sigma]=[\omega]\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{R})
. This implies that for
\nu\Sigma
a tubular neighbourhood of
\Sigma
,
X\backslash(\nu\Sigma)
is a Liouville domain, with Liouville form, say,
\theta_{X\backslash(\nu\Sigma)}
. (See e.g.
[
DL19
]
for a careful overview of such Liouville forms.)

(ii)

(M,\theta_{M})
and
(X\backslash(\nu\Sigma),\theta_{X\backslash(\nu\Sigma)})
are Liouville equivalent domains.

(iii)

The surface
\Sigma
projects to the graph
\Gamma
thickened at its vertices (for an arbitrarily small thickening).

(iv)

The Lagrangian torus fibration
\pi_{X}:X\to B
extends the Lagrangian torus fibration
\pi:M\to S^{2}
, compatibly with the inclusions of the
B_{i}
in
B
. Moreover, under the identification in (ii), the reference Lagrangian section
L_{0}\subset M
for
\pi
gets mapped to the reference Lagrangian section
L_{0}\subset X
for
\pi_{X}
.

Note that
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})=0
, which simplifies the requirements for Liouville equivalence.

Proof.

The integral affine manifold
B
has two different interesting covers by integral affine balls:

(i)

Using balls centred at each of the vertices of the triangulation, we have
B=\bigcup_{i}B_{i}(-\delta)
, where
B_{i}(-\delta)
denotes the interior of
B_{i}[-\delta]
(to use the notation of Section
2
). We arrange for it to contain all the singular points emanating from the vertex at which it’s centred, but none other. See Figure
3.3
.

(ii)

For each face of
\Delta
, let
\Delta_{v}\subset B
be the corresponding closed subset of
B
(where the index
v
is in
Y^{[2]}
), and let
\Delta_{v}(\delta^{\prime})
be a small open thickening of it in
B
(with no additional critical singularities). Then
B=\bigcup_{v}\Delta_{v}(\delta^{\prime})
is also an open cover.

Figure 3.3.
The triangulation
\Delta
(in black) with the singularities of
B
, the covering sets
B_{i}(-\delta)
(in green), and
\Gamma\subset B
(in orange).

The curve
\Sigma
can be constructed by working with the cover
\bigcup_{v}\Delta_{v}(\delta^{\prime})
and using standard techniques from tropical and (almost-)toric geometry, à la Mikhalkin–Viro. We spell this out in order to introduce relevant notation.
Draw a tropical line in each simplex of
\Delta
with trivalent vertex at the barycenter and legs intersecting the edges of the triangle at the midpoints. The resulting graph is
\Gamma
. Let
\Gamma_{v}
be its restriction to the simplex indexed by
v\in Y^{[2]}
.

Let
\Delta_{v}^{\circ}
be the interior of
\Delta_{v}
.
Assume first that there are no nodes in
\Delta_{v}^{\circ}
. Standard toric geometry gives an identification
\pi_{X}^{-1}(\Delta_{v}^{\circ})\cong(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2}
, where
(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2}
is equipped with the restriction of the Fubini-Study form and we’re free to choose to take
L_{0}
to
(\mathbb{R}_{>0})^{2}
.
Let
\Delta_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
be a toric polytope for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and
\Gamma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\subset\Delta_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
the graph for the standard line in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, say
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\text{std}}=\{X+Y+Z=0\}
(which is disjoint from the image of
L_{0}
).
In the symplectic category, there is a small Hamiltonian isotopy of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, supported on a neighbourhood of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\text{std}}
and fixing the toric anticanonical divisor in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
pointwise, which takes
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\text{std}}
to a symplectic curve
\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
with the following properties.
In a neighbourhood of the boundary of
\Delta_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
,
\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
is given by a ‘visible’ symplectic surface (in the sense of
[
Sym03
, Section 7.1]
) over each of the three legs of
\Gamma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
; moreover, we can take these to be linear (in terms of the affine linear structures on toric fibres), and diagonally opposite, on each fibre, to the parallel linear
S^{1}
containing the point on
L_{0}
.

Deleting the toric anti-canonical divisor in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we get a symplectic pair-of-pants in
(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2}
, and hence in
\pi_{X}^{-1}(\Delta_{v}^{\circ})
, say
\Sigma_{v}
. This is (almost) fibred over a graph
\Gamma_{v}\subset\Delta_{v}
, with a small smearing at the barycenter of
\Delta_{v}
.
If there are nodes
\Delta_{v}^{\circ}
, pushing them (along their invariant rays) into the corner of
\Delta_{v}
instead gives a degeneration of
\pi_{X}^{-1}(\Delta_{v}^{\circ})
to
(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2}
.
As this happens away from a neighbourhood of
\Gamma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
, we can similarly get a symplectic pair-of-pants
\Sigma_{v}\subset\pi_{X}^{-1}(\Delta_{v}^{\circ})
, fibred over a trivalent graph
\Gamma_{v}\subset\Delta_{v}
.
Extending linearly over each of the legs, we get pairs-of-pants
\Sigma_{v}
(for all
v\in Y^{[2]}
) which can be glued together to get a symplectic surface
\Sigma
.
Let
\Gamma\subset B
be the trivalent graph given by patching together the
\Gamma_{v}
.
The surface
\Sigma
is fibred over
\Gamma
away from neighbourhoods of the vertices of
\Gamma
(which can be taken to be arbitrarily small).
Also, note that
\Sigma
is independent of the auxiliary choices made above, including choices of toric models for the
(Y_{i},D_{i})
, up to (small) symplectic isotopy. (Recall that in this dimension, a tubular neighbourhood of a closed smooth symplectic surface in
X
is determined by its genus, its symplectic area and its self-intersection number.)

We want to show that
X\backslash(\nu\Sigma)
and
M
are Liouville equivalent. We’ll use the Weinstein handlebody decomposition of
M
from Section
2.3.4
to
construct a suitable symplectic embedding from
M
(with an appropriate ‘finite height’ Liouville cut-off) to
X\backslash(\nu\Sigma)
.
We start with our given embeddings, compatible with Lagrangian fibrations:

(3.23)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\,\,\bigsqcup_{i}M_{U_{i}}\,\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{U_{i}}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\,\,\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}[-\delta]\,\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{2}}

For each
i,j
such that
Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}\neq\emptyset
, let
r_{ij}^{X}\subset B
be the segment joining
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
, and let
A_{ij}^{X}
be the linear Lagrangian annulus above
r_{ij}^{X}
containing the point in
L_{0}
in each fibre.
Then, using the description of Section
2.3.4
, we extend the embeddings in Equation
3.23
to get an embedding
M^{\circ}\hookrightarrow X
, mapping
A_{ij}
to
A_{ij}^{X}
.
Finally, by taking the distinguished Lagrangians
L_{0}\subset M
to
L_{0}\subset N
in the obvious way, this extends to a symplectic embedding
\iota:M\subset X
.
Given the choices we made when constructing
\Sigma
(in particular, the fact that it is disjoint from
L_{0}
), by taking our Weinstein handles to be sufficiently thin, we may assume that
\iota(M)\cap\Sigma=\emptyset
.

It remains to compare
\iota(M)
with
X\backslash(\nu\Sigma)
.
First, notice that over neighbourhoods of each intersection point
\Gamma\cap r_{ij}^{X}
, our explicit choices for
\Sigma
, and for the linear annuli
A_{ij}^{X}
, mean that we can readily get
\iota(M)
and
X\backslash(\nu\Sigma)
to match up
(including, up to Liouville deformation their outward-pointing Liouville forms).
This reduces the question to a local problem, in the neighbourhood of each
\Sigma_{v}\subset\Delta_{v}(\delta^{\prime})
.

Let
N^{\mathrm{loc}}
be the space
(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2}\backslash(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\cap(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2})
(identified using our Hamiltonian isotopy with
(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2}\backslash(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\text{trop}}\cap(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2})
),
i.e. the complement of the pair-of-pairs in
(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{2}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Experts will recognise this as a central toy example for many flavours of mirror symmetry (including through its natural identification with the generalised pair-of-pants one dimension up, i.e.
\{x+y+z=1\}\subset(\mathbb{C}^{\ast})^{3}
). As such, it
has a well-studied Weinstein handlebody decomposition (playing a role in for instance in
[
GN20
,
GS22
]
, with a very careful treatment in
[
Zho20
]
).
This can be described as follows: let
p_{1}^{\mathrm{loc}},p_{2}^{\mathrm{loc}}
and
p_{3}^{\mathrm{loc}}
be the barycentres of the three components of
\Delta_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\backslash\Gamma_{\text{trop}}
; let
r_{ij}^{\mathrm{loc}}
be the segment joining
p_{i}^{\mathrm{loc}}
and
p_{j}^{\mathrm{loc}}
; and let
V^{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\Delta_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
be the subset enclosed by the three
r_{ij}^{\mathrm{loc}}
. For each
i
and
j
, let
T_{i}^{\mathrm{loc}}
be the toric fibre above
p_{i}^{\mathrm{loc}}
, let
A_{ij}^{\mathrm{loc}}
be the linear Lagrangian annulus above
r_{ij}^{\mathrm{loc}}
which contains the totally real point in each fibre, and let
L^{\mathrm{loc}}:=\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{-1}(V)\cap(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2})
.
Then
N^{\mathrm{loc}}
can be described by (generalised) Weinstein handlebody attachments. Start with
\sqcup_{i}D^{\ast}T_{i}^{\mathrm{loc}}
.
Then, glue on three generalised Weinstein handles of the form
D^{\ast}(S^{1}\times[0,1])
, with attaching Legendrians
A_{ij}^{\mathrm{loc}}\cap(D^{\ast}T_{i}^{\mathrm{loc}}\sqcup D^{\ast}T_{j}^{\mathrm{loc}})
.
Taking their cores to the
A_{ij}^{\mathrm{loc}}
, we get an embedding of the resulting space,
(N^{\mathrm{loc}})^{\circ}
, into
N^{\mathrm{loc}}
.
Finally, glue on a Weinstein 2-handle, with attaching Legendrian
L^{\mathrm{loc}}\cap(N^{\mathrm{loc}})^{\circ}
; making the core of this handle to
L^{\mathrm{loc}}
to extend our map
(N^{\mathrm{loc}})^{\circ}\hookrightarrow N^{\mathrm{loc}}
, we get the desired description of
N^{\mathrm{loc}}
(up to Weinstein deformation equivalence).

Returning to our comparison of
M
and
X\backslash\nu\Sigma
, the Weinstein handlebody
N^{\mathrm{loc}}
readily gives (local) Liouville equivalences between
M
and
X\backslash\nu\Sigma
, on patches indexed by the vertices
v\in Y^{[2]}
, with the obvious identifications suggested by our notational choices. In particular, this completes our proof.
∎

Definition 3.24
.

Let
Y
be a type III K3 surface with split mixed Hodge structure, and let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
be as constructed in Proposition
3.22
. Then we say that
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
is the mirror compactifying K3 surface with divisor for
Y
.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
Y
be a type III K3 surface with split mixed Hodge structure, and
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
its compactifying mirror K3 surface with divisor. Then
k(Y)
, the order of
H^{3}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
, is also equal to the divisibility of
\Sigma
in
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Let
i\colon B^{\circ}\hookrightarrow B
denote the smooth locus of the integral affine manifold with singularities
B
, and
T^{\mathbb{Z}}_{B^{\circ}}
the sheaf of integral tangent vectors on
B^{\circ}
. Let
g\colon X^{\circ}\rightarrow B^{\circ}
denote the restriction of the Lagrangian torus fibration
g\colon X\rightarrow B
to
B^{\circ}
.
The radiance obstruction
[\rho]
of
B
lies in
H^{1}(B,i_{*}T_{B^{\circ}}^{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R})
(because
B
has focus-focus singularities so the radiance obstruction is locally trivial). We have identifications
T_{B^{\circ}}^{\mathbb{Z}}=R^{1}g^{\circ}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X^{\circ}}
by construction and

i_{*}R^{1}g^{\circ}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X}^{\circ}=R^{1}g_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B}
by
\mathbb{Z}
-simplicity of focus-focus singularities in the terminology of Gross
[
Gro98
, Definition 2.1]
.
Under these identifications the radiance obstruction
[\rho]\in H^{1}(i_{*}T^{\mathbb{Z}}_{B^{\circ}}\otimes\mathbb{R})
corresponds to the class of the symplectic form
[\omega]\in H^{1}(R^{1}g_{*}\underline{\mathbb{R}}_{X})
, cf.
[
KS06
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.1.1]
, or equivalently (the Poincaré dual of) the class of
\Sigma
. In fact we may work with
\mathbb{Z}
coefficients here because the class
[\omega]\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})
is integral, cf.
[
KS06
,
\lx@sectionsign
6.7.3]
.

Under the identifications

R^{1}g_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(R^{1}g^{\vee}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{t}},\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{B})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R^{1}g^{\vee}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{t}},

the first (SYZ duality) by construction of
g
and the second by Poincaré duality on the fibres of
g^{\vee}
,
one finds that the class
\PD[\Sigma]\in H^{1}(R^{1}g_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X})
corresponds (up to sign) to
\PD[\Sigma^{\vee}]\in H^{1}(R^{1}g^{\vee}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{t}})
, where
\Sigma^{\vee}
is the
2
-cycle on
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
constructed in the Proof of Lemma
3.2
.
Recall that
M
is the complement of a tubular neighbourhood of
\Sigma\subset X
and the restriction
f\colon M\rightarrow B
of the torus fibration
g\colon X\rightarrow B
admits a section. Thus there is a section of
g
disjoint from
\Sigma
; let
s
denote the class of one such section. Let
\gamma
denote the class of a fibre of
g
. Then, as in the Proof of Lemma
3.2
, we have

H^{1}(R^{1}g_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X})=\gamma^{\perp}/\langle\gamma\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longleftarrow}}\langle\gamma,s\rangle^{\perp},

and
\langle\gamma,s\rangle
is unimodular. So we have a splitting

H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})=\langle\gamma,s\rangle\oplus\langle\gamma,s\rangle^{\perp},

with
\PD[\Sigma]\in\langle\gamma,s\rangle^{\perp}
, and so the divisibility of
\PD[\Sigma]\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
equals the divisibility of
\PD[\Sigma]\in H^{1}(R^{1}g_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X})=\gamma^{\perp}/\langle\gamma\rangle
.
The latter is equal to the divisibility of the class
\PD[\Sigma^{\vee}]\in H^{1}(R^{1}g^{\vee}_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{t}})=(\gamma^{\vee})^{\perp}/\langle\gamma^{\vee}\rangle
, which is the Friedman–Scattone index of
Y
by the Proof of Lemma
3.2
.
∎

Proposition 3.26
.

Suppose that
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are type III K3 surfaces with split mixed Hodge structure. Let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
and
(X^{\prime},\omega^{\prime};\Sigma^{\prime})
be their respective mirror compactifying K3 surfaces with divisors. Then any sequence of elementary modifications taking
Y
to
Y^{\prime}
induces a symplectomorphism
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}X^{\prime}
, taking
\Sigma
to
\Sigma^{\prime}
, uniquely determined up to symplectic isotopy.

Proof.

Let’s first start with elementary modifications of type 1. Fix a type III K3 surface
Y
with split mixed Hodge structure, and suppose it has components
(Y_{1},D_{1})
and
(Y_{2},D_{2})
such that
Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}=D_{12}\neq\emptyset
, and
E\subset Y_{1}
a
(-1)
curve intersecting
D_{12}
transversally in a point, and otherwise contained in
Y_{1}\backslash D_{1}
. Then we can use this configuration to perform a type 1 elementary modification. Let
Y^{\prime}
be the resulting K3 surface, with modified components
(Y_{1}^{\prime},D_{1}^{\prime})
,
(Y_{2}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime})
.

The proof that any maximal log Calabi-Yau surface admits a toric model
[
GHK15a
, Proposition 1.3]
shows that we can find toric models for
(Y_{1},D_{1})
and
(Y_{1}^{\prime},D_{1}^{\prime})
which are compatible, in the sense that
(Y_{1},D_{1})
has exactly the same toric model as
(Y_{1}^{\prime},D_{1}^{\prime})
, with one additional interior blow-up, giving the exceptional curve
E
. Similarly for
(Y_{2},D_{2})
and
(Y_{2}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime})
. Now let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
, respectively
(X^{\prime},\omega^{\prime};\Sigma^{\prime})
, be the mirror compactifying K3 surface with divisor for
Y
, respectively for
Y^{\prime}
. With our choices of toric models, it’s immediate that
X
is obtained from
X^{\prime}
by a nodal slide, along the invariant ray associated to
D_{12}
, and, using our previous notation, crossing (a segment of)
\Gamma
. At the point where the node crosses
\Gamma
, its vanishing cycle is a parallel (and disjoint) copy of the restriction of
\Sigma
. In particular, we immediately get a symplectomorphism from
X
to
X^{\prime}
taking
\Sigma
to
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Now suppose that we’re given a type III K3 surface
Y
with split mixed Hodge structure, and an elementary modification from
Y
to another type III K3 surface with split mixed Hodge structure, say
Y^{\prime}
.
We use the notation of Figure
3.2
for the components involved in the modification.

The components
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
intersect in a
(-1,-1)
curve
C
. By using elementary transformations of toric models (see
[
GHK15a
]
or
[
HK22
, Definition 3.24]
), we can ensure that both
(Y_{1},D_{1})
and
(Y_{2},D_{2})
are given toric models such that
C
is the pull-back of a component of the toric divisor, with no interior blow-ups applied. Similarly for
(Y_{3}^{\prime},D_{3}^{\prime})
and
(Y_{4}^{\prime},D_{4}^{\prime})
with
C^{\prime}
.
Let
(X,\Sigma)
and
(X^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
be as before, and let
B
,
B^{\prime}
be the integral affine
S^{2}
s with singularities associated to
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
. Given our choices of toric models,
B
and
B^{\prime}
are isomorphic, up to the usual ambiguity of small nodal slides near each of the vertices. Thus
X
and
X^{\prime}
are naturally symplectomorphic.

Figure 3.4.
Mirrors to type 2 elementary mutations: triangulations and tropical graphs.

Let
a
and
b
denote the triple points
C\cap Y_{3}
and
C\cap Y_{4}
, respectively; and let
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
denote
C^{\prime}\cap Y_{1}^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\cap Y_{4}^{\prime}
respectively.
Then using the same notation as the proof of Proposition
3.22
, we have an identification

\Delta_{a}(\delta^{\prime})\cup\Delta_{b}(\delta^{\prime})=\Delta_{a^{\prime}}(\delta^{\prime})\cup\Delta_{b^{\prime}}(\delta^{\prime})

and, inside this, the curve

\Sigma_{a}\cup\Sigma_{b}
is symplectic isotopic to
\Sigma_{a^{\prime}}\cup\Sigma_{b^{\prime}}
using standard tropical results. See Figure
3.4
for an illustration. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.27
.

We expect that the induced equivalence
\mathcal{W}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{W}(M^{\prime})
corresponds to the equivalence
\Coh Y\rightarrow\Coh Y^{\prime}

of Remark
3.17
. As we don’t need this for our main results we have not checked this.
Also, different sequences of elementary modifications from
Y
to
Y^{\prime}
will a priori induce different classes of symplectomorphisms from
X
to
X^{\prime}
. In particular, in the case
Y=Y^{\prime}
, we expect there is a well-defined group homomorphism

\BirAut^{\CY}(Y)\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\operatorname{Symp}(M))

from the group of birational modifications of
Y
acting trivially on
H^{0}(\omega_{Y})\simeq\mathbb{C}
, similarly to the story in the log CY2 case studied in
[
HK
]
.
The group
\BirAut^{\CY}(Y)
coincides with the group
\Aut^{\CY}(\mathcal{Y}_{\eta})
of automorphisms of
\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
over
\mathbb{C}((q))
acting trivially on
H^{0}(\omega_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{\eta}})
, where
\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}/\mathbb{C}((q))
is the generic fibre of the smoothing
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{C}[[q]]
such that all line bundles lift.

3.3.
Proof that the compactification of
M
is Kähler

Definition 3.28
.

[
FS86
, p. 3]

A type III K3 surface
Y
is in
(-1)
-form if for each irreducible double curve
C
,
C^{2}=-1
on both components
on which it lies, unless
C
is a singular nodal curve, in which case
C^{2}=1
on the smooth component which contains it and
\widetilde{C}^{2}=-1
on the normalisation of the other component.

Lemma 3.29
.

Let
n
and
k
be positive integers such that
k^{2}\,|\,n
. Then there exists a type III degenerate K3 surface
Y
in
(-1)
form with
2n
triple points,
|H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{Z})|=k
, and split mixed Hodge structure.

Proof.

We use the construction of
[
FS86
,
\lx@sectionsign 2
]
. Specifically, Figures
3
and
4
in op. cit. show the dual cell complex of triangulations of
S^{2}
with
t=2n
triangles for
n
even and odd respectively corresponding to (trivial) deformation types of type III K3 surfaces in
(-1)
-form with
2n
triple points. As explained in
[
FS86
]
, these type III K3s have monodromy invariant
k=1
by the main theorem of
[
Fri83a
]
: this implies that a type III K3 in
(-1)
-form with monodromy invariant
k
has so called ‘special
k
-bands of hexagons’ (see
[
FS86
, p. 4]
, for the definition); in this case,
since the cell complexes we are considering contain adjacent cells which are not hexagons, it follows that
k=1
.

To obtain a type III K3
Y
with
2n
triple points and
|H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{Z})|=k
, where
n=mk^{2}
, some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we begin with a type III K3
W
with
2m
triple points and
|H^{3}(W,\mathbb{Z})|=1
, as above and perform an abstract base change and semi-stable resolution of order
k
. In terms of the dual complex, this has the effect of subdividing each triangle in the dual complex for
W
into
k^{2}
triangles, as in
[
FS86
, Figure B]
. It’s immediate that
Y
has
2m\times k^{2}
triple points; moreover, it has the correct monodromy invariant by
[
FS86
, Theorem 0.6]
.

Finally, by Lemma
2.19
, within a given (trivial) deformation class, there exists a unique representative with split mixed Hodge structure, which we are free to pick.
∎

Definition 3.30
.

Given
n
and
k
positive integers such that
k^{2}|n
,
Y
the associated type III K3 surface from Lemma
3.29
, and
d
an integer, we define:

•

Y^{\prime}
to be the type III K3 surface given by applying to
Y
an abstract base change of order
d
and semi-stable resolution; this has split mixed Hodge structure and is in
(-1)
form;

•

Y^{\prime\prime}
to be the type III K3 surface with split mixed Hodge structure given as follows: if
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is even, we take
Y^{\prime\prime}=Y^{\prime}
; and if
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is odd, we apply elementary modification of type 2) to
Y^{\prime}
at each of the double curves meeting, but not contained in, the components of
Y^{\prime}
with boundary cycle of length
2
(all such double curves will be of
(-1,-1)
type).

Proposition 3.31
.

Let
n
and
k
be positive integers such that
k^{2}\,|\,n
. Then there exists an integer
d
such that for
Y^{\prime\prime}
the type III K3 surface of Definition
3.30
,
there exists a birational morphism
Y^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow Z
,
with exceptional locus a disjoint union of chains of smooth rational curves, such that

(i)

each chain is contained in a unique component of
Y^{\prime\prime}
and has self-intersection numbers
-1,-2,\ldots,-2
;

(ii)

each component
(Z_{i},\sum_{j\neq i}Z_{j}\cap Z_{i})
is a toric surface together with its toric boundary;

(iii)

Z
is projective.

Moreover, if
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is even we may take any
d\geq 1
, and if
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is odd we may take any
d\geq\nicefrac{{4}}{{k}}
.

This can be interpreted as giving compatible toric models for the components of
Y^{\prime\prime}
, with the feature that none of them involve corner blow-ups.

Proof.

Let
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
be as in Lemma
3.29
and Definition
3.30
.
If
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is even, then the non-toric components of
Y
have boundary a cycle of
(-1)
-curves of length
3
, so are obtained from
\mathbb{P}^{2}
together with its toric boundary by blowing up six points, two in the interior of each boundary curve, and taking the strict transform of the boundary.
Thus, in this case, we can define
Z
by replacing each such component with its blowdown
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. To see that
Z
is projective (for an appropriate choice of gluing), it suffices to note that the line bundle
-K_{S}
is ample on the toric surface
S
with boundary a cycle of
6

(-1)
-curves, and has degree
1
on each boundary component.
This means that there is an ample line bundle on
Z
whose restriction to each irreducible component of type
S
is
-K_{S}
and to each irreducible component
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

If
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is odd, then the non-toric components
S
of
Y
have boundary a cycle of
(-1)
-curves of length
4
or
2
.
In the former case,
S
is obtained from
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
together with its toric boundary by blowing up
4
points, one in the interior of each boundary curve, and we can replace
S
by its blowdown
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
Z
.
In the latter case,
S
may be described as follows.
Let
\tilde{S}\rightarrow S
be the blowup of both nodes of the boundary of
S
together with the full inverse image of the boundary. Then
\tilde{S}
has boundary a
4
-cycle of smooth rational curves with self-intersection numbers
-3,-1,-3,-1
, and is obtained from
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
together with its toric boundary by blowing up
8=3+1+3+1
points in the interior of these boundary curves.
In order to realise this birational modification on the surface
Y
, we first apply an abstract base change and semi-stable resolution
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
of order
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that the vertices of the dual complex corresponding to non-toric components are at distance
kd\geq 4
(that is, we require
d\geq\lceil 4/k\rceil
).
Now for each component
S
of
Y^{\prime}
with boundary cycle of length
2
we perform the elementary modifications of the two double curves of
Y^{\prime}
meeting
S
but not contained in it, as shown in Figure
3.5
.

Figure 3.5.
Case with a cycle of length
2

Now we blowdown the strict transform
\tilde{S}
of
S
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
as above.
To check that the resulting degenerate surface
Z
is projective (for suitable gluing), observe that the irreducible components of
Z
are the toric surfaces (together with their toric boundary) of the following types:

(1)

The toric surface with boundary a cycle of
6

(-1)
-curves.

(2)

The toric surface with boundary a cycle of
7
curves with self-intersections
-1
,
-1
,
-1
,
-1
,
-2
,
-1
,
-2
(obtained by blowing up a surface of type (1) at a node of the boundary).

(3)

\mathbb{F}_{1}=\mathrm{Bl}_{p}\mathbb{P}^{2}

(4)

\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}

Moreover, the surfaces of the latter
3
types are arranged as in the figure, with adjacent surfaces of type (1).
Now we specify an ample line bundle
A
on
Z
by its restriction
A|_{S}
to each of the types of component
S
as follows: on type (1), we take
A|_{S}=-2K_{S}
, with degree
2
on each boundary curve; on type (2) we take
A|_{S}=\pi^{*}(-2K_{\bar{S}})-E
where
S\rightarrow\bar{S}
is the blowdown to a surface of type
(1)
and
E
is the exceptional curve, with degrees
2,2,2,2,1,1,1
on the boundary curves in the order listed above; on type (3) we take
A|_{S}=\pi^{*}(-K_{\bar{S}})-E
, where
S\rightarrow\bar{S}
is the blowdown to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
E
the exceptional curve, with degrees
2,1,3,1
on the boundary curves with self-intersections
-1,0,1,0
; on type (4) we take
A_{S}=\mathcal{O}(1)\boxtimes\mathcal{O}(3)
with degrees
1,3,1,3
on the boundary curves, chosen so that the degrees on adjacent components of types (2) and (3) match.
∎

For the rest of this section, we fix positive integers
n
and
k
such that
k^{2}|n
, and type III K3 surfaces
Y
,
Y^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
and a morphism
Y^{\prime\prime}\to Z
as above. (These are all in
(-1)
form with split mixed Hodge structure.) Let
\Delta
denote the dual complex of
Y
,
\Delta^{\prime}
the dual complex of
Y^{\prime}
, and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
the dual complex of
Y^{\prime\prime}
. The following is immediate from our constructions.

Lemma 3.32
.

The dual complex
\Delta^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Delta
by dividing each triangle into
d^{2}
triangles in the standard, as in Figure
3.6
or
[
FS86
, p. 4, Figure B]
.
The dual complex
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
is obtained from
\Delta^{\prime}
by the following re-triangulation: for each triangle
\sigma\in\Delta^{\prime}
with a vertex of valency
2
, we flip the edge of
\sigma
not containing that vertex.

Figure 3.6.
Local picture for the complexes
\Delta
,
\Delta^{\prime}
(for
d=4
) and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
. Here we assume the original bottom-left vertex in
\Delta
had valency 2.

Definition 3.33
.

Let
B=B_{Z}
be the integral affine
S^{2}
with singularities associated, by the construction of Section
3.2.1
, to
Y^{\prime\prime}
with the choices of toric models given by
Y^{\prime\prime}\to Z
.

Definition 3.34
.

Let
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
be the scheme obtained by glueing copies of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
along their toric boundaries, with combinatorics dictated by
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
. We use the standard gluing (identifying boundary components with
\mathbb{P}^{1}
in the obvious way) so that there is a line bundle
\bar{L}
on
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
restricting to
\mathcal{O}(1)
on each component.

Remark 3.35
.

As
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
is a simplicial complex in our case,

\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
can be realised in the projective space with homogeneous coordinates indexed by the vertices of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, such that the
0
-strata of
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
correspond to the standard basis of the associated vector space, each stratum is a projective linear subspace, and
\bar{L}
is the restriction of
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Definition 3.36
.

For each vertex
v\in\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, let
A_{v}
be an ample line bundle on the corresponding irreducible component
Z_{v}
of
Z
, such that if
e=vw
is an edge of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
then, writing
Z_{e}=Z_{v}\cap Z_{w}
for the associated
1
-stratum of
Z
,
we have
A_{v}\cdot Z_{e}=A_{w}\cdot Z_{e}
.

Remark 3.37
.

These line bundles exists by our assumption that
Z
is projective.
For the purposes of Proposition
3.38
below, and more generally for showing that
(X,\omega)
is Kähler, any choices of
A_{v}
are suitable.
However, to later compare the symplectic submanifold
\Sigma\subset X
with a holomorphic curve, it will be helpful to specifically work with the ample line bundles
A_{v}
as described in the Proof of Proposition
3.31
.

Proposition 3.38
.

Fix
Y^{\prime\prime}\to Z
from Proposition
3.31
and our choices of ample line bundles
A_{v}
from Definition
3.36
. Then one can construct, via
the Gross-Siebert programme, the following structures:
a new polyhedral subdivision
\hat{\Delta}
of
B_{Z}
, with focus-focus singularities in the interior of edges of
\hat{\Delta}
;
a normal crossing maximal Calabi–Yau surface
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
with split MHS;
and a ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\hat{L}
on
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, such that these have the following properties:

(i)

The polyhedral subdivision
\hat{\Delta}
of
B_{Z}
is such that the (focus-focus) singularities of
B_{Z}
lie in the interior of edges of
\hat{\Delta}
.

(ii)

\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is a normal crossing union of toric surfaces glued along their toric boundaries,
such that the irreducible components of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, together with the restriction of
\hat{L}
, are the polarised toric surfaces with moment polytopes the maximal cells in the polyhedral subdivision
\hat{\Delta}
.

(iii)

There is a a proper morphism with connected fibres
g:\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, determined by replacing
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
by
\hat{\Delta}
.

The surface
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is uniquely determined by
Z
and the
A_{v}
up to elementary modifications of type 2. (Its precise representative depends additionally on choice of an auxiliary function
\varphi
which will be described in the proof.)

Proof.

These are central constructions in the Gross-Siebert programme, as first set up in
[
GS06
]
. To summarise for experts, using the terminology of that programme: the ample line bundles
A_{v}
on the components
Z_{v}
of
Z
determine a smooth log structure near the vertices
v\in\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
. This determines, up to elementary modifications, our normal crossing surface
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
together with the proper morphism with connected fibres
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, with the following property:
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
admits a log smooth structure over the standard log point
(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C},\mathbb{N}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{*})
away from a finite set of points contained in the interior of the
1
-strata of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
corresponding to the focus-focus singularities of
B_{Z}
contained in the associated edges of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
.

We now spell things out in more detail.
First, let
P_{v}
denote the moment polytope of the toric surface
Z_{v}
with respect to the ample line bundle
A_{v}
, a lattice polygon.
Note that by our assumption for each edge
e=vw
of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, the length of the corresponding (dual) edges of
P_{v}
and
P_{w}
coincide. Identifying these edges, we obtain a polyhedral decomposition of
S^{2}
, say
\check{B}=|\mathcal{P}|
.
Moreover,
\check{B}
inherits an integral affine structure away from finitely many points on the edges dual to the edges of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
containing focus-focus singularities of
B_{Z}
, as follows. In the interior of each cell
P_{v}
, we use the integral affine structure on the lattice polygon
P_{v}
.
At a vertex of
\mathcal{P}
corresponding to a triangle
\sigma\in\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, we locally identify the subdivision
\mathcal{P}
with the toric fan of the associated component
(\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0})_{\sigma}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
of
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
. The resulting ambiguity in the integral affine structure along the edges of
\mathcal{P}
dual to edges of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
containing focus-focus singularities is resolved by introducing an equal number of dual focus-focus singularities, with invariant direction along the edge of
\mathcal{P}
.

Let
\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
denote the set of integral points of the integral affine manifold
\check{B}
.
We will need the following additional data: we require a function
\varphi\colon\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
with the following properties:

(1)

For each vertex
v\in\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, the restriction

\varphi^{0}_{v}\colon\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}\cap P_{v}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

determines an integral convex piecewise linear function

\varphi_{v}\colon P_{v}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

(defined by taking the graph of
\varphi_{v}
to be the lower boundary of the convex hull of the graph of
\varphi^{0}_{v}
)
such that
\varphi_{v}(a)=\varphi^{0}_{v}(a)
for all
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and the maximal domains of linearity of
\varphi_{v}
are cells of a unimodular triangulation of
P_{v}
(that is, the maximal domains of linearity are lattice triangles with no integral points besides the vertices).

(2)

For each edge
e=vw\in\Delta^{\prime\prime}
there are (necessarily adjacent) integral points
a,b
in the dual edge
e^{\vee}=P_{v}\cap P_{w}
of
\mathcal{P}
such that
\varphi(a)=\varphi(b)
.

To see that such a function
\varphi
exists, notice that the values of
\varphi^{0}
on the integral points of the
1
-skeleton of
\mathcal{P}
can be assigned arbitrarily subject to the following conditions: on each edge
e
the function
\varphi_{e}
defined by the lower convex hull of the graph of
\varphi^{0}
satisfies
\varphi_{e}(a)=\varphi^{0}(a)
for
a
integral, and
\varphi^{0}(a)=\varphi^{0}(b)
for some integral
a,b\in e
.

The ample line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1)
on
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
determines a multi-valued convex
\mathcal{P}
-piecewise integral affine linear function, say
\psi
, on the integral affine manifold
\check{B}
: this is given in a neighbourhood of a vertex of
\mathcal{P}
by the integral piecewise linear function on the fan of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
corresponding to
\mathcal{O}(1)
, which is determined up to an integral affine linear function.
We replace the polyhedral subdivision
\mathcal{P}
of
\check{B}
by the triangulation
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
obtained by replacing each cell
P_{v}
by the triangulation induced by the convex function
\varphi_{v}
, and the function
\psi
by the multi-valued
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
-piecewise linear function
\hat{\psi}:=\psi+\epsilon\varphi
, for
0<\epsilon\ll 1
,
\epsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
, where
\varphi|_{P_{v}}:=\varphi_{v}
.
We further assume that the position of the focus-focus singularities on the edges of
\mathcal{P}
have been chosen such that they lie in the interior of the edge of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
contained in the given edge of
\mathcal{P}
along which
\varphi
is constant.

Now we consider the discrete Legendre transform of the integral affine manifold with singularities
\check{B}
together with polyhedral subdivision
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
and multi-valued piecewise linear function
\hat{\psi}
, as defined in
[
GS06
, Section 1.4]
.
The key point is that this recovers the integral affine manifold with singularities
B_{Z}
but with a different polyhedral subdivision
\hat{\Delta}
(and multi-valued piecewise linear function) to that considered initially. Note that, initially, the polyhedral subdivision of
B_{Z}
was given by the triangulation
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, and the multi-valued
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
-piecewise linear function
\check{\psi}
was given in a neighbourhood of a vertex
v\in\Delta^{\prime\prime}
by the convex piecewise linear function on the fan of
Z_{v}
associated to the ample line bundle
A_{v}
(although it was not explicitly considered earlier).

This new polyhedral subdivision
\hat{\Delta}
corresponds to the morphism
g\colon\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
.
(Here we use the standard toric identifications of the
1
-strata (or equivalently such that
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
has split mixed Hodge structure by Lemma
2.19
.)
The faces of
\hat{\Delta}
are indexed by points of
\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
\hat{X}_{a}
denote the irreducible component of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
associated to the integral point
a\in\check{B}
, and define

\hat{X}_{a}|_{\hat{X}_{b}}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}_{b}}(\hat{X}_{a}\cap\hat{X}_{b})&\text{if $a\neq b$}\\
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}_{b}}(-\sum_{c\neq b}\hat{X}_{c}\cap\hat{X}_{b})&\text{if $a=b$}\end{cases}

Then the line bundle
\hat{L}
is given by

(3.39)

\hat{L}=g^{*}\bar{L}+\epsilon\sum_{a\in A}\varphi(a)\hat{X}_{a}|_{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}

where
\epsilon
is as above.
To check that
\hat{L}
is a well-defined line bundle, it’s enough to notice that that
\sum\lambda_{a}\hat{X}_{a}|_{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}
defines a line bundle on
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
(or equivalently, that its degree on each component of the double curve of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is well-defined) if and only if
\lambda_{a}=\lambda_{b}
whenever
ab
is an edge of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
containing focus-focus singularities of
\check{B}
.
∎

Remark 3.40
.

For the purposes of showing that the symplectic form on
X
is Kähler, any choice of
\varphi
is suitable; in general, different choices for
\varphi
give representatives for
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
which are related by type 2 elementary modifications.
However, to later compare the symplectic submanifold
\Sigma\subset X
with a holomorphic curve, it will be helpful to work with
a specific choice. Assume that we are using our favourite ample line bundles
A_{v}
, as in Remark
3.37
.
This gives us polarised toric surfaces of the form (
Z_{v},A_{v})=(\mathrm{Bl}^{3}\mathbb{P}^{2},-2K)
, with moment polytope the hexagon with side length 2. (The
Z_{v}
are the components of type (1) in the proof of Proposition
3.31
.) We will want to choose
\varphi
so that for each such
v
, the induced unimodular triangulation of the side length 2 hexagon is the standard one. A local picture for the resulting
\hat{\Delta}
is given in Figure
3.7
.

Figure 3.7.
Local picture for
\hat{\Delta}
with the choices of Remark
3.40
.

Definition 3.41
.

Suppose we’re given
Y^{\prime\prime}\to Z
as in Proposition
3.31
, and that we’ve fixed auxiliary data
(the ample line bundles
A_{v}
on components of
Z
, and a function
\varphi:\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathbb{Z}
), to construct

\hat{\Delta}
,
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
and
\hat{L}
as in Proposition
3.38
.
We define a type III K3 surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
as follows:
for each edge of
\hat{\Delta}
containing a focus-focus singularity of
B_{Z}
, we pick an irreducible component of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
containing the corresponding
1
-stratum, and blowup points in the interior of this
1
-stratum, one for each focus-focus singularity. (For each focus-focus singularity we get to choose the component, and also exactly which point to blow up in the interior of the stratum.)

Let
h:\mathcal{X}_{0}\to\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
be given by the blow-down maps (this is sometimes called a birational modification).
For the face of
\hat{\Delta}
indexed by
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
X_{a}
denote the corresponding irreducible component of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, let
D_{a}\subset X_{a}
denote the strict transform of the toric anti-canonical divisor
\hat{D}_{a}
in
\hat{X}_{a}
, and let
h_{a}:X_{a}\to\hat{X}_{a}
denote the restriction of
h
.

Proposition 3.42
.

Using the same notation as above, we can choose the blow-ups
h\colon\mathcal{X}_{0}\to\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
so that

(i)

\mathcal{X}_{0}
is projective.

(ii)

There’s a ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
given by

L=(h\circ g)^{*}\bar{L}+\epsilon\sum\lambda_{a}X_{a}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}

where as before, we define

X_{a}|_{X_{b}}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}_{{X}_{b}}({X}_{a}\cap{X}_{b})&\text{if $a\neq b$}\\
\mathcal{O}_{{X}_{b}}(-\sum_{c\neq b}{X}_{c}\cap{X}_{b})&\text{if $a=b$}\end{cases}

and where
\epsilon
is the same as in the definition of
\hat{L}
, and the
\lambda_{a}
are suitable rational perturbation of the
\varphi(a)
.

(iii)

\mathcal{X}_{0}
is d-semistable in the sense of Friedman
[
Fri83b
]
.

Proof.

We first want to show that we can arrange for
\mathcal{X}_{0}
to be projective with such an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
L
.
In order to do so, start by (rationally) perturbing
\varphi(a)
to
\lambda_{a}
, for each
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
so that, for each edge
ab
of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
containing focus-focus singularities, we have

\lambda_{a}\neq\lambda_{b}
.
Now choose blow-ups
h\colon\mathcal{X}_{0}\to\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
so that for each focus-focus singularity on the edge
ab
, the corresponding blow-ups are done on the component
\hat{X}_{a}
if
\lambda_{a}<\lambda_{b}
, and on
\hat{X}_{b}
otherwise. (Note that this means that if there are multiple focus-focus singularities on the same edge, the corresponding blow-ups are all on the same component.)

Assume now that we’ve made such choices. We still have the freedom to choose the exact location of the points we blow up (so far we’ve only fixed the components on which they get blown up). Using
[
FS86
, Proposition 4.14]
, we see that we can choose them in such a way that
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is d-semistable; moreover, if there are multiple blow-ups on the same components, we see that we have the freedom to make these at distinct points.
∎

For each
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
\delta_{a}
be the constant such that
\lambda_{a}=\varphi(a)+\delta_{a}
, and for each pair
a\neq b\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\hat{X}_{a}\cap\hat{X}_{b}\neq\emptyset
, set
\hat{D}_{ab}:=\hat{X}_{a}\cap\hat{X}_{b}
. The following is immediate.

Corollary 3.43
.

For each component

(X_{a},D_{a})

of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, the restriction
h_{a}\colon X_{a}\rightarrow\hat{X}_{a}
is given by non-toric blow-ups of distinct points of the toric pair

(\hat{X}_{a},\hat{D}_{a})
.
Moreover, we have

L|_{X_{a}}=h_{a}^{*}\hat{L}^{\prime}_{a}-\epsilon\sum_{ab\in\hat{\mathcal{P}},\,\delta_{b}-\delta_{a}>0}(\delta_{b}-\delta_{a})\sum_{i}E_{ab,i}

where the
E_{ab,i}
are the exceptional curves of
h_{a}
resulting from blow-ups on
\hat{D}_{ab}
, and for each component
\hat{X}_{a}
of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, we set
\hat{L}^{\prime}_{a}
to be the following ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
\hat{X}_{a}
:

(3.44)

\hat{L}^{\prime}_{a}:=\hat{L}|_{\hat{X}_{a}}+\epsilon\sum_{ab\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}}(\delta_{a}-\delta_{b})\hat{D}_{ab}

Proposition 3.45
.

We can construct the following structures:

(a)

a polyhedral subdivision of the integral affine manifold
B_{Z}
with focus-focus singularities with maximal cells integral affine polygons
Q_{a}
indexed by
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(b)

for each
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, a symplectic form
\omega_{a}
on
X_{a}
;

such that the following properties hold:

(i)

The decomposition
B_{Z}=\cup_{a}Q_{a}
is obtained by a small perturbation of the decomposition
\hat{\Delta}
, and is such that the focus-focus singularities of
B_{Z}
all lie in the interior of the
Q_{a}
; let
S_{a}
denote the singularities inside
Q_{a}
.

(ii)

For each
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
,
there is an almost-toric fibration
\pi_{a}:(X_{a},\omega_{a})\to(Q_{a},S_{a},\partial Q_{a})
, where we use the conventions in
[
Sym03
]
for the components of an almost-toric base.

(iii)

The symplectic form
\omega_{a}
is Kähler, and satisfies
[\omega_{a}]=c_{1}(L|_{X_{a}})\in H^{2}(X_{a};\mathbb{Q})
.

(iv)

Fix an edge of the polyhedral decomposition
B_{Z}=\cup_{a}Q_{a}
, say dual to
ab\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}
, and consider the inclusions
i_{a}:D_{ab}\hookrightarrow X_{a}
,
i_{b}:D_{ab}\hookrightarrow X_{b}
of the corresponding divisor. Then we have
i^{\ast}\omega_{a}=i^{\ast}\omega_{b}
.

In particular, the
\pi_{a}
patch together to give a generalised almost-toric fibration from
\mathcal{X}_{0}
to
B_{Z}
, which we call
\pi_{L}
.

By ‘generalised’ almost-toric fibration, we mean the following: we extend the definition from
[
Sym03
,
Eva23
]
to allow local models of singularities which appear when restricting toric fibrations of smooth toric manifolds to their toric boundaries.

Proof.

For each
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
\hat{\Delta}_{a}
be the corresponding face of the polyhedral decomposition
\hat{\Delta}
. This is the moment polytope for the polarised toric surface
(\hat{X}_{a},\hat{L}_{a})
. Its edges are labelled by the (dual) edges
ab\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}
, for varying
b
.
For each
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we can also consider the polarised toric surface
(\hat{X}_{a},\hat{L}^{\prime}_{a})
. Say this has moment polytope
\hat{Q}_{a}
, with almost-toric fibration
\hat{\pi}_{a}:\hat{X}_{a}\to\hat{Q}_{a}
. From Equation
3.44
, we get that
\hat{Q}_{a}
can be obtained by starting with the integral affine polygon
\hat{\Delta}_{a}
(with rational vertices), say viewed inside
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and replacing the edge labelled by
ab\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}
with a parallel copy of it displaced by integral affine distance
\epsilon(\delta_{b}-\delta_{a})
. (Here the sign is chosen so that the distance is measured outwards from the inside of the polygon.)
Let
\hat{\omega}_{a}
be the toric Kähler form on
\hat{X}_{a}
corresponding to
\hat{Q}_{a}
. By definition, we have
c_{1}(\hat{L}^{\prime}_{a})=[\hat{\omega}_{a}]
.

Now fix
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and consider
\hat{\pi}_{a}:\hat{X}_{a}\to\hat{Q}_{a}
.
We want to modify this using the standard local model for non-toric symplectic blow-ups, following
[
Sym03
, Section 5.4]
(see also
[
Eva23
, Section 6.1]
).
For each blow-up which arises in
h_{a}:X_{a}\longrightarrow\hat{X}_{a}
, we modify the fibration
\hat{\pi}_{a}:\hat{X}_{a}\to\hat{Q}_{a}
by ‘cutting out a triangle’ from the corresponding edge of
\hat{Q}_{a}
, and inserting a nodal singularity at the apex of the triangle, with invariant direction parallel to the edge. See
[
Eva23
, Figure 9.2]
. For each blow-up, we get to choose the exact position of the node, all of which are related by nodal slides; we choose ours to match the choices in Proposition
3.42
. Also, we get to choose the affine edge-length of the triangle we cut out (or, equivalently, the symplectic area of the newly-created
(-1)
curve), so long as it is sufficiently small. Say we’re working on an edge indexed by
ab\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}
; then we choose our cut so that the
(-1)
curve has symplectic area
\epsilon(\delta_{b}-\delta_{a})
. Inspecting the construction of
L
in Proposition
3.42
, we see that for a fixed
\epsilon
(and so polyhedral decomposition
\hat{\Delta}
of
B_{Z}
), we are free to choose our perturbations
\lambda_{a}
of
\varphi(a)
so that
\delta_{a}
is arbitrarily small. In particular, we may assume that
\epsilon(\delta_{b}-\delta_{a})
is small enough so as to have room to cut out (disjoint) almost-toric triangles for all of the blow-ups in
h_{a}
which appear on the corresponding component.

This gives a symplectic form
\omega_{a}
on
X_{a}
together with an almost-toric fibration
\pi_{a}:X_{a}\to Q_{a}
.
The fact that
\omega_{a}
is Kähler follows from worked examples of Auroux for the non-toric blow-up, see
[
Aur08
, Example 3.9]
and
[
AAK16
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.2]
.
For claim (i), recall that
\hat{Q}_{a}
has edges displaced from those of
\hat{\Delta}_{a}
by integral affine distance
\epsilon(\delta_{b}-\delta_{a})
. As this matches the affine side-length of the triangles we cut out for the blow-up, we see that the resulting singular points precisely land back on the original edge for
\hat{\Delta}_{a}
(and with the same invariant direction as in
B_{Z}
). Claim (i) is then clear. Claim (ii) follows from the standard blow-up formula, and (iv) from the fact that
\omega_{a}
and
\omega_{b}
both pull back to toric symplectic forms with the same moment maps.
∎

Corollary 3.46
.

Suppose
Y
is a type III K3 with split mixed Hodge structure, and let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
be its mirror compactifying K3 surface with divisor.
Then there exists a complex structure on
X
such that the symplectic form
\omega
is Kähler, and
\Sigma
is a holomorphic submanifold.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.26
, we are free to modify
Y
by any sequence of elementary modifications. In particular, we may assume it is in
(-1)
form.
Let
Y^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
be the auxiliary type III K3 surfaces introduced in Definition
3.30
, choosing
d
as follows: for
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
, we take
d=1
; and for
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
odd, we take
d=4
.
These are in the range for which Proposition
3.31
holds, and we have our auxiliary birational morphism
Y^{\prime\prime}\to Z
. Assume also that for the constructions of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
and
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, we have chosen auxiliary data as in Remarks
3.37
and
3.40
.

Let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
,
(X^{\prime},\omega^{\prime};\Sigma^{\prime})
and
(X^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime};\Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime})
be the mirror compactifying K3 surfaces with divisors for, respectively,
Y
,
Y^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
. Say these are equipped with almost-toric fibrations
\pi_{X}:X\to B
,
\pi_{X^{\prime}}:X^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
and
\pi_{X^{\prime\prime}}:X^{\prime\prime}\to B^{\prime\prime}
.

Step 1:

(X,\omega)
is Kähler.
From Lemma
3.32
, we see, first, that the integral affine manifold
B^{\prime}
is given by scaling
B
by a factor of
d
, and that there’s a diffeomorphism
X\to X^{\prime}
, intertwining the fibrations, which is a conformal symplectomorphism with constant conformal factor
d
. As
Y^{\prime\prime}
is obtained from
Y^{\prime}
by type II modifications, by Proposition
3.26
, it’s enough to show that
(X^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})
is Kähler.

We will now input Propositions
3.42
and
3.45
. First, let
f:X^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
be the standard ‘toric collapse map’ such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{X^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X^{\prime\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{L}}
\textstyle{B_{Z}}

(Here we implicitly assume that the Vũ Ngọc invariants of nodal fibres above the same singular point of
B_{Z}
have been chosen to agree, which we are free to do.)
Let
Q^{[1]}\subset B_{Z}
be the union of the one-strata in the polyhedral decomposition
B_{Z}=\cup_{a}Q_{a}
. The map
f
gives a symplectomorphism when restricted to the complement of the preimages of
Q^{[1]}
:

X^{\prime\prime}\backslash(\pi_{X^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}(Q^{[1]}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{X}_{0}\backslash(\pi_{L}^{-1}(Q^{[1]})).

On the other hand, we know that
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is semistable. Take any semistable smoothing
(\mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathcal{X})/(0\in\mathbb{D})
, with
L
lifting to a relatively ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle on
\mathcal{X}
, say
\mathcal{L}
.
(Note that for now we may work with any such smoothing; in Step 2 we will add an extra condition.)
This determines an embedding
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N}\times\mathbb{D}
, and we get a Kähler form
\upvarpi
on the total space by setting

\upvarpi:=(\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}+K\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{std}})|_{\mathcal{X}}

where
\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}
denotes the Fubini-Study form on
\mathbb{P}^{N}
,
\omega_{\mathrm{std}}
the standard symplectic form on
\mathbb{D}
, and
K\gg 0
.
Let
\mathcal{X}_{t}
be the smooth fibre above
t\in\mathbb{D}^{\ast}
; then
\upvarpi
restricts to a Kähler form, say
\upvarpi_{t}
, in the class of
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{t})
; similarly, on each irreducible component
X_{a}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, it restricts to a Kähler form, say
\upvarpi_{a}
, in the class of
c_{1}(L|_{X_{a}})
.
Now symplectic parallel transport using
\upvarpi
(and a choice of path in
\mathbb{D}
from
t
to the origin) determines a continuous map
g:\mathcal{X}_{t}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{0}
, which is a symplectomorphism (for the restrictions of
\upvarpi
) away from
\operatorname{Crit}\mathcal{X}_{0}
and its preimage.

For each
a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, as
\omega_{a}
and
\upvarpi_{a}
are cohomologous Kähler forms, we can perform Moser’s trick using their linear interpolation. This gives an isotopy
\rho_{a}
of
X_{a}
such that
\rho_{a}^{\ast}\upvarpi_{a}=\omega_{a}
.
Moreover, observe that in setting up the Moser vector field, we have the freedom to arrange for
\rho_{a}
to fix each of the irreducible components of
D_{a}
setwise, and, on
D_{ab}=D_{a}\cap D_{b}\neq\emptyset
, for
\rho_{a}
and
\rho_{b}
to agree. Let
\rho
be the induced self-map of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
We now have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho\circ f}
\scriptstyle{l^{\circ}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{0}}

where the map
l^{\circ}\coloneqq g^{-1}\circ\rho\circ f
is defined (and a symplectomorphism) from
X^{\prime\prime}\backslash(\pi_{X^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}(Q^{[1]}))
to
\mathcal{X}_{t}\backslash(\pi_{L}^{-1}(Q^{[1]}))
.

We want to extend
l^{\circ}
to a diffeomorphism
X^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{X}_{t}
.
Let’s first work locally.
Holomorphically, there are two local models for neighbourhoods in
\mathcal{X}
of points in
\operatorname{Crit}\mathcal{X}_{0}
: the degeneration
\{xyz=t\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}_{x,y,z}\times\mathbb{D}_{t}
(for triple points), and the degeneration
\{xyz=t\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}_{x,y,z}\times\mathbb{D}_{t}
(otherwise).
Fix
c\in\operatorname{Crit}\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and the relevant local model.
For each local model, the ‘straight-line’ symplectic parallel transport map, say
g_{\mathrm{loc}}
, from the smooth fibre, say
\mathcal{X}_{t,\mathrm{loc}}
, to the singular fibre, say
\mathcal{X}_{0,\mathrm{loc}}
, is well understood, and known to agree with the corresponding local model for the collapse map
X^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Concretely, the preimages
(\rho\circ f)^{-1}(c)
and
g_{\mathrm{loc}}^{-1}(c)
are diffeomorphic, and there is a diffeomorphism
l_{\mathrm{loc}}
from a neighbourhood of
(\rho\circ f)^{-1}(c)
to a neighbourhood of
g_{\mathrm{loc}}^{-1}(c)
such that locally,
\rho\circ f=l_{\mathrm{loc}}\circ g_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

As we used a
holomorphic
local model for our critical points, we will a priori have changed the symplectic parallel transport map. However, note that we will still get the same local models for (neighbourhoods of) generalised vanishing cycles above points, and collapse maps.
(This is similar to the argument for a standard vanishing cycle, and well-known to experts.
One way to set this up formally would be to adapt the arguments which show that the Milnor fibre of an isolated hypersurface singularity, as a Liouville domain, only depends on the germ of the singularity as a holomorphic map, as in
[
Kea15
, Lemma 2.7]
.)
The upshot is that we get a local diffeomorphism
l_{\mathrm{loc},c}
from a neighbourhood of
(\rho\circ f)^{-1}(c)
to a neighbourhood of
g^{-1}(c)
such that locally,
\rho\circ f=l_{\mathrm{loc}}\circ g
.
It’s immediate that
l_{\mathrm{loc},c}
agrees with
l^{\circ}
where they are both defined.
Moreover, as every point in
\pi_{X^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}(Q^{[1]})
is a limit of points in the complement
X^{\prime\prime}\backslash\pi_{X^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}(Q^{[1]})
, by uniqueness of limits, we see that for
c\neq c^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Crit}\mathcal{X}_{0}
,
l_{\mathrm{loc},c}
and
l_{\mathrm{loc},c^{\prime}}
must agree on the overlaps of their domains of definition.
Patching them together, we get a diffeomorphism
l:X^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
which agrees with
l^{\circ}
on
X^{\prime\prime}\backslash\pi_{X^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}(Q^{[1]})
. Now using the fact that the vanishing locus of the 2-form
l^{\ast}\upvarpi_{t}-\omega^{\prime\prime}
is closed in
X^{\prime\prime}
, we see that
l
is a symplectomorphism from
(X^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})
to
(\mathcal{X}_{t},\upvarpi_{t})
. In particular,
(X^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})
, and hence
(X,\omega)
, is Kähler.

Step 2:
holomorphic representative for
\Sigma
.
Recall the construction of
\Sigma
in the proof of Proposition
3.22
.
We have a tropical graph
\Gamma
obtained from drawing a tropical line in each simplex of
\Delta
with trivalent vertex at the barycenter and legs intersecting the edges of the triangle at the midpoints.
The curve
\Sigma
was given by taking tropical lines over each face of
\Delta
and gluing them together over edges of
\Delta
to be a symplectic submanifold in
X
.
(In particular, we get
\Gamma\subset B_{Z}
, and
\pi_{X}
maps
\Sigma
to an amoeba which retracts onto
\Gamma
.)

Let’s first track
\Gamma
as we modify our triangulations.
If
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is even, the triangulations
\Delta
,
\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
all agree.
If
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is odd, then
\Delta^{\prime}
is obtained by starting with
\Delta
and subdividing each triangle into
d^{2}=16
triangles, and
\Gamma
now passes through some vertices of valency 6.
See Figure
3.8
.
The triangulation
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
is obtained from
\Delta^{\prime}
by flipping some edges; as we’ve taken
d=4
, observe that all of these edge flips happen away from the simplices intersecting
\Gamma
. (Using the notation of the proof of Proposition
3.38
, formally,
\Gamma
is a subset of the one-skeleton of
\mathcal{P}
.)

Figure 3.8.
Local picture for
\Delta
,
\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
together with
\Gamma
.

Recall that
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
denotes the surface given by the union of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
’s with intersection complex
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
(and such that there exists a line bundle
\bar{L}
restricting to
\mathcal{O}(1)
on each component).
The graph
\Gamma
determines a Weil divisor
\bar{C}
in
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, which is a union of lines in components of
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, passing through some vertices of
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
of valency 6. Under the toric moment map for each component of
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, the corresponding component of
\bar{C}
maps to an amoeba which retracts onto the restriction of
\Gamma
.

The divisor
\bar{C}
can be obtained explicitly as follows. As
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
is a simplicial complex, we can embed
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
in the projective space
\mathbb{P}^{V}
with homogeneous coordinates indexed by the set of vertices
V
of the
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, using the line bundle
\bar{L}
and the distinguished basis of
H^{0}(\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0},\bar{L})
indexed by
V
.
For each component
S=\mathbb{P}^{2}
of
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
with homogeneous toric coordinates
X,Y,Z
corresponding to a
2
-simplex
\sigma\in\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, consider the restriction of the graph
\Gamma
to
\sigma
, which is either a
Y
-vertex, a line segment passing through a vertex of
\sigma
(without loss of generality corresponding to
(1:0:0)\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
), or empty. Then the restriction of
\bar{C}
to
S
is the line
X+Y+Z=0
, the line
Y+Z=0
, or empty, respectively.

The next step is to pass from
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
to the subdivision
\hat{\Delta}
. Recall this determines a normal crossing surface
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
together with
h:\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, which is a proper morphism with connected fibres. (There are exceptional surfaces over the vertices of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, and curves over points in the interior of the one-stratum of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
.)
The choices of Remarks
3.37
and
3.40
now become relevant: these imply that

\bar{C}
lifts to a Cartier divisor
\hat{C}
on the normal crossing surface
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
meeting each stratum transversely,
with properties as follows. Recall that the faces of
\hat{\Delta}
are index by the points in
\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
; formally,
\Gamma
is a subset of the one-skeleton of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
(whose vertices are the points in
\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
).
Let
\hat{C}_{a}
be the restriction of
\hat{C}
to
\hat{X}_{a}
.
We have the following:

(1)

If
a
is a vertex of
\Gamma
, then it corresponds to a dual triangle in
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, say
\sigma\in\Delta^{\prime\prime}
be the corresponding dual triangle of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
; then
\hat{C}_{a}
is the pullback of
a line under the birational morphism
\hat{X}_{a}\rightarrow(\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0})_{a}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

(2)

If
a
is an interior point of an edge of
\Gamma
, then
\hat{C}_{a}
is a smooth fibre of a (birational) ruling

\hat{X}_{a}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In the case where
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is odd (and so
d=4
), these are all rulings of copies of
\mathrm{Bl}^{3}\mathbb{P}^{2}
; in the case where
\nicefrac{{n}}{{k^{2}}}
is even, we just need to worry about the case where
\hat{X}_{a}
is an exceptional surface contracting to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\operatorname{Sing}\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
corresponding to an edge of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, in which case
\hat{C}_{a}
is the fibre above a point.

(3)

Finally,
\hat{C}_{a}
is empty if
a\notin\Gamma
.

See Figure
3.9
.

Figure 3.9.
Local picture for
\hat{\Delta}
(in blue) and
\Gamma
(in red). The black triangulation on the left is part of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
.

The modification
\mathcal{X}_{0}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is an isomorphism near
\hat{C}
;
let
C
denote the inverse image of
\hat{C}
in the
d
-semistable type III K3
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
We can choose the semi-stable smoothing
\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
(which we were already working with in Step 1) to satisfy the additional property that the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}(C)
lifts to
\mathcal{X}
by
[
FS86
, Lemma 5.5]
.

As the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}(C)
deforms to
\mathcal{X}_{t}
, we have that
C
deforms too: one computes that
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}(\mathcal{C}_{0}))=0
, which implies that all sections of the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}(C)
lift, by cohomology and base change
[
Har77
,
\lx@sectionsign
III.12]
.
Such a deformation, say
\mathcal{C}
, is a (semistable) smoothing of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
because
\mathcal{X}
is a semistable smoothing of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{0}
