is transverse to the strata of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{t}\subset\mathcal{X}_{t}
be the fibre over
t\in\mathbb{D}
.

Let’s now revisit Step 1, together with our construction of
\Sigma
in the proof of Proposition
3.22
.
First, consider the image of
\Sigma
under the ‘collapse map’
f:X^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
Comparing our tropical constructions, we see that this essentially agrees with
C
: more precisely, with respect to the almost-toric symplectic form, there’s a one parameter family
\Sigma_{s}
of symplectic divisors in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
with
\Sigma_{s}=\{\Sigma_{s,a}\}_{a\in\check{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}}
for smooth symplectic surfaces
\Sigma_{s,a}\subset X_{a}
such that:

(1)

we have
\Sigma_{0}=f(\Sigma)
and
\Sigma_{1}=C
;

(2)

for each
s
,
\Sigma_{s}
transversally intersects all strata of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
;

(3)

any combinatorially adjacent
\Sigma_{s,a}
and
\Sigma_{s,b}
intersect at a point;

Recall the symplectic neighbourhood theorem in this dimension: the local invariants of a closed symplectic surface in a symplectic 4-manifold are
the symplectic area of the surface together with its symplectic normal bundle. This implies that we can find compatible Hamiltonian isotopies of the
(X_{a},\omega_{a})
whose composition with
f
gives a one-parameter family of maps
f_{s}:X^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
such that
f_{s}=f
on
f^{-1}(\operatorname{Crit}\mathcal{X}_{0})=f_{s}^{-1}(\operatorname{Crit}\mathcal{X}_{0})
;
f_{s}
is a symplectomorphism away from this locus;
f_{0}=f
; and
f_{1}
maps
\Sigma
to
C
.

Second, for each
a
, the curve
C_{a}
must be a symplectic surface for both Kaehler forms, i.e.
\omega_{a}
and
\upvarpi_{a}
(and have the same symplectic area for both), so, again the symplectic neighbourhood theorem, we can additionally arrange for the Moser isotopy
\rho
to fix each
C_{a}
set-wise. Assume we’ve made such a choice.

Third, consider the path from
\mathcal{X}_{t}
to
\mathcal{X}_{0}
which gets used for parallel transport in Step 1. Consider the restrictions of
\mathcal{C}
to fibres of
\mathcal{X}
over this path.
Their images under parallel transport give a one-parameter family of symplectic divisors (with respect to the
\upvarpi_{a}
) from
C
to
g(\mathcal{C}_{t})
, with the same properties as the path
\Sigma_{s}
above.
Similarly to the first step, we can use this to get compatible Hamiltonian isotopies of the
(X_{a},\upvarpi_{a})
whose composition with
g
is a one-parameter family of maps
g_{s}:\mathcal{X}_{t}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}

which are symplectomorphisms away from
g_{s}^{-1}(Q^{[1]})
, and such that
g_{0}=g
and
g_{1}
takes
\mathcal{C}_{t}
to
C
.

Putting everything together, we get a family
l^{\circ}_{s}\coloneq g^{-1}_{s}\circ\rho\circ f_{s}
, defined on
X^{\prime\prime}\backslash\pi^{-1}_{X^{\prime\prime}}(Q^{[1]})
, and a symplectomorphism onto its image. The same arguments as in Step 1 then show that for each
s
,
l^{\circ}_{s}
extends uniquely to a symplectomorphism
l_{s}\colon(X^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})\to(\mathcal{X}_{t},\mathcal{C}_{t})
. Moreover, by construction,
l_{0}=l
and
l_{1}
takes
\Sigma
to
\mathcal{C}_{t}
.
This concludes our proof.
∎

Remark 3.47
.

The reader may with to compare Step 1 in the proof of Corollary
3.46
with
[
CG
, Theorem 1.6]
, which appeared when the present article was at the final stages of completion. It shows the following. Suppose that you’re given a smoothing
X\subset\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}
of a type III K3, together with a tame symplectic form
\omega
on
\mathcal{X}
. Then provided it restricts to the toric form on the components of
X
, and has a standard form near triple intersection points, then
(\mathcal{X}_{t},\omega|_{\mathcal{X}_{t}})
admits an almost-toric fibration with integral affine base (in our notation)
B
.

Assume that
Y
is a type III K3 surface with split mixed Hodge structure.
Let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
be the mirror compactifying K3 surface.
Suppose
Y
has
2n
triple points, and Friedman-Scattone index
k
.
Then by construction,
\Sigma
has genus
n+1
; and by Lemma
3.25
, it has divisibility
k
in
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.
Combining our results, we see that
[
LU24
, Conjecture 1]
by Lekili and Ueda is correct:

Corollary 3.48
.

Suppose that
M
is the complement of a smooth ample divisor of genus
g=n+1
and divisibility
k
in a K3 surface
X
, and that
Y
is a type III K3 surface with
2n
triple points and Friedman-Scattone index
k
, with split mixed Hodge structure. Then there are quasi-isomorphisms

\mathcal{W}(M)\simeq\Coh Y\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}(M)\simeq\Perf Y

Proof.

By Lemma
3.16
, Remark
3.17
, and Proposition
3.26
, we may assume that
Y
is projective.
Now the result follows from Corollary
3.46
with Theorems
2.22
and
2.52
.
∎

4.
Deformation argument

4.1.
Overview and properties of smoothings of type III K3 surfaces

Let
Y
be a type III K3 with split mixed Hodge structure. Let
Y\subset\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
be the versal deformation of
Y
with the property that all line bundles lift. See Lemma
4.3
below. Let
\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
denote the generic fibre of
\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
, a K3 surface over the field
\mathbb{C}((q))
.

Let
(M,\omega=d\theta)
be the Liouville domain of Definition
2.20
associated to
Y
. From Section
3
, we know there exists a complex K3 surface
X
equipped with a Kähler form
\omega
such that the class
[\omega]\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,\mathbb{R})
is integral, a smooth complex curve
\Sigma\subset X
such that
[\omega]=\PD[\Sigma]
, a tubular neighbourhood
\nu(\Sigma)\subset X
of
\Sigma\subset X
, and a choice of primitive
\omega|_{X\setminus\nu(\Sigma)}=d\theta^{\prime}
, such that
(X\setminus\nu(\Sigma),\theta^{\prime})
is a Liouville manifold and is Liouville equivalent to
(M,\theta)
.

There exists an ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}/X
such that
c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=[\omega]\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})

and a hermitian metric
h
on
\mathcal{L}
such that the associated Chern connection
\nabla
on
\mathcal{L}
has curvature
\Theta
with
\omega=\frac{i}{2\pi}\Theta
. See e.g.
[
GH94
]
.
Let
\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
denote the (derived, split-closed) Fukaya category of
(X,\omega)
generated by oriented Lagrangian submanifolds
L\subset X
such that the flat connection
\nabla|_{L}
has trivial holonomy (equipped with a grading, spin structure, covariantly constant section
\lambda_{L}
of the circle bundle of
\mathcal{L}|_{L}
, and choice of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J_{L}
such that
L
is regular with respect to
J
). Cf.
[
Sei15
,
\lx@sectionsign
8c]
.

Remark 4.1
.

Seidel works over
\bigcup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{C}((q^{1/d}))
(which is the algebraic closure of the field
\mathbb{C}((q))
of formal Laurent series) and considers Lagrangian submanifolds
L\subset X
such that
\nabla|_{L}
has finite monodromy (equipped with a covariantly constant multi-section of the circle bundle of
\mathcal{L}|_{L}
).
We work over
\mathbb{C}((q))
in the same manner by restricting to Lagrangian submanifolds such that the flat connection
\nabla|_{L}
has trivial holonomy.
Cf.
[
GHH+
, Remark 1.9]
, and
[
Fuk03
]
. (Alternatively, we can work over
\bigcup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{C}((q^{1/d}))
as in
[
Sei15
]
, restricting to Lagrangian submanifolds
L\subset X
such that
\nabla|_{L}
has finite holonomy, or over the Novikov field

\Lambda:=\left\{\sum a_{i}q^{\lambda_{i}}\ |\ a_{i}\in\mathbb{C},\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\lambda_{i}=\infty\right\}

with no restriction on the holonomy.)

The purpose of this section is to prove our main theorem:

Theorem 4.2
.

There is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra automorphism
\psi
of
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
and a
\mathbb{C}((q))
-linear equivalence of
A_{\infty}
-categories

\psi^{\ast}\Coh(\mathcal{Y}_{\eta})\simeq\mathcal{F}(X,\omega).

We start by identifying the deformation
Y\subset\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
Y
be a type III K3 with split mixed Hodge structure. Then the locus
S\subset\Def Y
in the universal deformation space of
Y
such that all line bundles lift is smooth of dimension
1
and the restriction of the universal family to this locus has smooth total space.

Remark 4.4
.

If
Y
is projective then the restriction
\mathcal{Y}/S
of the universal family to
S\subset\Def Y
is projective over
S
(because an ample line bundle on
Y
lifts to
\mathcal{Y}
by definition of
S
).

Proof.

The versal deformation space
\Def Y
of
Y
is universal because
H^{0}(T_{Y})=0

[
FS86
,
\lx@sectionsign
7(2)]
.

By
[
Fri83b
, Theorem 5.10]
the deformation space
V=\Def Y
of a d-semistable type III K3
Y
is a union
V=V_{1}\cup V_{2}
of two smooth irreducible components such that
V_{1}
corresponds to locally trivial deformations of
Y
,
V_{2}
has dimension
20
and contains the smoothings, and
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
intersect transversely in a smooth germ of dimension
19
.

Following
[
FS86
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
, let
Y^{[i]}
denote the disjoint union of the normalisations of the codimension
i
strata of
Y
and write

L=\operatorname{ker}(H^{2}(Y^{[0]},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y^{[1]},\mathbb{Z})).

Let
v
denote the number of components of
Y
.
We claim that the lattice
L
has rank
19+(v-1)
.
This follows by combining
\lx@sectionsign
3, (4.13), and Lemma 1.1 from
[
FS86
]
.
More precisely, as in
[
FS86
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
,
by the Mayer–Vietoris spectral sequence for the normal crossing scheme
Y
(cf.
[
Mor84b
]
), the lattice
L
is equal to the graded quotient
W_{2}/W_{0}
of the weight filtration of the Deligne mixed Hodge structure
W_{0}=W_{1}\subset W_{2}=H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
on
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
, and
W_{0}=H^{2}(|\Delta_{Y}|,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
where
\Delta_{Y}
is the dual complex of
Y
. Let
(Y\subset\mathcal{Y})/(0\in\mathbb{D})
be a semistable smoothing of
Y
and let
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
be a general fibre of
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
.
Let
T\colon H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
denote the monodromy, and
N=\log T
(note
T
is unipotent since
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
is semistable).
Let
Y=\bigcup Y_{i}
denote the irreducible components of
Y
and
\xi_{i}=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{i}))|_{Y}\in H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
.
By
[
FS86
, (4.13)]
, we have an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\rightarrow(\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}\cdot\xi_{i})/\mathbb{Z}\cdot\sum\xi_{i}\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z}).}

Consider the monodromy weight filtration on
H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
.
Let
\gamma
be a primitive generator of
W_{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, choose
\gamma^{\prime}\in H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
such that
\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}=1
and define
\delta=N\gamma^{\prime}
(then
\gamma
is determined up to sign and
\delta
is determined modulo
W_{0}=\mathbb{Z}\cdot\gamma
given
\gamma
).
With these notations, we have
[
FS86
, Lemma 1.1]

Nx=(x\cdot\gamma)\delta-(x\cdot\delta)\gamma.

In particular,
\operatorname{ker}N=\langle\gamma,\delta\rangle^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{t},\mathbb{Z})
has rank
20
.
(Note that
\gamma
and
\delta
are linearly independent since
T
is maximally unipotent, i.e.,
(T-I)^{2}\neq 0
.)
Combining, we deduce that
L
has rank
19+(v-1)
, which establishes our claim.

The mixed Hodge structure on
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
is classified by the class of the extension of pure Hodge structures

0\rightarrow W_{0}\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow W_{2}/W_{0}\rightarrow 0,

which corresponds to a homomorphism
\phi\colon L\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
.
We have an injective homomorphism
\operatorname{Pic}Y\rightarrow L
given by the first Chern class and restriction.
Then
\operatorname{ker}\phi=\operatorname{Pic}Y

[
FS86
, Proposition 3.4]
. In particular,
Y
has split mixed Hodge structure if and only if
\operatorname{Pic}Y=L
.
The component
(0\in V_{1})
of the versal deformation space of
Y
corresponding to locally trivial deformations of
Y
is naturally identified with the germ of the algebraic torus
\operatorname{Hom}(L,\mathbb{C}^{*})
at the point
\phi

[
FS86
, (3.9)]
(the local Torelli theorem for type III K3s).

For any line bundle
A\in\operatorname{Pic}Y
, the locus where
A
deforms is the zero locus of a holomorphic function on
\Def Y
by
[
FS86
, Proof of Lemma 5.5]
.
The subgroup of line bundles generated by the restriction of the components of
Y
in a semistable smoothing
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{D}
has rank
v-1
, and the locus where these line bundles deform is the component
V_{2}
of
V=\Def Y
corresponding to smoothable (or equivalently d-semistable) surfaces
[
FS86
, p. 25]
.

In the case that
Y
has split MHS, we have
\operatorname{Pic}Y=L
of rank
19+(v-1)
.
This implies that the locus
S\subset\Def Y
where all line bundles deform is contained in
V_{2}
and has dimension at least
1
.
The locus
S
intersects
V_{1}
transversely in the point
0\in\Def Y
by the local Torelli theorem for type III K3s recalled above.
Thus
S
is a smooth curve in
V_{2}
intersecting
V_{1}\cap V_{2}
transversely at
0
.

The fibre of the normal bundle to
V_{1}\cap V_{2}\subset V_{2}
at
0\in V_{2}
is identified with the quotient
H^{0}(\cExt^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y}))=\Ext^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y})/H^{1}(Y,T_{Y})
of the space of first order deformations of
Y
by the locally trivial deformations. The image of
T_{0}S
in
H^{0}(\cExt^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y}))
is non-trivial by transversality of
S
and
V_{1}\cap V_{2}
in
V_{2}
. It follows that the restriction of the universal family to
S
has smooth total space by
[
Fri83b
, Proposition 2.5]
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
Y
be a type III K3 surface with split MHS, and
Y\subset\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
the versal deformation of
Y
such that the restriction map
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}Y
is surjective, see Lemma
4.3
.

Let
Y\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
be an elementary modification of
Y
of type 1) or 2) with exceptional locus
C\subset Y
.
Let
\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
be the flop of the
(-1,-1)
-curve
C\subset\mathcal{Y}
. Then
Y^{\prime}
has split mixed Hodge structure and
Y^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
is the versal deformation of
Y^{\prime}
such that
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}Y
is surjective.
In particular, the elementary modification
Y\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{\eta}

of the generic fibres of
\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
and
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
.

Proof.

The modification
Y^{\prime}
of
Y
has split MHS by Proposition
3.13
. The total space
\mathcal{Y}
of the deformation
Y\subset\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
is smooth by Lemma
4.3
. Now a direct calculation shows that
C\subset\mathcal{Y}
has normal bundle
\mathcal{O}(-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}(-1)
and the flop
\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
of
C
restricts to the elementary modification
Y\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
.
The flop
\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
on Picard groups and the elementary modification
Y\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
induces a compatible isomorphism
\operatorname{Pic}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Pic}Y^{\prime}
on Picard groups by Proposition
3.13
. So
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}Y
surjective implies
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}Y^{\prime}
surjective.
∎

This implies that to prove Theorem
4.2
, for each pair
n,k
, it is enough to establish the claim for a single type III K3 surface
Y
with these invariants (and split mixed Hodge structure). In particular, we will take it to be projective.

4.2.
Mirror deformations for some preferred objects

We start by identifying the projective type III K3 surfaces we will work with.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
n
and
k
be any positive integers such that
k^{2}|n
. Then there exists a projective type III K3 surface
Y
with split mixed Hodge structure,
2n
triple points and
|H^{3}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|=k
, with the following property. There exists a Cartier divisor
C\subset Y
that is a union of two
(-1)
-curves, say
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, which lie in adjacent components of
Y
, say
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
, and meet the common
1
-stratum transversely in one point.

Proof.

First, we claim that there exists a projective type III K3 surface
Y^{\prime}
with split mixed Hodge structure and the invariants
n
and
k
with the following property: there exists an irreducible component
Y^{\prime}_{2}
of
Y^{\prime}
containing a
(-1)
-curve
C^{\prime}_{1}
meeting the boundary transversely in one point and a
(-2)
-curve
C^{\prime}_{2}
disjoint from the boundary and intersecting
C^{\prime}_{1}
transversely in one point.

A log Calabi–Yau surface with boundary a cycle of
n\leq 3

(-1)
-curves and split mixed Hodge structure contains such a configuration
C^{\prime}_{1}\cup C^{\prime}_{2}
intersecting any given component of the boundary,
see e.g.
[
Loo81
, Chapter I, Theorem 1.1,
\lx@sectionsign
2.1, and Proposition 5.2]
. Our claim then follows from
[
FS86
, Figures 3 and 4]
together with the abstract base change construction as in the Proof of Lemma
3.29
.

Now perform a type 1) elementary modification with exceptional locus
C^{\prime}_{1}
, say
f\colon Y^{\prime}\dashrightarrow Y
. The pair
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
of
(-1)
-curves on
Y
given by the exceptional locus of
f^{-1}
and the strict transform of
C^{\prime}_{2}
then satisfy the required properties.

We claim that
Y
is again projective.
Let
\pi\colon Y^{\prime}\rightarrow\bar{Y}
be the contraction of
C^{\prime}_{1}
.
It suffices to prove that
\bar{Y}
is projective.
Recall that the type III K3
Y^{\prime}
is in
(-1)
-form, so in particular admits an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
A
such that
A
has degree
1
on each irreducible component of the singular locus (cf. Proof of Lemma
3.16
). Moreover, since
Y^{\prime}
has split MHS, any choice of line bundles
L_{i}
on the irreducible components
Y^{\prime}_{i}
of
Y^{\prime}
glue to give a line bundle
L
on
Y^{\prime}
. Let
\pi_{0,2}\colon Y_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow\bar{Y}_{2}
denote the contraction of
C^{\prime}_{1}
. It suffices to describe an ample
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\bar{A}_{2}
on
\bar{Y}_{2}
such that
\bar{A}_{2}
has degree
1
on each irreducible component of the boundary of
\bar{Y}_{2}
. Indeed, let
L
be the line bundle on
Y^{\prime}
given by glueing
A^{\otimes d}|_{Y^{\prime}_{i}}
on
Y^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\neq 2
and
{\pi_{0,2}}^{*}\bar{A}_{2}^{\otimes d}
on
Y^{\prime}_{2}
for
d
sufficiently divisible, then
\bar{L}={\pi_{0}}_{*}L
is an ample line bundle on
\bar{Y}
. In each case the
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\bar{A}_{2}
on
\bar{Y}_{2}
can be constructed as follows: there is a unique effective rational linear combination
B
of the boundary curves with degree
1
on each boundary curve. This
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor defines a birational contraction with exceptional locus the union of the
(-2)
-curves
E_{i}
in the interior of
\bar{Y}_{2}
, cf.
[
GHK15a
, Lemma 6.9]
. Now the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
\bar{A}_{2}:=B-\sum\epsilon_{i}E_{i}
is ample on
\bar{Y}_{2}
for suitable
0<\epsilon_{i}\ll 1
,
\epsilon_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
Y
be as in Lemma
4.6
, with Cartier divisor
C=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
. Let
E=i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C}
, and let
F
be the pushforward to
\Coh Y
of the holomorphic line bundle on
C
with degree
-1
on
C_{1}
and degree
1
on
C_{2}
. In other words,
F=E\otimes\mathcal{L}
, where
\mathcal{L}
is the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{1})|_{Y}
.
Then
\operatorname{Hom}(E,E)
,
\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)
and
\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)
are
1
-dimensional, and the product

\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)\to\operatorname{Hom}(E,E)

is trivial.

Proof.

We repeatedly use that for any sheaves
A,B\in\Coh C
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(i_{*}A,i_{*}B)=\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(i^{*}i_{*}A,B)=\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(A,B)

It’s then immediate that
\operatorname{Hom}(E,E)=\mathbb{C}
. Also, we get that
\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{C},F)=H^{0}(C,F)
. If
L
is a line bundle on
C
with restriction
L_{i}
to
C_{i}
for
i=1,2
, then tensoring the short exact sequence of sheaves on
C

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C_{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{C_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{p}\longrightarrow 0

with
L
yields a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow L_{1}\oplus L_{2}\longrightarrow L|_{p}\longrightarrow 0

and so an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow H^{0}(L)\longrightarrow H^{0}(L_{1})\oplus H^{0}(L_{2})\longrightarrow H^{0}(L|_{p})

In the case
L=F
we have
H^{0}(L_{1})=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{C_{1}}(-1))=0
and thus

H^{0}(L)=\operatorname{ker}(H^{0}(L_{2})\to H^{0}(L|_{p}))=H^{0}(L_{2}(-p))\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{C_{2}})=\mathbb{C}.

Reversing the roles of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
(or using Serre duality), we get
\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)=H^{0}(F^{\vee})\simeq\mathbb{C}
.

Finally, the product

\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(E,E)

is trivial using the fact that the global sections of
\mathcal{H}om(E,F)
vanish on
C_{1}
and the global sections of
\mathcal{H}om(F,E)
vanish on
C_{2}
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

The Cartier divisor
C\subset Y
smooths to an irreducible
(-2)
curve
\mathcal{C}_{\eta}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
, and
E
and
F
both deform to the (spherical) sheaf
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}_{\eta}}\in\Coh\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
.

Suppose
Y
is as in Lemma
4.6
. Choose toric models for the irreducible components of
Y
so that
C_{1}
, respectively
C_{2}
, come from blowing up interior points for
Y_{1}
, respectively
Y_{2}
. (Note that one can always do this: if
E
is an internal
(-1)
-curve on a maximal log Calabi–Yau surface
(S,B)
then there is a toric model of
(S,B)
contracting
E
, because we can first contract
C
to obtain
(S^{\prime},B^{\prime})
and take a toric model of
(S^{\prime},B^{\prime})
.)

Let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
be the mirror compactifying K3 surface.
We work with the relative Fukaya category of the pair
(X,\Sigma)
as in
[
Sei15
, Section 8d]
, say
\mathcal{F}(X,\Sigma)
. Objects are given by compact exact Maslov zero Lagrangians
L
in
X\backslash\Sigma
, equipped with a grading, a spin structure, and an
\omega_{X}
-compatible almost complex structure
J_{L}
on
X
which is regular with respect to
L
and such that
\Sigma
is almost-complex for
J_{L}
.
Recall that we say an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
on
X
is
regular
with respect to a Lagrangian
L\subset X
if there are no nonconstant
J_{L}
-holomorphic spheres or discs with boundary on
L
. In our case, the set of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
J
on
X
that are regular with respect to a graded Lagrangian
L\subset X
is dense within the space of all
\omega
-compatible complex structures
[
Sei15
, Lemma 8.4]
, using
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}X=4
and
c_{1}(X)=0
.

From the description of the almost-toric fibration
\pi_{X}\colon X\to B
in Section
3.2.1
,
there is an open subset of
B
homeomorphic to a disc, say
B_{\mathrm{loc}}
, such that the restriction of
\pi_{X}
to it, say
\pi_{\mathrm{loc}}\colon X_{\mathrm{loc}}\longrightarrow B_{\mathrm{loc}}
, has exactly two nodal fibres, say above points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
associated to the blow-ups for
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
. These fibres have the same invariant direction, and
\Sigma
restricts to a visible annulus which is fibred over a line which intersects the segment between
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
transversally, say
\Sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}
. See Figure
4.1
.

Figure 4.1.
The almost-toric fibration
\pi_{\mathrm{loc}}\colon X_{\mathrm{loc}}\longrightarrow B_{\mathrm{loc}}
, with the spheres
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
.

We consider Lagrangian spheres
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
in
X_{\mathrm{loc}}\backslash\Sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}
, defined as follows.
Both are fibred over the segment joining the two nodes. We’re free to choose
S_{0}
to be any such fibred sphere.
Given
S_{0}
, we take
S_{1}
to be its image under the generalised Lagrangian translation
\sigma_{L}
, from Proposition
2.49
,
corresponding under mirror symmetry to
\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{1})|_{Y}\in\Auteq\Coh Y
.
Note that
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
are isotopic inside
X_{\mathrm{loc}}
, by a fibre-preserving Hamiltonian isotopy.
We can describe them explicitly in terms of the SYZ fibration
\pi_{\mathrm{loc}}\colon X_{\mathrm{loc}}\to B_{\mathrm{loc}}
by using
[
HK
, Proposition 5.2]
(adapted to the relative case, where we separately work with the portions of
B_{\mathrm{loc}}
on either side of
\pi_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{loc}})
).
This is given in Figure
4.1
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
Y
be the projective type III K3 with split mixed Hodge structure from Lemma
4.6
, and let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
be its mirror compactifying surface. Set
M=X\backslash\nu\Sigma
, and let
S_{0}
,
S_{1}
be the Lagrangian spheres in
M
defined above.

Then there exists brane data on
S_{0}
,
S_{1}
such that:

(1)

The Floer cohomology groups
HF^{0}(S_{0},S_{1})
and
HF^{0}(S_{1},S_{0})
are both one-dimensional.

(2)

The product

HF^{0}(S_{0},S_{1})\otimes HF^{0}(S_{1},S_{0})\longrightarrow HF^{0}(S_{1},S_{1})

is trivial in
\mathcal{F}(M)
.

(3)

The same product is non-trivial to first order in
q
in the relative Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X,\Sigma)
: letting
\hom
denote morphisms in this category, we have that the following product is non-zero (as in
[
Sei15
, Lemma 3.11]
):

H^{0}(\hom(S_{0},S_{1})\otimes\mathbb{C}[q]/q^{2})\otimes H^{0}(\hom(S_{1},S_{0})\otimes\mathbb{C}[q]/q^{2})\longrightarrow H^{0}(\hom(S_{1},S_{1})\otimes\mathbb{C}[q]/q^{2})

where the tensor products are all over
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
.

We will prove this in several steps. The first result we need is as follows.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
Y
is as in Lemma
4.6
, let
(X,\omega;\Sigma)
be its mirror compactifying K3 surface, and let
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
be the Lagrangian spheres introduced above. Then we can choose
S_{0}
, and gradings for
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
, so that under the homological mirror symmetry isomorphism of Theorem
2.22
,
S_{0}
is mapped to
E
and
S_{1}
is mapped to
F
.

Proof.

First, let’s work with each of the irreducible components
(Y_{i},D_{i})
.
We claim that for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can identify the mirror to
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C_{i}}(k)\in\Coh Y_{i}
. Let’s start with
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
. (This is a key step in
[
HK22
]
when inducting on the number of interior blow-ups to get the general homological mirror symmetry theorem.)
On the B-side, by
[
Orl92
, Theorem 4.3]
, we have a semi-orthogonal decomposition

\Coh Y_{i}=\langle\mathcal{O}_{C_{i}}(-1),Lf^{\ast}\Coh\bar{Y}_{i}\rangle

where
f:Y_{i}\to\bar{Y}_{i}
is given by contracting
C_{i}
.
Say our blow-up is on
D_{ij}\subset D_{i}
corresponding to a toric ray
v_{j}
.
On the A-side, in terms of Weinstein Lefschetz fibrations, the mirror to
\bar{Y}_{i}
has smooth fibre
S_{i}
and some distinguished collection of vanishing cycles;
and the Weinstein Lefschetz fibration mirror to
Y_{i}
is obtained by adding to this one more critical fibre, with vanishing cycle the meridian
S^{1}_{j}\subset S_{i}
corresponding to the toric ray
v_{j}
(in the notation of
[
HK22
]
, this is
W_{j}
). See
[
HK22
, Proposition 3.8 and Remark 3.9]
.
Under the HMS equivalence of
[
HK22
, Theorem 1.1]
,
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C_{i}}(-1)
is mirror to the added Lefschetz thimble, say
V_{i}
. Equivalently, in terms of Weinstein handlebody decompositions,
V_{i}
can be identified with the Lagrangian co-core of the additional handle added to go from the mirror of
\bar{Y}_{i}
to the mirror of
Y_{i}
.
In terms of almost-toric fibrations,
V_{i}
is fibred over the segment from
p_{i}
to
\pi_{i}(S_{j}^{1})
. Here we are still using the directed Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}^{\to}(w_{i})
, so that
V_{i}
has boundary equal to
S_{j}^{1}
. To get the corresponding object under the sequence of quasi-equivalences

\mathcal{F}^{\to}(w_{i})\simeq\mathcal{W}(M_{U_{i}},\mathfrak{f}_{i})\simeq\mathcal{W}(M_{i})

we push
V_{i}
off the core
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
by a small Reeb flow before deleting the (even smaller) neighbourhood of
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
, similar to the mirror to
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}}
in the proof of Lemma
2.46
.
Given
V_{i}
, we can now get the mirrors to the sheaves
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C_{i}}(k)
for
k\neq-1
by applying Proposition
2.42
(using for instance the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(D_{ij})
), say
V_{i}(k+1)
.

Say
p_{12}\subset D_{12}\subset D_{i}
(for
i=1,2
) is the point which is blown up. Under the mirror isomorphism
\Coh D_{12}\simeq(T^{\ast}S^{1})^{-}
,
i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{p_{12}}
is mirror to the zero section in
(T^{\ast}S^{1})^{-}
(with a preferred brane structure), which can be stabilised to get its mirror in

T^{\ast}[0,1]\times(T^{\ast}S^{1})^{-}
, say
A_{12}
.

We can then proceed as in the proof of Lemma
2.46
, using the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C_{1}}\oplus i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{C_{2}}\longrightarrow i_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{p_{12}}\longrightarrow 0

and similarly for
F
. The mirror to
E
will be obtained by the Polterovich surgeries (for cleanly intersecting Lagrangians) for
V_{1}(1)
,
A_{12}
and
V_{2}(1)
; and the mirror to
F
, by using
V_{1}(0)
,
A_{12}
and
V_{2}(2)
. The fact that they are related by the Lagrangian translation mirror to
\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{1})|_{Y}\in\Auteq\Coh Y
follows by checking intersection numbers.
∎

Lemmas
4.7
and
4.10
, taken together, imply that
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
satisfy points (1) and (2) for Proposition
4.9
.
To check point (3), we will use the following alternative viewpoint
X_{\mathrm{loc}}
, based on
[
Aur07
, Section 5]
and carefully revisited in
[
HK
, Section 6.4]
.
Up to truncating conical ends,
X_{\mathrm{loc}}
is given by

X_{\mathrm{loc}}=\{xy+(z-1)(z-2)=0\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{\ast}

equipped with the Kähler form with potential
|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}+(\log|z|)^{2}
.
The projection to
z
, say
f\colon X_{\mathrm{loc}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
,
makes it the total space of a Lefschetz fibration.

In terms of these coordinates, the singular Lagrangian torus fibration is given by
(x,y,z)\mapsto(|z|,\delta_{z}(x,y))
, where
\delta_{z}(x,y)
is the signed area between the equator
\{|x^{\prime}|=|y^{\prime}|\}\subset f^{-1}(z)
and the orbit of
(x,y,z)
under the
S^{1}
-action

(x,y,z)\mapsto(e^{i\theta}x,e^{-i\theta}y,z)
, see
[
Aur07
, Section 5.1]
.

Comparing the two fibrations, we see that without loss of generality
\Sigma
can be described locally as the subset
\{z=\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\,i\}\subset X_{\mathrm{loc}}
. The sphere
S_{0}
corresponds to the ‘standard’ matching sphere between the critical fibres of
f
, as in Figure
4.2
.
Moreover, we already understand the effect of (relevant) Lagrangian translations on
S_{0}
, again by comparing with Section
[
HK
, Section 6.4]
. In particular, we see that
S_{1}
is also a matching sphere, with the matching path given in Figure
4.2
.

Figure 4.2.
Local model for the spheres
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
in
X
: Lefschetz fibration.

Lemma 4.11
.

The Lagrangian spheres
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
satisfy property (3) in the statement of Proposition
4.9
: letting
\hom
denote morphisms in the relative Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X,\Sigma)
, we have that:

(4.12)

H^{0}(\hom(S_{0},S_{1})\otimes\mathbb{C}[q]/q^{2})\otimes H^{0}(\hom(S_{1},S_{0})\otimes\mathbb{C}[q]/q^{2})\longrightarrow H^{0}(\hom(S_{1},S_{1})\otimes\mathbb{C}[q]/q^{2})

Proof.

The local patch
X_{\mathrm{loc}}
comes with the standard complex structure on
\{xy+(z-1)(z-2)=0\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
(which can be extended to an almost-complex structure on the whole of
X
). The Lagrangian spheres
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
are both regular for this choice. Using the maximal principle, the product in Equation
4.12
can be calculated by working in
X_{\mathrm{loc}}
. Experts will recognise that this brings us back to a setting in which holomorphic curves have been very well studied, going back to e.g.
[
Sei03
,
KS02
]
. There are now many ways to proceed.
For instance, using the fact that
S_{0}
is Hamiltonian isotopic to
S^{1}
in
X
(respecting their gradings) and that we have a ring isomorphism
HF_{X}^{\ast}(S_{0},S_{0})\simeq H^{\ast}(S^{0})
, we see that setting
q=1
, some holomorphic triangles must exist for the product

(4.13)

\hom_{0}(S_{0},S_{1})\otimes\hom_{0}(S_{1},S_{0})\longrightarrow\hom_{0}(S_{1},S_{1})

in
\mathcal{F}(X,\Sigma)
(here we assume we’re using the obvious minimal models). Suppose we’re working with a small Hamiltonian perturbation for the second copy of
S_{1}
. Then the generator of
H^{0}(\hom(S_{0},S_{1}))
corresponds to one of the singular points of
f
, and the generator of
H^{0}(\hom(S_{1},S_{0}))
corresponds to the other one. Using for instance the open mapping theorem (working with small perturbations of the holomorphic
J
), we get that all of the holomorphic triangles we are counting must intersect
\Sigma
transversally in a single point, and so contribute to the
q
-order term in the product, which establishes our claim. (Alternatively, one can consider the holomorphic disc in the base
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
which is bounded by the matching paths for
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
, and directly consider its holomorphic lifts with suitable boundary conditions.)
∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.9
.

4.3.
Conclusion of argument

We may assume that
Y
satisfies the conclusions of Lemma
4.6
by Lemma
4.5
.

Definition 4.14
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subset\Perf Y
be the full
A_{\infty}
-subcategory with objects the line bundles on
Y
, the skyscraper sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{q_{i}}
for
q_{i}\in Y_{i}\setminus D_{i}
as in Corollary
2.26
, and the sheaves
E
and
F
of Lemma
4.10
.

Remark 4.15
.

In fact, instead of taking all line bundles on
Y
, it suffices to take
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
,
A
,
A^{\otimes 2}
where
A
is very ample, together with a basis of
\operatorname{Pic}Y
. This is because
\mathcal{O}_{Y},A,A^{\otimes 2}
split generate
\Perf Y
by
[
Orl09
, Theorem 4]
, and these objects
E
have the property that the kernel of the obstruction map

\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\Perf Y)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{E}\operatorname{Hom}^{2}(E,E)

has dimension
1
, as required by Proposition
4.18
below. In particular, if desired, we may assume that
\mathcal{B}_{0}
has finitely many objects.

For each line bundle
L
on
Y
, there exists a unique lift
\mathcal{L}
of
L
to a line bundle on
\mathcal{Y}
by construction.
(Note that the restriction map
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}Y
is injective for any deformation
(Y\subset\mathcal{Y})/(0\in S)
of a type III K3
Y
since
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})=0
.)
For each (smooth) point
q_{i}\in Y
as above we choose a section
\sigma_{i}
of
\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
such that
\sigma_{i}(0)=q_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma_{i}}
denote the structure sheaf of (the image of)
\sigma_{i}
, which is in particular a coherent sheaf on
\mathcal{Y}
, flat over
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{*}
be a finite locally free resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma_{i}}
(which exists since
\mathcal{Y}
is smooth). Since
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma_{i}}
is flat over
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
,
E_{i}^{*}:=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[[q]]}\mathbb{C}
is a (finite locally free) resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma_{i}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[[q]]}\mathbb{C}=\mathcal{O}_{q_{i}}
.
(This is because the cohomology sheaf of the complex
E_{i}^{*}
in degree
-k
computes
\mathcal{T}or_{k}^{\mathbb{C}[[q]]}(\mathcal{O}_{\sigma_{i}},\mathbb{C})
, which vanishes for
k>0
by flatness of
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma_{i}}
over
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
; by
[
Wei94
, Lemma 3.2.8]
,
\mathrm{Tor}
can be computed using resolutions by flat (but not necessarily projective) modules.) Similarly, we define lifts of the sheaves
E
and
F
of Lemma
4.10
as follows. Recall that there is a Cartier divisor
C=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
on
Y
such that, for each
i=1,2
,
C_{i}
is a
(-1)
-curve on an irreducible component
Y_{i}
of
Y
meeting the boundary transversely at a point of the
1
-stratum
Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}
, and
E=i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}
and
F=i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{1})|_{Y}
where
i\colon C\subset Y
denotes the inclusion. The Cartier divisor
C\subset Y
lifts uniquely to a Cartier divisor
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{Y}
(with generic fibre a
(-2)
-curve on
\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
). Indeed, the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(C)
lifts by definition of
Y\subset\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
, and the restriction map on global sections is surjective since
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(C))=0
; uniqueness follows from
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(C))=1
. Now as before, since
\mathcal{C}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
is flat, we can take
\mathcal{E}^{*}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
finite locally free resolutions of the coherent sheaves
\mathcal{E}:=i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathcal{F}:=i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{1})
on
\mathcal{Y}
,
where
i\colon\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{Y}
denotes the inclusion.
Then the restrictions
E^{*}
,
F^{*}
to
Y
are finite locally free resolutions of
E
,
F
.

These choices of lifts of the objects of
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subset\Perf Y
to
\Coh\mathcal{Y}=\Perf\mathcal{Y}
define an uncurved
A_{\infty}
deformation
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
over
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
.
Here we define the
A_{\infty}
structure using finite locally free resolutions and the Cech complex for an affine open covering, see
[
Sei15
,
\lx@sectionsign
5a]
.
(Note that we are in fact defining a dg category here, which we regard as an
A_{\infty}
category with
m^{k}=0
for
k>2
.)

Remark 4.16
.

This is quasi-equivalent to the definition using injective resolutions by
[
Sei15
, Lemma 5.1]
. But we use the definition in terms of locally free resolutions and Cech complexes because then it is clear that
\mathcal{B}
is an uncurved deformation of the
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{B}_{0}
in the sense of
[
She19
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.3]
(here an
uncurved
deformation of an
A_{\infty}
category is a deformation for which
m^{0}=0
).

The deformation
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is non-trivial because
\mathcal{B}_{0}
split generates
\Perf Y
, so that
\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{0})=\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\Perf Y)
, the deformation
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{C}[[q]]
of
Y
is non-trivial at first order by construction, and the map
\Ext^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\Perf Y)
from first order deformations of
Y
to first order deformations of
\Perf Y
is injective by
[
BF08
, Theorem 3.1.3]
.

Definition 4.17
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}(M)
be the full
A_{\infty}
-subcategory corresponding to
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subset\Perf Y
under the limit HMS equivalence

\Perf Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}(M)

of Theorem
2.52
.

Explicitly, by Corollary
2.50

and Corollary
2.26
, the objects of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
are represented by Lagrangian sections of the SYZ fibration
f\colon M\rightarrow S^{2}
, (exact) Lagrangian torus fibres of
f
, and the Lagrangian spheres
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
of Lemma
4.10
, all equipped with suitable brane data.

Consider the relative Fukaya category of the pair
(X,\Sigma)
as in Section
4.2
.
Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{F}(X,\Sigma)
be the full subcategory of the relative Fukaya category of the pair
(X,\Sigma)
with the same objects as
\mathcal{A}_{0}
together with a choice of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J_{L}
which is regular with respect to the Lagrangian
L\subset X
representing the object. Then
\mathcal{A}
is an uncurved
A_{\infty}
deformation of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
over
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
. See
[
Sei15
, Proposition 8.7]
.
The deformation
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is non-trivial at first order by Proposition
4.9
above and
[
Sei15
, Lemma 3.11]
.

The following proposition was communicated to us by Nick Sheridan, building on
[
GHH+
]
.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
F_{0}\colon\mathcal{B}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}
be a quasi-equivalence of
A_{\infty}
categories. Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}
be uncurved
A_{\infty}
deformations of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}
respectively over
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
, each non-trivial at first order. We further assume that
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is split generated by a finite set
S
of objects such that
\hom^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A)=0
for all
A\in S
.

Assume that

(1)

The kernel of the obstruction map

\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\rightarrow\prod_{A\in\Ob\mathcal{A}_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}^{2}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(A,A)

has dimension
1
.

(2)

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, objects
A
of
\mathcal{A}
, and
\alpha\in\hom^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A)
, we have

m^{k}(\alpha,\alpha,\cdots,\alpha)=0.

Then there exists a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra automorphism
\psi\colon\mathbb{C}[[q]]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[[q]]
and a curved
A_{\infty}
-functor

F\colon\psi^{*}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}

extending
F_{0}
. Equivalently, let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the
A_{\infty}
-category obtained from
\mathcal{A}
by equipping each object
A
with the bounding cochain
F^{0}_{A}\in\hom^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A)
. Then we have an uncurved
A_{\infty}
-functor

F^{\geq 1}\colon\psi^{*}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime},

which is a quasi-equivalence.

Proof.

We use the formalism of
A_{\infty}
pre-functors described in
[
GHH+
]
and follow the proof of op. cit, Proposition A.6 closely. For
\mathbb{C}[[q]]
-linear
A_{\infty}
categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, an
A_{\infty}
pre-functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a map
F\colon\Ob\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\Ob\mathcal{D}
and an element

F=(F^{s})_{s\geq 0}\in CC^{1}(\mathcal{C},F^{*}\mathcal{D}),

where

CC^{*}(\mathcal{C},F^{*}\mathcal{D}):=\\
\prod_{X_{0},\ldots,X_{s}}\operatorname{Hom}(\hom(X_{0},X_{1})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom(X_{s-1},X_{s})[1],\hom(F(X_{0}),F(X_{s}))[1])[-1]

such that
F^{0}_{X}\in q\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(F(X),F(X))
for all
X\in\Ob\mathcal{C}
. Define an element

\delta(F)\in CC^{2}(\mathcal{C},F^{*}\mathcal{D})

by the formula

\delta(F)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{s})=\\
\sum m_{\mathcal{D}}(F^{*}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{s^{1}}),\ldots,F^{*}(\ldots,c_{s^{j}}))-\sum(-1)^{\dagger}F^{*}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{s_{0}},m_{\mathcal{C}}(\ldots,c_{t_{1}}),\ldots,c_{s_{1}})

where the sums are over
0\leq s^{1}\leq s^{2}\leq\cdots\leq s^{j}=s
and
0\leq s_{0}<t_{1}\leq s_{1}=s
.
The signs (which we will not need) are given by
\dagger=|c_{1}|^{\prime}+\cdots+|c_{s_{0}}|^{\prime}
where
|c_{i}|^{\prime}
denotes the degree of
c_{i}
in the shifted complex
\hom(X_{i-1},X_{i})[1]
. Then
\delta(F)=0
if and only if
F
is a (non-unital, filtered, curved)
A_{\infty}
functor.
See
[
GHH+
,
\lx@sectionsign
A.2]
.

We construct a sequence of
(\psi_{n})_{n\geq 0}
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebra homomorphisms
\psi_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}[[q]]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[[q]]
and a sequence
(F_{n})_{n\geq 0}
of
A_{\infty}
pre-functors
F_{n}\colon\psi_{n}^{*}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
such that, for all
n\geq 0
,
\psi_{n+1}=\psi_{n}\bmod q^{n+1}
,
F_{n+1}=F_{n}\bmod q^{n+1}
, and
\delta(F_{n})=0\bmod q^{n+1}
, so that
F_{n}
defines an
A_{\infty}
functor
\overline{F}_{n}
modulo
q^{n+1}
. Given this data, let
\overline{\psi}_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}[q]/(q^{n+1})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[q]/(q^{n+1})
be the reduction of
\psi_{n}
modulo
q^{n+1}
and
\psi:=\varprojlim\overline{\psi}_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}[[q]]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[[q]]
. Then
F:=\varprojlim\overline{F}_{n}\colon\psi^{*}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
is an
A_{\infty}
-functor such that
F=F_{0}\bmod q
.

Given
\psi_{n}
and
F_{n}
we construct
\psi_{n+1}
and
F_{n+1}
as follows. Consider the maps of cochain complexes

CC^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{1}_{F_{0}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{0}^{*}:=CC^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0},F_{0}^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}^{1}_{F_{0}}}}{{\longleftarrow}}CC^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{0})

given by left composition and right composition with
F_{0}
, see
[
GHH+
,
\lx@sectionsign
A.3]
, cf.
[
Sei08
,
\lx@sectionsign
1e]
.
These maps are quasi-isomorphisms
[
GHH+
, Lemma A.12]
.
Consider
\delta(F_{n})\in CC^{2}(\psi_{n}^{*}\mathcal{B},F_{n}^{*}\mathcal{A})
. We fix an identification
\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(A_{1},A_{2})=\hom_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(A_{1},A_{2})\hat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
for each pair of objects
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
and the corresponding objects in the deformation
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
(also denoted by
A_{1},A_{2}
). Similarly for the deformation
\psi_{n}^{*}\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. This gives an identification

CC^{*}(\psi_{n}^{*}\mathcal{B},F_{n}^{*}\mathcal{A})=CC^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0},F_{0}^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})\hat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[q]]=C_{0}^{*}\hat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
.
Using this identification, we have
\delta(F_{n})=q^{n+1}\cdot\delta(F_{n})_{n+1}\mod q^{n+2}
for some
\delta(F_{n})_{n+1}\in C_{0}^{2}
. The cochain
\delta(F_{n})_{n+1}\in C_{0}^{2}
is closed, see
[
GHH+
, Proof of Proposition A.6]
. Consider the subcomplex

\tilde{C}^{*}_{0}:=\operatorname{ker}\left(C^{*}_{0}\rightarrow\prod_{A\in\Ob\mathcal{A}_{0}}\hom^{2}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(A,A)\right)

of
C_{0}^{*}
. The cocycle
\delta(F_{n})_{n+1}
lies in
\tilde{C}^{*}_{0}
by our assumption (2). Indeed, the projection of
\delta(F_{n})
to
\hom^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A)
for
A\in\Ob\mathcal{A}=\Ob\mathcal{A}_{0}
equals

\sum_{k\geq 1}m^{k}_{\mathcal{A}}(F^{0}_{n,A},\cdots,F^{0}_{n,A})

which vanishes by assumption (2).
Let
b\in CC^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
denote the first order term of the
A_{\infty}
-deformation
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, i.e
m^{*}_{\mathcal{B}}=m^{*}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}+q\cdot b\bmod q^{2}
. We have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}CC^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{0})@>{\mathcal{L}^{1}_{F_{0}}}>{}>C^{*}_{0}@<{\mathcal{R}^{1}_{F_{0}}}<{}<CC^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\displaystyle{\prod\hom^{2}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(B,B)}@>{F_{0}}>{}>\displaystyle{\prod\hom^{2}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(F_{0}(B),F_{0}(B))}=\displaystyle{\prod\hom^{2}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(F_{0}(B),F_{0}(B))}\end{CD}

where the products are over
B\in\Ob\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
So the cocycle
\mathcal{L}^{1}_{F_{0}}(b)
lies in the subcomplex
C^{*}_{0}
because
\mathcal{B}
is uncurved.
The vector space
H^{2}(\tilde{C}^{*}_{0})
is one dimensional by our assumption (1) (recall
F_{0}
is a quasi-equivalence and
\mathcal{L}^{1}_{F_{0}}
and
\mathcal{R}^{1}_{F_{0}}
are quasi-isomorphisms).
Thus
[\delta(F_{n})_{n+1}]=c_{n+1}\cdot\mathcal{L}^{1}_{F_{0}}([b])
for some
c_{n+1}\in\mathbb{C}
, using non-triviality of the deformation
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
at first order.
Define
\psi_{n+1}
by
\psi_{n+1}(q)=\psi_{n}(q)+c_{n+1}q^{n+1}
, then, regarding
F_{n}
as an
A_{\infty}
pre-functor
F_{n}\colon\psi_{n+1}^{*}\mathcal{B}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}
, we have
[\delta(F_{n})_{n+1}]=0
, cf.
[
GHH+
, Proof of Prop. A.6, Step 1]
.
Choose
f_{n+1}\in C^{1}_{0}
such that
\mu^{1}(f_{n+1})=\delta(F_{n})_{n+1}
and define
F_{n+1}=F_{n}-f_{n+1}q^{n+1}
. Then one computes that
\delta(F_{n+1})=0\bmod q^{n+2}
, cf. op. cit.

The assertion that
F\colon\psi^{*}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
determines an uncurved
A_{\infty}
functor
F^{\geq 1}\colon\psi^{*}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that is a quasi-equivalence is a special case of
[
She20
, Lemma 2.16]
.

Finally, since the deformation
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is non-trivial at first order, the same is true of the induced deformation
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
of the full subcategory
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}_{0}
of
\mathcal{A}
with objects the subset
S\subset\Ob\mathcal{A}_{0}
in the statement. Indeed we have an identification
\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\mathcal{A}_{0})=\operatorname{HH}^{2}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}_{0})
since
S
split generates
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Now when we pass to the category
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathcal{A}
by equipping each object
A
with the bounding cochain
F^{0}_{A}
, we still have
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
a full subcategory because
\hom^{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(S,S)=0
for all
A\in S
so there are no bounding cochains for objects of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
. Thus the deformation
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is non-trivial at first-order.
The quasi-isomorphism
F^{\geq 1}\colon\psi^{*}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
implies that the homomorphism
\psi
is non-trivial at first order, thus
\psi
is an automorphism.
∎

Proposition 4.19
.

Assumption (1) of Proposition
4.18
is satisfied for the
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{A}_{0}
of Definition
4.17
.

Proof.

Recall that in Theorems
2.22
and
2.52
we have constructed an equivalence

\mathcal{W}(M)\simeq\Coh Y
and proved that it restricts to an equivalence
\mathcal{F}(M)\simeq\Perf Y
.
The inclusion
\Perf Y\subset\Coh Y
induces an identification
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\Perf Y)=\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\Coh Y)
by
[
BZFN10
, Theorem 1.2]
, cf.
[
BZ11
]
.
This means the inclusion
\mathcal{F}(M)\subset\mathcal{W}(M)
induces an identification

\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(M))=\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{W}(M)).

The closed–open map
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{O}\colon SH^{*}(M)\rightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{W}(M))
is an isomorphism for a Liouville manifold
M
by
[
Gan12
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
GPS24
, Theorem 1.13]
.

For
(X,\Sigma)
general in complex moduli,
\operatorname{Pic}X
is rank
1
, generated by the primitive ample class
\frac{1}{k}[\Sigma]
.
In particular there are no rational curves on
X
.
Now by
[
GP20
, Theorem 1.4]
and
[
GP21
, (1.2)]

we have a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow H^{2}(M)\rightarrow SH^{2}(M)\rightarrow SH^{2}_{+}(M)\rightarrow 0

with
\dim SH^{2}_{+}(M)=1
. (Note that in
[
GP21
, (1.2)]
the variable
t
has degree
2
, see
[
GP21
, (3.5),(3.20)]
.)

Let
L\subset M
be an exact compact Lagrangian. Let
L\subset W\subset M
be a Weinstein neighbourhood of
L\subset M
, symplectomorphic to a tubular neighbourhood of the zero section in the cotangent bundle of
L
. We have a commutative diagram

(4.20)

\begin{CD}H^{*}(M)@>{}>{}>SH^{*}(M)@>{}>{}>HF_{M}^{*}(L,L)\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\Big\|\\
H^{*}(W)@>{}>{}>SH^{*}(W)@>{}>{}>HF_{W}^{*}(L,L)\end{CD}

where the left horizontal arrows are the canonical maps
c^{*}
defined in e.g.
[
Rit13
,
\lx@sectionsign
5]
, the top right horizontal arrow is the composition of the isomorphism
SH^{*}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(M))
given by the closed–open map
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{O}
discussed above and the natural map
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(M))\rightarrow HF^{*}(L,L)
, the bottom right horizontal arrow is the same composition for
L\subset W
, and the vertical arrows are given by the restriction map on cohomology and the Viterbo restriction map on symplectic cohomology. So the composition
H^{*}(M)\rightarrow SH^{*}(M)\rightarrow HF^{*}(L,L)
given by the top row coincides with the restriction map
H^{*}(M)\rightarrow H^{*}(L)
under the identification
H^{*}(L)=H^{*}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}HF^{*}_{W}(L,L)
given by the bottom row (which coincides with the identification given by the Morse complex).

Let
\{L_{i}\ |\ i\in I\}
denote the set of Lagrangians in
M
representing the objects of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
It suffices to show that
H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\oplus_{i\in I}H^{2}(L_{i},\mathbb{Q})
is injective, or equivalently that
H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Q})
is spanned by the classes
[L_{i}]
,
i\in I
.
We consider the Leray spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(B,R^{q}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})\Rightarrow H^{p+q}_{c}(M,\mathbb{Z})

for the Lagrangian fibration
\pi\colon M\rightarrow B
(first introduced in Lemma
2.21
)
computing the compactly supported cohomology of
M
.
Recall that we have in Section
2
a detailed description of the Lagrangian fibration
\pi\colon M\rightarrow B
.
The Lagrangian fibration
\pi\colon M\rightarrow B
is the restriction of the almost toric fibration
\pi_{X}\colon X\rightarrow B
to
M=X\backslash\nu(\Sigma)
.
Let
R:=\pi_{X}(\nu(\Sigma))\subset B
; this is a ribbon graph which is a thickening of
\Gamma
.
Recall that we described
\pi\colon M\rightarrow B
in detail in Section
2
.
In particular, we can compute, first, that
H^{0}(B,R^{2}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})=\mathbb{Z}
, using the fact that
R^{2}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}
is given at a point
\mathrm{pt}
by
H^{2}_{c}(\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{pt}),\mathbb{Z})\simeq H_{0}(\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{pt}),\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
.
And second, we compute that
H^{2}(B,\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})=\mathbb{Z}^{g+1}
, corresponding to the connected components of
B\setminus R
(the locus where the fibres of
\pi
are compact tori), where
g
is the genus of
\Sigma
.

The map
H^{2}_{c}(M,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{0}(B,R^{2}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})=H^{0}(B,\mathbb{Z})
corresponds under Poincaré duality to the map
\pi_{*}\colon H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{2}(B,\mathbb{Z})
.
The map
H^{2}(B,\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})\rightarrow H^{2}_{c}(M,\mathbb{Z})
corresponds under Poincaré duality to the map
\mathbb{Z}^{g+1}\rightarrow H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
given by
e_{i}\mapsto\gamma_{i}
, where
\gamma_{i}
is the class of the fibre over the
i
th connected component of
B\setminus R
.
The graded piece
H^{1}(B,R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})
of the Leray filtration on
H^{2}_{c}(M,\mathbb{Z})=H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
is identified with
\operatorname{Pic}Y
via the map

\operatorname{Pic}Y\rightarrow H^{1}(B,R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}),\quad\mathcal{L}\mapsto[L]-[L_{0}],

where
L
and
L_{0}
are the Lagrangian sections of
\pi
corresponding to
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
under the HMS equivalence of Theorem
2.52
, by Corollary
2.50
.
Since
\mathcal{B}_{0}
includes the objects
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
,
\mathcal{O}_{p_{i}}
, and a basis of
\operatorname{Pic}Y
,
corresponding under the equivalence
\Perf Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}(M)
to a reference Lagrangian section of
\pi
, a Lagrangian torus fibre over the
i
th connected component of
B\setminus R
, and Lagrangian sections
L
of
\pi
such that the classes
[L]-[L_{0}]
give a basis of
H^{1}(B,R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})
, we deduce that
H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
is generated by the classes of the Lagrangians representing the objects of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
∎

Proposition 4.21
.

Assumption (2) of Proposition
4.18
is satisfied for the
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{A}_{0}
of Definition
4.17
.

Proof.

Every graded Lagrangian
L\subset X
is tautologically unobstructed for generic
J
since
X
is Calabi–Yau of complex dimension
2
,

[
Sei15
, Lemma 8.4]
. It follows that
CF^{*}(L,L)
is quasi-isomorphic to
C^{*}(L)
as
A_{\infty}
-algebras (via the Morse complex)
[
Abo11
]
. The Lagrangians representing the objects of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
are either spheres or tori.
We use a perfect Morse function so that
m^{1}=0
. Then
CF^{1}(L,L)=0
in case
L
is a sphere.

C^{*}(L)
is formal for a torus (this holds for any Lie group, see
[
Sul77
,
\lx@sectionsign
12]
), so
m^{2}=\cup
and
m^{k}=0
for
k\neq 2
.
Assumption (2) follows.
∎

Now we localise, passing to
(\cdot)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[[q]]}\mathbb{C}((q))
, and also restrict to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}
corresponding to line bundles, so that the associated full
A_{\infty}
subcategory
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}
has no bounding cochains (because we may assume
\hom^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,A)=0
for
A\in\Ob\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
since
A
is represented by a Lagrangian
S^{2}
in
X
).

We have a quasi-embedding
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[[q]]}\mathbb{C}((q))\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
cf.
[
Sei15
, Proposition 8.8]
.

Since
\mathcal{Y}/\mathbb{C}[[q]]
is projective,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
split generates
(\Coh\mathcal{Y}_{\eta})

[
Orl09
, Theorem 4]
. Then by Ganatra’s proof of automatic generation for Fukaya categories of Calabi–Yau manifolds recalled in
[
SS21
, Proof of Proposition 4.8]
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
split generates the Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
. Note that the assumptions of
[
SS21
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.5]
concerning Fukaya categories needed to apply Ganatra’s results are verified for K3 surfaces by
[
SS21
, Remarks 2.5, 2.6, and 2.7]
.
Thus we obtain an
A_{\infty}
quasi-equivalence
\psi^{*}(\Coh\mathcal{Y}_{\eta})\simeq\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
as claimed. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Remark 4.22
.

One can show that the HMS isomorphism of Theorem
4.2
takes
a Lagrangian torus to the structure sheaf of a point.
One just observes that for one of our exact Lagrangian tori
L_{i}
in
M
, instead of equipping
L_{i}
with a bounding cochain as in Proposition
4.18
we can change the choice of section
\sigma_{i}
of
\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
with
\sigma_{i}(0)=p_{i}
to
\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
so that
L_{i}
(with trivial bounding cochain) corresponds to
\mathcal{O}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{C}((q))
. This implies that the main theorem of

[
SS20
]
applies in our situation. (Beyond this, one expects to get mirrors to a dense subset of the SYZ fibres by using the flux homomorphism, as in the Family Floer approach of Fukaya and Abouzaid cf. e.g.
[
Abo21
]
, provided we are willing to work over the Novikov field.)

We can also explicitly identify mirrors to line bundles in this case.

Proposition 4.23
.

The equivalence
\Coh\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
of Theorem
4.2
induces an isomorphism from the Picard group
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
of
\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
to the group of equivalence classes of Lagrangian sections of the almost toric fibration
\pi_{X}\colon(X,\omega)\rightarrow B
up to fibre-preserving Hamiltonian isotopy.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Y}/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
be a projective semistable model of
\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
as above. Then the restriction map
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}Y
is an isomorphism by construction and we have the exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{g+1}/\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}\rightarrow 0.

Note that the Picard group of a K3 surface over any field is torsion-free, see e.g.
[
Huy16
, Remark I.2.5]
.
In particular the composition

\operatorname{Pic}Y=\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}

is surjective with kernel a primitive subgroup of rank
g
.

Using the Leray spectral sequence for
\pi_{X}\colon X\rightarrow B
and the Leray spectral sequence with compact supports for its restriction
\pi\colon M\rightarrow B
to
M
, together with the Gysin sequence for
M\subset X
, we find that we have an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{g+1}/\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow H^{1}(B,R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})\rightarrow H^{1}(B,R^{1}\pi_{X,*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X})\rightarrow H^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}),

cf. Proof of Proposition
4.19
. In particular the map

H^{1}(B,R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})\rightarrow H^{1}(B,R^{1}\pi_{X,*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X})

has kernel a primitive subgroup of rank
g
, and image equal to the kernel
K
of the map

H^{1}(B,R^{1}(\pi_{X})_{\ast}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

given by cup product with the class
\PD[\Sigma]
.
Recall that
\PD[\Sigma]=[\omega]
represents the radiance obstruction of the singular integral affine structure on
B
associated to the almost toric fibration
\pi_{X}\colon(X,\omega)\rightarrow B
.

Combining
[
ABC+09
, Proposition 6.69]
and
[
HK
, Proof of Lemma 4.3]
(which extends the result of op. cit. in real dimension
4
to the case where we allow focus–focus singularities of the Lagrangian torus fibration), together with the fact that we already know that
\pi_{X}
has one Lagrangian section
L_{0}
, we get that the group
K
classifies Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{X}
up to fibre-preserving Hamiltonian isotopy.

Now consider the commutative diagram

(4.24)

\begin{CD}\operatorname{Pic}Y=\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}@>{}>{}>\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
H^{1}(B,R^{1}\pi_{!}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{M})@>{}>{}>H^{1}(R^{1}\pi_{X,*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{X})\end{CD}

with the vertical arrows being induced by the HMS equivalences of Theorem
2.52
and Theorem
4.2
.
The left vertical arrow is the isomorphism of Proposition
2.32
and Corollary
2.50
that identifies isomorphism classes of line bundles on
Y
and Lagrangian sections of
\pi
up to fibre-preserving Hamiltonian isotopy.
Now since the horizontal arrows both have primitive kernel of rank
g
and the top horizontal arrow is surjective, we deduce that
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
maps isomorphically onto the image
K
of the bottom horizontal arrow, which classifies Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{X}
up to fibre-preserving Hamiltonian isotopy.
∎

Corollary 4.25
.

For each
\mathcal{L}\in\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
, there is a symplectomorphism
\sigma_{L}
of
(X,\omega)
, intertwining the almost toric fibration
\pi_{X}\colon(X,\omega)\to B
, such that
\sigma_{L}
induces a well-defined autoequivalence of
\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
, and, under the equivalence
\Coh\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
,
[\sigma_{L}]\in\Auteq\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
is mirror to
\otimes\mathcal{L}\in\Auteq\Coh\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
.

Proof.

Let
L
be the Lagrangian section of
\pi_{X}
mirror to
\mathcal{L}
from Proposition
4.23
, and as before let
L_{0}
be the mirror to
\mathcal{O}
. These determine a Lagrangian translation
\sigma_{L}\in\operatorname{Symp}(X,\omega)
, defined as in
[
HK
, Section 4]
(or Proposition
2.40
). We readily get that
\sigma_{L}
has the required action on Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{X}
, and so that its mirror has the correct action on line bundles on
\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
. As these split-generate, this proves the claim.
∎

One can similarly ‘import’ other results about autoequivalences. For instance, suppose
\phi_{0}
is
an automorphism of an irreducible component
Y_{i}
of
Y
, fixing
D_{i}
pointwise. This lifts to an isomorphism
\phi\colon\mathcal{Y}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[[q]]
.
In
[
HK
, Section 6]
we construct a mirror symplectomorphism
\rho\in\pi_{0}\operatorname{Symp}_{c}(M_{U_{i}})
, called a ‘nodal slide recombination’. As this is a compactly supported symplectomorphism, it gives by inclusion a symplectomorphism of
X
, say
\tilde{\rho}
. This preserves the set of Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{X}
, and induces a well-defined autoequivalence of
\mathcal{F}(X,\omega)
. Moreover, by comparing the action of
(\phi_{\eta})_{\ast}
on
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{Y}_{\eta}
with the action of
\tilde{\rho}
on Lagrangian sections of
\pi_{X}
, we see that
[\widetilde{\rho}]\in\Auteq(\mathcal{F}(X,\omega))
is
mirror to
[(\phi_{\eta})_{\ast}]\in\Auteq(\Coh\mathcal{Y}_{\eta})
.
